Hashes and duplicate screens cannot prove transcription accuracy, exclude every translated or variant duplicate, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Three individually documented Dutch-language poems introduce **Herman Gorter**, **Hieronymus van Alphen** and **Guido Gezelle**: *Zie je ik hou van je*, *De Pruimeboom* and *Het Schryverke*. Each is one complete work, not a separate edition, translation, musical setting or excerpt. The Netherlands and Belgium are associated places, not claims about composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Gorter's untitled sixteen-line poem is identified here by its opening. It occupies printed p. 102, PDF p. 110 of [*Verzen*, Amsterdam, 1890](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Herman_Gorter,_Verzen_(1890).pdf); neighboring pages contain other poems. The reading was transcribed from that page, preserving four four-line stanzas. Gorter died in 1927.
  - Van Alphen's complete twenty-four-line *De Pruimeboom*, subtitled `Eene vertelling`, occupies printed pp. 393–394, PDF pp. 463–464 of [*Dichtwerken*, volume 1, second printing, 1857](https://archive.org/details/dichtwerkenvanm00alphgoog). The final two lines continue on the next page; the following poem is excluded. The verse was transcribed from the historical scan; typographic small capitals in names are normalized. Van Alphen died in 1803. The [Dutch national library](https://collecties.kb.nl/themas/nederlandse-poezie/historische-dichters/hieronymus-van-alphen-1746-1803/de-pruimenboom-van) discusses the poem and translations; those translations are not reproduced or counted separately.
  - Gezelle's complete forty-four-line *Het Schryverke* follows the [DBNL diplomatic transcription of the 1858 *Vlaemsche dichtoefeningen*, pp. 36–37](https://www.dbnl.org/tekst/geze002vlae01_01/geze002vlae01_01_0009.php), consulted via DBNL (KB, national library). DBNL's [colophon](https://www.dbnl.org/tekst/geze002vlae01_01/colofon.php) identifies the Antwerp library copy and editorial interventions; page images for this copy were not available for independent inspection. The transcription's subtitle `(Gyrinus natans.)` is retained in reader context. The transcript reads `kabotseken` and `regtte`; [a later e-text](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/29289) differs, so its wording is not spliced into this reading. DBNL [marks the 1858 work copyright-free](https://www.dbnl.org/titels/gebruiksvoorwaarden.php?id=geze002vlae01) and requests customary source attribution; Gezelle died in 1899.
  - A [1997 review](https://www.dbnl.org/tekst/_tsj001199701_01/_tsj001199701_01_0028.php) reports all three works in a 1992 selection based on a Dutch teachers' survey. This documents educational reception, not a quantitative global ranking; the review is not a transcription source. The dates above identify editions, not necessarily composition dates. Spacing, indentation and punctuation typography are normalized; historical wording is retained.
  - These historical printings are public domain in the United States; copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished; no modern editorial notes, illustrations, translations or recordings are included. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-dutch-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-dutch-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two inspected PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Three individually documented poems introduce **Wilfred Owen**: *Anthem for Doomed Youth*, *Dulce et Decorum Est* and *Futility*. Each is one complete work as printed, not an additional manuscript, edition, adaptation or excerpt. England is an associated place, not a claim about composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - The transcriptions follow the posthumous [*Poems*, London, Chatto & Windus, 1920](https://archive.org/details/poemsowenwil00owenrich), with an introduction by Siegfried Sassoon. Printed pp. 11, 15 and 25 are PDF pp. 31, 35 and 45 in the University of California copy. Each complete poem occupies one printed page: *Anthem* has an eight-line stanza and a six-line stanza; *Dulce* has stanzas of eight, six, two and twelve lines; *Futility* has two seven-line stanzas. The 1920 printings were compared with printed pp. 11, 15 and 25 in a separately scanned [March 1921 reprint](https://archive.org/details/poemswilf00owenuoft), PDF pp. 27, 31 and 41. The edition year is not a claim about composition dates.
  - Both inspected printings of *Dulce* read `Bitten as the cud` as a full line. The [Project Gutenberg e-text](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/1034), which says it transcribes a 1921 reprint, instead has `Bitter as the cud`; a [Poetry Foundation transcription](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/poems/46560/dulce-et-decorum-est) has `Obscene as cancer, bitter as the cud`. The latter credits a Viking Press edition dated 1921, but [Viking was founded in 1925](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_Press); its attribution is not accepted here as a verified witness date. These are not separate works, and the 1920 reading is not a claim about Owen's final manuscript. The Latin title and closing words are marked in Latin; no translation is supplied. [Oxford's manuscript resource](https://war.site.ox.ac.uk/dulce-et-decorum-est-compare-manuscripts) describes four surviving drafts; no manuscript text or images are reproduced here.
  - A [secondary transcription](https://wykontario.org/index.php/features-category/1150-100-favorite-poems-of-britain) of the BBC's 1995 *Bookworm* viewer poll lists all three poems individually. This is evidence of attention in a British poll, not a quantitative global ranking; the poll transcription is not the source of the poems.
  - The 1920 printing is public domain in the United States; Owen died in 1918 and Sassoon in 1967. Copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. The scan is linked, not republished. Verse was transcribed from its historical pages with spacing, indentation and punctuation typography normalized; neither Sassoon's introduction nor the portrait is reproduced.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-owen-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-owen-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies both PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Two individually documented poems introduce Romanian poet **George Coșbuc**: *Nunta Zamfirei* and *Rea de plată*. Each is one complete work, not a separate excerpt, alternate spelling, translation or adaptation. Romania is an associated place, not a claim about composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Both readings follow the [1897 second edition of *Balade și idile*](https://archive.org/details/baladeiidile00cogoog), a Google digitization of the New York Public Library copy. *Nunta Zamfirei* occupies printed pp. 16–23, PDF pp. 27–34; *Rea de plată* occupies printed pp. 33–34, PDF pp. 44–45. A [second 1897 copy](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:George_Cosbuc_-_Balade_si_idile_1883-1890.pdf) was compared by printed page: PDF pp. 25–32 and 42–43 respectively. The edition year is not a claim about composition dates.
  - The ballad has 28 six-line stanzas. It reads `să-și dee zor`, `Tinteș` and `Pe-un grad`; [some modern readings](https://ro.wikisource.org/wiki/Nunta_Zamfirei) have `s-alerge-n zbor`, `Ținteș` and `Pe-un gard`. *Rea de plată* has three eight-line stanzas, the second continuing across the page break; its 1897 third line is `Punând un sac, în urmă`, unlike `Punându-și sacul, iacă` in [some modern readings](https://ro.wikisource.org/wiki/Rea_de_plat%C4%83). Modern variants are discovery aliases, not spliced into the 1897 readings or counted as new works.
  - [Liceunet](https://liceunet.ro/george-cosbuc-poezii/nunta-zamfirei) discusses *Nunta Zamfirei* individually in a Romanian educational context; [Libertatea](https://www.libertatea.ro/lifestyle/george-cosbuc-poezii-2888642) selects *Rea de plată* among Coșbuc's verses. These show attention to each work, not a quantitative global ranking. Neither modern source is the transcription source.
  - The 1897 printing is public domain in the United States; Coșbuc died in 1918. Copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. Scans are linked, not republished. The verse was transcribed from the historical pages, preserving their wording while mapping historical `ĕ` to `ă`, `ê` to `â`, and short `ĭ`/`ŭ` marks to `i`/`u`; heading accents, `ș`/`ț` letterforms, punctuation typography, indentation and spacing are normalized. Other historical spellings are retained.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-cosbuc-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-cosbuc-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies both PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Six individually documented Romanian poems introduce **Mihai Eminescu**'s *Lacul*, *Dorința*, *Floare albastră*, *Somnoroase păsărele*, *Și dacă ramuri bat în geam* and *Pe lângă plopii fără soț*. Each is one complete work, not an additional edition, short title, translation or musical setting. Romania is an associated place, not a claim about composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - The readings follow the Iași Central University Library's [*Poesii*, title-page 1884](https://dspace.bcu-iasi.ro/handle/123456789/36442), edited by Titu Maiorescu. The library identifies this first book edition as offered for sale in December 1883; the title-page year is not a composition date. Its scan has 326 PDF pages; printed pp. 9–10, 63–64, 87–88, 97–100, 139–142 and 167–168 correspond to PDF pp. 23–24, 77–78, 101–102, 111–114, 153–156 and 181–182, respectively. A [separately scanned copy](https://archive.org/details/mihai-eminescu-poezii-1884-editura-librariei-socecu-comp.) was compared page by page, not assumed to be a complete scan of the book.
  - In the displayed order the works have five, six, fourteen, four, three and eleven quatrains. *Floare albastră* retains the printed dotted divider before its final stanza; the divider counts as a displayed line, not a separate stanza or work. The first-edition reading has `riuri in soare` and `bolta cea senină`, unlike some later `râuri de soare` and `balta cea senină` readings. *Dorința* has `O să-ți cază` and `Ori să cadă`; *Somnoroase păsărele* has `Trece lebeda pe apă`. These are retained rather than silently conflated with modern versions.
  - [Libertatea](https://www.libertatea.ro/lifestyle/poezii-de-mihai-eminescu-2805295) names *Lacul*, *Somnoroase păsărele* and *Floare albastră* as familiar and selects *Și dacă* and *Pe lângă plopii fără soț* individually. [Liceunet](https://liceunet.ro/mihai-eminescu/dorinta/eseu) discusses *Dorința* alongside *Lacul* and *Floare albastră* in a Romanian educational context. These demonstrate attention to individual poems, not a quantitative global popularity ranking; the modern sites' commentary and readings are not the transcription source.
  - The historical printing is public domain in the United States; Eminescu died in 1889 and editor Maiorescu in 1917. Copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. The scans are linked, not republished. The verse was transcribed from the printed pages; historical spelling, including `ĕ`, is retained while heading accents, the `ș`/`ț` letterforms, punctuation typography, indentation and spacing are normalized. Modern title and text aliases are search metadata, not alternative catalogue records.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-romanian-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-romanian-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks both PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Two further individual **Mihai Eminescu** works, *O, rămâi* and *Glossă*, are transcribed from the same [Iași *Poesii* scan](https://dspace.bcu-iasi.ro/handle/123456789/36442), title-page 1884 and edited by Titu Maiorescu. Printed pp. 31–33 and 143–147 correspond to physical PDF pages [45–47](https://dspace.bcu-iasi.ro/bitstream/handle/123456789/36442/BCUIASI_CS_CR2155_1884.pdf?sequence=2&isAllowed=y#page=45) and [157–161](https://dspace.bcu-iasi.ro/bitstream/handle/123456789/36442/BCUIASI_CS_CR2155_1884.pdf?sequence=2&isAllowed=y#page=157). The [separate Socecu copy](https://archive.org/details/mihai-eminescu-poezii-1884-editura-librariei-socecu-comp.) contains these pages at PDF 36–38 and 128–132; the scans' physical page offsets are not uniform throughout the second copy. Each is one complete work, not an additional edition, translation, variant or musical setting.
  - *O, rămâi* has seven quatrains; the first five are the forest's speech. Its print uses `vuetul` and `Șueram`, where modern versions often have `vuietul` and `Șuieram`. A single opening quotation mark replaces the repeated opening marks at the start of printed lines, and the printed closing mark is retained. *Glossă* has ten eight-line stanzas; the closing stanza revisits the opening in reverse order, with differences in punctuation and spelling and without the opening `Tu` before `te intreabă`. The 1884 text has `Vremea trece, vremea vine`, `mizerii` and `Să supun`, rather than the `Vreme trece, vreme vine`, `mișeii` and `Se supun` found in common modern versions. Italic type is rendered as plain text; no later reading is silently substituted.
  - [Libertatea](https://www.libertatea.ro/lifestyle/poezii-de-mihai-eminescu-2805295) selects *O, rămâi* individually. [Liviu Chiscop's essay](https://centruleminescubm.wordpress.com/2022/11/15/teze-existentialiste-in-glossa-lui-eminescu/) discusses *Glossă* individually, including its fixed-form predecessors and Eminescu's compositional innovations; an [educational analysis](https://ebacalaureat.ro/c/glossa-de-mihai-eminescu---redacteaza-un-eseu-de-minimum-400-de-cuvinte-in-care-sa-prezintiparticularitati-ale-unui-text-poetic-studiatapartinand-luimihai-eminescu/1915) treats the same work in a Romanian examination context. These are evidence of attention to individual works, not a global ranking or sources of the transcribed text. Modern alternate spellings and the later opening are search aliases, not separate works.
  - The 1884 printing is public domain in the United States; Eminescu died in 1889 and Maiorescu in 1917. Copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. Scans are linked, not republished; no translations or recordings are included. Historical spelling, including `ĕ`, is retained; heading accents, `ș`/`ț` letterforms, punctuation typography, spacing and indentation are normalized. Romania is an associated place, not a claim about composition location; no religious classification is inferred.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-romanian-classics-2.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-romanian-classics-2-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two consulted PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Three individually documented works introduce Slovenian **France Prešeren**'s *Zdravljica* and *Povodnji mož*, and the anonymous Dutch *Wilhelmus*. Each is one complete work; anthem excerpts, alternate readings, translations, ballet and musical settings are not counted separately. Slovenia and the Netherlands are associated places, not claims about composition locations. Printing dates do not establish composition dates. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Both Prešeren readings follow Luka Pintar's [*Dóktorja Francéta Prešérna poezije*, third impression, 1914](https://archive.org/details/dktorjafranctap00pintgoog/page/n4/mode/1up). *Zdravljica*, [pp. 17–19](https://archive.org/details/dktorjafranctap00pintgoog/page/n27/mode/1up), has eight seven-line stanzas and the subtitle `(Ob novíni leta 1844.)`. This edited reading begins `Spet trte so rodile` and ends `Bog, kar nas dobrih je ljudi`; it is not the [1848 newspaper reading](https://www.dlib.si/details/URN:NBN:SI:DOC-9JWAEOHS) or the spelling-modernized anthem text. Page 19 reads `Ki dôbro`. The [Slovenian government](https://www.gov.si/en/topics/national-symbols/) identifies the seventh stanza as its national anthem; its English translation and musical materials are not reproduced here.
  - The ballad, [pp. 54–57](https://archive.org/details/dktorjafranctap00pintgoog/page/n64/mode/1up), has fourteen six-line stanzas and follows the printed title `Povôdnji mož`, normalized here as *Povodnji mož*. *Povodni mož* is a modern title alias, not a separate work. Earlier versions use Zalika rather than Urška; see [Slovenian Wikipedia's discussion](https://sl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Povodni_mo%C5%BE_(Pre%C5%A1eren)). A tiny mark following `mizi` on p. 55 is unclear in this scan and is not treated as punctuation. The [Slovenian National Theatre](https://www.sng-mb.si/program-opera-balet-sng-mb/povodni-moz/) documents a later ballet drawing on Prešeren's legacy, not permission to reuse the adaptation.
  - *Wilhelmus* follows Dutch text A in Florimond van Duyse's [*Wilhelmus van Nassouwe*, second edition (1911), pp. 5–7](https://archive.org/details/wilhelmusvannass00sgra/page/n8/mode/1up). Its fifteen eight-line stanzas form the `WILLEM VAN NASSOV` acrostic. The last stanza crosses the two columns of p. 7; German text B begins beneath it and is excluded. Van Duyse cites a 1581 source, but this transcription is of his 1911 printing, not an independently examined 1581 facsimile. The [Dutch royal house](https://www.koninklijkhuis.nl/onderwerpen/koninklijk-huis/volkslied) documents its national-anthem status and commonly sung stanzas. Although that source gives a customary attribution, authorship is disputed and is not asserted here.
  - These 1911 and 1914 historical printings are public domain in the United States; Prešeren died in 1849, Pintar in 1915 and van Duyse in 1910. Copyright, edition-related and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. Scans are linked rather than republished. Verse was transcribed from these historical pages, with heading accents, indentation, spacing and punctuation typography normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-european-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-european-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Six individually documented English poems add Browning's *My Last Duchess*, Herbert's *Love* (III), Hardy's *The Darkling Thrush*, Marvell's *To His Coy Mistress*, Tennyson's *Ulysses* and Waller's *Go, lovely Rose*. [Poetry Foundation's 2006 survey](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/articles/68445/hit-singles) identifies each individually among frequently anthologized English poems; its author explicitly describes the survey of 17 anthologies as **unscientific**, not a global popularity ranking. Alternate editions, titles, additions and musical settings are not counted as further works. No translations or recordings are added. England, and Wales/England for the Wales-born Herbert, are associated places rather than claims about composition locations.
  - *My Last Duchess* follows the [Porter–Clarke edition of Browning's revised text, pp. 143–145](https://archive.org/details/dramaticlyricsd01browgoog/page/n169/mode/1up). The catalogue and copyright page give 1898, but the scanned impression itself is undated. The fifty-six lines begin beneath the preceding poem and conclude before *Count Gismond*. A hanging wrap on p. 144 is rejoined. The earlier title *Italy* refers to the same dramatic monologue. This copy was chosen because another scan cuts off the beginning of several lines on p. 145.
  - Herbert's *Love* follows the 1912 *Oxford Book of English Verse*, [p. 295](https://archive.org/details/cu31924011997909/page/n310/mode/1up), where it has three six-line stanzas. The display title *Love (III)* distinguishes the work from Herbert's other poems titled *Love*. An early [1633 *Temple* edition](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Temple:_Sacred_Poems_and_Private_Ejaculations/Love_(III)) places book-closing matter after the poem; that material is not included as verse.
  - Hardy's *The Darkling Thrush* follows [*Poems of the Past and the Present*, second edition (1902), pp. 169–171](https://archive.org/details/poemsofpastprese00hardrich/page/n182/mode/1up). Its four eight-line stanzas end before the printed date `December 1900`, which is recorded as source context, not as another line or an independently established composition date.
  - Marvell's *To His Coy Mistress* follows the 1912 *Oxford Book*, [pp. 387–388](https://archive.org/details/cu31924011997909/page/n402/mode/1up), with 46 lines and paragraph breaks supplied from the indentations. The anthology prints `wingèd` and `Thorough`, with no punctuation at the end of line 2. A small mark after `gates` in the first scan is absent from a [second copy of the same printing](https://archive.org/details/oxfordbookofengl0000unse_j7s8/page/n403/mode/1up); the reading does not treat that mark as an apostrophe. The editor's `slow-chapt` gloss is not a line of the poem.
  - Tennyson's *Ulysses* follows [*Poems*, volume II (1842), pp. 88–91](https://archive.org/details/tennysonpoems1842-02/page/n99/mode/1up), as one complete seventy-line work. Indentation supplies paragraph breaks. This printing's spellings include `Vext` and `wherethro’`; later printings may differ.
  - Waller's *Go, lovely Rose* follows the 1912 *Oxford Book*, [pp. 310–311](https://archive.org/details/cu31924011997909/page/n325/mode/1up): its four complete five-line stanzas end before *Old Age*. A [Dictionary of National Biography entry](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1885-1900/White,_Henry_Kirke) identifies an added concluding stanza by Henry Kirke White in a later reprinting; that later addition is not silently attributed to Waller or counted separately.
  - The 1842, 1902 and 1912 printings and Browning's text in the edition catalogued and copyright-dated 1898 are public domain in the United States; the scanned 1898-edition impression is not independently dated. The authors died in 1633–1928; editors Charlotte Porter and Helen A. Clarke died in 1942 and 1926, and 1912 anthology editor Arthur Quiller-Couch died in 1944. Copyright, edition-related rights and moral rights vary elsewhere; check local law. The scans are linked rather than republished. Typography, quotation-mark style, spacing and indentation are normalized, and source line wraps are rejoined. The Oxford anthology's editor disclosed modernizing spelling and sometimes selecting or excising material; the boundaries above were checked individually rather than assumed from the anthology.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-anthology-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-anthology-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks the five named PDF hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every translated duplicate or variant, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Eight individually documented Swedish-language works introduce **Edith Södergran**'s *Dagen svalnar*, *Vierge moderne* and *Landet som icke är*, and **Karin Boye**'s *I rörelse*, *Jag vill möta …*, *Från en stygg flicka*, *Du är min renaste tröst* and *Morgon*. Each is counted once, not as separate sections, editions, translations or musical settings. Finland and Sweden are associations, not claims about where the poems were composed; printing years do not establish composition dates. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - *Dagen svalnar* follows *Dikter* (1916), [pp. 6–9](https://runeberg.org/sedikter/0010.html). Its four numbered sections are one complete work; the displayed line count includes the section numbers. The Runeberg scan of [p. 7](https://runeberg.org/sedikter/0011.html) lacks a visible `II`; a [second copy of the same 1916 printing](https://litteraturbanken.se/f%C3%B6rfattare/S%C3%B6dergranE/titlar/Dikter/sida/7/faksimil) clearly shows it but is damaged at `räcker`, which is legible in the Runeberg copy. Page 8 prints `skära, skära`, whereas [Boktips' modern reading](https://www.boktips.no/dikt/dikt-om-kjaerlighet/dagen-svalnar-av-edith-sodergran/) uses `sköra sköra`. Boktips discusses the poem's individual reception and four-part boundary; its modern text is not our witness.
  - *Vierge moderne* follows [*Dikter*, pp. 24–25](https://runeberg.org/sedikter/0028.html). Its French title is tagged separately from its Swedish verse. [Stockholm University](https://www.su.se/forskning/nyheter-forskning/nyhetsartiklar/2025-01-27-verket--vierge-moderne) discusses the individual poem. The complete title poem *Landet som icke är*, not the entire book, follows the posthumous 1925 collection, [pp. 71–72](https://litteraturbanken.se/f%C3%B6rfattare/S%C3%B6dergranE/titlar/LandetSomIcke/sida/71/faksimil); the break between pages is editorial and hanging wraps are rejoined. [Boktips](https://www.boktips.no/dikt/dikt-om-livet/landet-som-icke-ar-av-edith-sodergran/) discusses this poem's reception, not permission to reproduce its modern lineation.
  - Boye's five poems follow Litteraturbanken's proofread, unmodernized e-text of [*Härdarna* (1927), pp. 42–44 and 66](https://litteraturbanken.se/f%C3%B6rfattare/BoyeK/titlar/H%C3%A4rdarna/sida/42/etext), and [*Moln* (1922), pp. 30 and 46](https://litteraturbanken.se/f%C3%B6rfattare/BoyeK/titlar/Moln/sida/30/etext). Its [1927](https://litteraturbanken.se/api/get_work_info?authorid=BoyeK&titlepath=H%C3%A4rdarna&exclude=content_vector) and [1922](https://litteraturbanken.se/api/get_work_info?authorid=BoyeK&titlepath=Moln&exclude=content_vector) work metadata identifies these source editions and labels their e-texts CC0. **We did not inspect the Boye book scans.** An [archival manuscript](https://foretagskallan.se/kalla-karin-boyes-dikt-i-rorelse/) corroborates the ten-line boundary of *I rörelse*. The source-edition e-text capitalizes `Jag` on the last line of *Från en stygg flicka*; [Runeberg's 1994 electronic edition](https://runeberg.org/boyehard/3_10.html) has lowercase `jag`. [SelmaStories](https://www.selmastories.se/artikel/karin-boyes-kanda-dikter/) selects each of these five poems individually. Its modern presentation and any recordings are not the transcription sources.
  - The 1916, 1922, 1925 and 1927 originals/printings are public domain in the United States. Södergran died in 1923 and Boye in 1941; edition-related rights, copyright and moral rights can vary elsewhere, so check local law. Södergran's scans are linked rather than republished; line wraps, indentation and punctuation typography are normalized. Boye's readings adapt the identified CC0 e-texts; the exact underlying historical book pages were not independently inspected. The poet alias [Edith Irene Södergran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edith_S%C3%B6dergran) is her full name, not a separate author; spelling-sensitive search distinguishes Swedish `å`, `ä`, `ö`, including in aliases.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-swedish-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reviewed reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-swedish-classics-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks the named scan, manuscript and e-text page hashes and compares the five Boye readings line by line with the saved e-text pages. Hashes and duplicate screens cannot prove transcription accuracy, exclude every translated duplicate or variant, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Five individually documented Chinese works add the anonymous lyrics *江南* and *長歌行*, and the Tang poems *詠鵝*, *風* and *詠柳*. Each is a complete, separately bounded work, not a translation, alternate title or musical setting. China is an associated place, not an assertion about where each was composed. No translation, recording or inferred religious classification is added.
  - *江南* follows [*樂府詩集*, 卷二十六, PDF pp. 23–24](https://archive.org/download/06054428.cn/06054428.cn.pdf#page=24): the seven-line song begins `江南可採蓮` and ends `魚戲蓮葉北`, before the compiler's note. *長歌行* follows [卷三十, PDF pp. 8–10](https://archive.org/download/06054429.cn/06054429.cn.pdf#page=9): `青青園中葵` through `老大徒傷悲` is the first ancient-song reading, ending before `同前` and the next ancient song. Both are anonymous; an anonymous browsing group does not assert common authorship or an exact Han-era composition date. [Research on the anthology's compilation](https://ccsndb.ncl.edu.tw/nclccs/pdf/01_038_002_01_01.pdf) discusses the historical construction of the category “Han Yuefu.” Hong Kong's Education Bureau individually documents [*江南*](https://www.edb.gov.hk/attachment/tc/curriculum-development/kla/chi-edu/recommended-passages/KS1_03.pdf) and [*長歌行*](https://www.edb.gov.hk/attachment/tc/curriculum-development/kla/chi-edu/resources/primary/lang/jilei_shi_pdf/jilei_shi_003.pdf).
  - *詠鵝* follows [*御定全唐詩*, 卷七十九, PDF pp. 107–108](https://archive.org/download/06063889.cn/06063889.cn.pdf#page=107), where the four-line poem concludes the volume. The scan has `鵞`, normalized here to `鵝`; its note that Luo Binwang wrote it at seven is a tradition, not independently established here. The [Education Bureau](https://www.edb.gov.hk/attachment/tc/curriculum-development/kla/chi-edu/recommended-passages/KS1_05.pdf) recommends this individual poem.
  - Li Qiao's four-line *風* follows [卷六十一, PDF p. 131](https://archive.org/download/06063886.cn/06063886.cn.pdf#page=131), from `解落三秋葉` to `入竹萬竿斜`. A [China Youth Writers News article](https://qnzj.cyol.com/html/2024-11/19/nw.D110000qnzjb_20241119_2-04.htm) discusses this schoolbook poem and distinguishes another, eight-line poem with the same title by Li; the latter is not included. Li's name is also romanized Li Jiao and published estimates of his dates differ; an exact composition year is not asserted.
  - He Zhizhang's four-line *詠柳* follows [卷一百十二, PDF p. 43](https://archive.org/download/06063895.cn/06063895.cn.pdf#page=43). The witness gives `栁枝詞` as an alternate heading; `栁` and `髙` are normalized to `柳` and `高`. The reading retains `綠絲縧`; `綠絲絛` and simplified `绿丝绦` are discoverable alternatives, not separate works. The [Education Bureau](https://www.edb.gov.hk/attachment/tc/curriculum-development/kla/chi-edu/resources/primary/lang/jilei_shi_pdf/jilei_shi_015.pdf) includes the individual poem in primary-school recitation materials.
  - The linked witnesses are scans of the Qing-era *四庫全書* versions of the anthologies; the Archive metadata describes ancient-book reproductions but does not identify the particular manuscript or reproduction edition. Do not treat the scans as first editions or assign them a more specific reproduction date. The historical texts and anthology readings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights differ elsewhere, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished. The verse was independently transcribed from the unpunctuated scans; line breaks and punctuation are supplied and historical glyphs normalized as noted. The modern educational pages document individual reception, not permission to reproduce their commentary or translations.
  - Explicit simplified-character title, text and author-name aliases are search metadata for these records, not additional readings or automatic conversion of prior records. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-chinese-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-chinese-classics-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks the five named witness hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens cannot prove transcription accuracy, exhaust every textual variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Seven individually documented works add **Antonio Machado**'s *Retrato*, *Anoche cuando dormía* and *La saeta*; **Pierre de Ronsard**'s *Mignonne, allons voir si la rose*; **Sara Teasdale**'s *There Will Come Soft Rains*; **Robert Frost**'s *Fire and Ice*; and **William Butler Yeats**'s *Aedh Wishes for the Cloths of Heaven*. Each is counted once, not once per edition, epigraph, musical setting or later title. Printing dates do not establish composition dates and associated countries do not establish composition places. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - *Retrato* follows the Toronto copy of [*Campos de Castilla* (1912), printed pp. 7–9](https://archive.org/details/camposdecastilla00mach/page/n12/mode/1up). It has nine complete quatrains; the scan prints `Hay en mi venas`, apparently omitting an `s`, and retains historical accents. These are not silently replaced by a later edition. [UNED's PoeMAS](https://poemas.uned.es/poema/retrato-antonio-machado/) documents the individual poem and its musical reception.
  - The National Library of Spain copy of [*Poesías completas* (1928), pp. 74–75](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Antonio_Machado_-_Poes%C3%ADas_completas_-_bdh0000252161.pdf?page=80), prints *Anoche cuando dormía* as LX; its third stanza crosses the page break. It clearly reads `Di` and `en donde nunca bebí?`. The earlier [1917 printing](https://archive.org/details/poesascompletas00machgoog/page/n88/mode/1up) also contains the poem, but the marks in the Michigan scan are unclear; we follow the 1928 witness throughout, rather than splice readings. A [modern anthology](https://www.zendalibros.com/anoche-cuando-dormia-antologia-poetica-de-antonio-machado/) takes its title from the poem but reads `de donde nunca bebí?`; its text is not our witness. Editor Arturo Ramoneda's [publication-history note](https://www.alianzaeditorial.es/primer_capitulo/soledades-galerias-otros-poemas.pdf) says this poem was added in 1917, despite sources associating it with the 1907 collection; we do not reproduce the modern edition.
  - *La saeta* occupies [pp. 206–207](https://archive.org/details/poesascompletas00machgoog/page/n213/mode/1up) of the Michigan copy of *Poesías completas* (1917). Its four introductory lines are explicitly attributed in the scan to `SAETA POPULAR.`; the complete sixteen-line Machado poem follows. The epigraph and its attribution are included and identified, not attributed to Machado or counted as another work. The displayed line count is 21 because it includes the epigraph and attribution. The [1914 periodical version](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Nuevo_mundo_2_abril_de_1914.png) lacks that introductory quatrain; both printings are one work. [UNED's PoeMAS](https://poemas.uned.es/poema/la-saeta-antonio-machado/) documents later musical settings without granting rights in them.
  - Ronsard's three stanzas follow [Hugues Vaganay's 1923 edition, tome 3, pp. 75–76](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Ronsard_-_Œuvres_complètes,_Garnier,_1923,_tome_3.djvu?page=91), headed `A CASSANDRE. ODE XVII.` The familiar opening is the display title; `avoit desclose`, `beautez`, `vostre`, `A point` and `O vrayment` follow the scan, not later modernizations. Stanza breaks follow its indentations. [Radio France](https://www.radiofrance.fr/franceinfo/podcasts/ces-chansons-qui-font-l-ete/ces-chansons-qui-font-l-ete-mignonne-allons-voir-si-la-rose-en-1576-et-pour-des-siecles-8846162) discusses the poem and its reception; musical settings are not included.
  - Teasdale's six couplets follow [*Flame and Shadow* (1920), pp. 89–90](https://archive.org/details/flameandshadow00teasgoog/page/n107/mode/1up); the poem's heading prints the subtitle `(War Time)`. [Poetry Database](https://www.poetrydatabase.com/poems/there-will-come-soft-rains) notes that the 1918 version reads `calling` rather than this printing's `circling`; it is not counted separately. [American Literature](https://americanliterature.com/author/sara-teasdale/poem/there-will-come-soft-rains) includes the poem in its 100 Great Poems; a [Bryn Mawr exhibit](https://jvergara.digital.brynmawr.edu/scalarexhibitions/con/coil-oil-nuclear-energy-extraction/there-will-come-soft-rains-1) discusses its use in Ray Bradbury's later story. Frost's nine lines follow [*New Hampshire* (1923), p. 80](https://archive.org/details/NewHampshireRobertFrost1923/page/n94/mode/1up): the scan reads `To say`, while the [1920 *Harper's* printing](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:FireFrost20.jpg) reads `To know`. [Oliver Tearle](https://interestingliterature.com/2019/12/analysis-robert-frost-fire-and-ice/) describes this individual poem as widely anthologized.
  - Yeats's eight lines follow [*The Wind Among the Reeds* (1899), p. 60](https://archive.org/details/windamongreeds00yeat/page/n71/mode/1up), including `half light` without a hyphen. The later title *He Wishes for the Cloths of Heaven* is provided for discovery, not counted as another poem; [Oliver Tearle](https://interestingliterature.com/2015/11/06/a-short-analysis-of-yeatss-he-wishes-for-the-cloths-of-heaven/) discusses that title and the poem's reception.
  - These historical printings are public domain in the United States. Machado died in 1939, Ronsard in 1585, editor Vaganay in 1936, Teasdale in 1933, Frost in 1963 and Yeats in 1939; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The scans are linked, not republished; readings are transcribed from historical pages with indentation, spacing and apostrophe/dash typography normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-lyric-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-lyric-classics-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the seven named witness hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens cannot prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Five individually documented works add **Eino Leino**'s *Hyvä on hiihtäjän hiihdellä*, *Laulu onnesta*, *Hautalaulu* and *Mirjamin laulu*, and **Christopher Marlowe**'s *The Passionate Shepherd to His Love*. These are complete, separately bounded readings, not additional editions, translations or internal sections. Printing dates are not necessarily composition dates; associated countries do not establish composition places. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - The Finnish readings follow [*Hiihtäjän virsiä* (Helsinki, 1900)](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907469). The scan's actual printed pages are [70–71](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907469?page=80), [84](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907469?page=94), [88](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907469?page=98) and [89–90](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907469?page=99), respectively; the contents page lists the last three one page too early. *Hautalaulu* retains the printed `ijäks`. [Kainuu's Eino Leino society](https://www.kainuuneinoleinoseura.fi/eino-leino/runoteokset/) names these four individually among the collection's well-known poems. Its separately listed *Yksin hiihtäjä* is a section of *Metsän kosija* and is not counted here.
  - Marlowe's six stanzas follow the Cornell copy of [*The Oxford Book of English Verse* (1912), printed pp. 173–174](https://archive.org/details/cu31924011997909/page/n188/mode/1up). The independently presented [Academy of American Poets reading](https://poets.org/poem/passionate-shepherd-his-love) has the same six-stanza boundary but different wording, including `valleys, groves, hills, and fields` rather than this printing's `hills and valleys, dales and fields`. The editor, Arthur Quiller-Couch, says in his preface that he modernized spelling and sometimes excised or extracted material; the following separately headed reply is not included. The [Poetry Foundation's unscientific survey](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/articles/68445/hit-singles) found this individual poem in ten of seventeen selected anthologies.
  - The 1900 and 1912 printings are public domain in the United States; Leino died in 1926, Marlowe in 1593 and Quiller-Couch in 1944. Copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The scans are linked, not republished; the readings were transcribed from them with spacing, indentation and apostrophe/dash typography normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-early-classics.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-early-classics-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two witness hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens do not prove transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Four individually documented Finnish works introduce **Eino Leino**'s *Nocturne* and **Aleksis Kivi**'s *Kaukametsä*, *Sunnuntai* and *Onnelliset*. They are complete, separately bounded readings, not variants, translations or parts of another work. Printing dates do not establish composition dates; Finland is an associated place, not a claim about composition location. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - *Nocturne* follows the National Library of Finland's [*Talvi-yö* (1905), printed pp. 34–35 / scan pp. 42–43](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/teos/binding/1907397?page=42). The scan reads `En ma` where some transcriptions read `En mä`; both occurrences follow the scan. The stanza break at the page boundary is editorial. Other versions, including the three-stanza manuscript [described by Finnish Wikipedia](https://fi.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturne_%28runo%29), differ and are not counted separately. [Yle](https://yle.fi/a/20-150917) identifies the poem as one of Leino's best loved; the recorded year identifies this printing, not a composition or first-publication date.
  - Kivi's three poems follow the separately headed readings in [*Kirjallinen Kuukauslehti*, 1 August 1866, pp. 185–186](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/aikakausi/binding/111042?page=11); the issue credits them to `A. K.`, and the contents of [*Runot; Lyhyet kertomukset* (1916)](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/12907) attribute the journal poems to Kivi and list the three titles separately. The 1916 text is not the transcription witness. *Kaukametsä* retains `Alas kalliolta` and `ilmanrannat`, unlike later versions; a [2020 literary study](https://journal.fi/joutsen-svanen-erikois-special/article/view/100563/58086) calls it one of Kivi's best-known poems. *Sunnuntai* retains `Kultaset santarannat` and `oil` as printed, rather than substituting different later readings. *Onnelliset* retains the third refrain's `Ja linnut laulelee` without `ne`, and its stanza-specific punctuation. A [2009 review](https://www.ts.fi/a/80057) names the latter two among Kivi's much-loved poems. These were journal publications, not poems from *Kanervala*.
  - The historical printings are public domain in the United States; Kivi died in 1872 and Leino in 1926. The [National Library's FAQ](https://digi.kansalliskirjasto.fi/faq?set_language=en) describes Finland's ordinary life-plus-70-year term and says public-domain material may be used with citation. Copyright and moral rights vary by jurisdiction, so check local law. The scans are linked, not republished; these readings were independently transcribed from them, with indentation, spacing and quotation-mark typography normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-finnish.py --check` verifies the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-finnish-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two witness hashes. Hashes and duplicate screens cannot establish transcription accuracy, exclude every translation or variant, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Four individually documented works add **W. E. Henley**'s *Invictus*, **Gabriela Mistral**'s *Balada*, **Rainer Maria Rilke**'s *Herbsttag* and **Rubén Darío**'s *Lo fatal*. Each is a complete reading, not an additional edition, translation or separately counted section. Associated countries do not assert composition places; printing dates need not be composition dates. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - *Invictus* follows the complete four stanzas in [*A Book of Verses*, fourth edition (New York, 1893), pp. 56–57](https://archive.org/details/bookofverses00henluoft/page/n73/mode/1up). The historical heading is `IV`, with a dedication to `R. T. H. B.` and a printed date `1875`; the familiar title *Invictus* was attached later. [Victorian Web](https://www.victorianweb.org/authors/henley/invictus.html) discusses the individual poem and shows a later edition with a different date; neither the title nor that version is another work.
  - *Balada* follows [*Desolación* (New York, 1922), pp. 112–113](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu?page=134). Its four six-line stanzas are included; the final stanza appears on the next page. The scan prints initial `El` without an accent and no punctuation after `yo le vi pasar`; those readings are retained rather than silently modernized. The reading adapts the Spanish Wikisource contributor transcriptions, pinned to revisions [1604056](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Página:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu/134&oldid=1604056) and [1604054](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Página:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu/135&oldid=1604054), under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). A [1976 University of Chile journal essay](https://revistaliteratura.uchile.cl/index.php/RCL/article/download/41803/43356/145730) calls the poem well known and analyzes it individually; disputed biographical interpretations are not presented as fact. A [2009 UNAM selection](https://materialdelectura.unam.mx/images/stories/pdf5/gabriela-mistral-65.pdf) independently includes the poem; its later reading is not reproduced here.
  - *Herbsttag* follows the uncropped University of Wisconsin copy of [*Das Buch der Bilder*, second expanded edition, printed p. 48 / PDF p. 49](https://archive.org/download/DasBuchDerBilder2/DasBuchDerBilder2.pdf#page=49), catalogued as 1906. The text adapts [German Wikisource contributors' revision 2353125](https://de.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Herbsttag&oldid=2353125) under CC BY-SA 4.0; its Austrian National Library scan crops the right edges of two lines, so the complete Wisconsin page was consulted too. Historical `Deinen`, `laß`, `gieb` and the sparer punctuation are retained. [EBSCO](https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/autumn-day-rainer-maria-rilke) identifies a 1906 publication, while some other sites call the 1902 composition date the first publication. [Deutschland-Lese](https://www.deutschland-lese.de/streifzuege/gedichte/herbstgedichte/herbsttag/) reports a 2000 WDR popularity poll for this individual poem.
  - *Lo fatal* follows the BNE copy of the [1905 Madrid printing, PDF p. 187](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Cantos_de_vida_y_esperanza_(1905).pdf?page=187); its title page also says 1905. The complete thirteen-line reading, dedicated to René Pérez, is followed by `FIN`. The historical capitalization, `á donde` and unpaired final exclamation mark are preserved. The [2013 book description](https://www.elem.mx/obra/datos/222232) names it as a classic, and a [scholarly study](https://hpr-ojs-tamu.tdl.org/hpr/article/download/208/193) discusses it as the book's concluding poem. Neither modern commentary nor translations are reproduced.
  - These historical printings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary by jurisdiction, including Chile, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-crossroads.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-crossroads-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the named witness hashes. Normalized spacing and indentation, transcription error and edition variants remain possible; hashes and duplicate screens cannot settle rights in every jurisdiction or exclude every translated duplicate.

- Two individually documented works add **Arthur Rimbaud**'s *Bateau ivre* and **Gabriela Mistral**'s *Los sonetos de la muerte*. They are complete readings, not additional printings, translations or individual parts of a cycle. Print dates do not establish composition dates, and associated countries are not claims about composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Rimbaud's 25 quatrains follow the University of Michigan copy of [*Poésies complètes*, pp. 17–22](https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_EZA7AQAAMAAJ/page/n37/mode/1up), whose [publisher's wrapper](https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_EZA7AQAAMAAJ/page/n4/mode/1up) is dated **1895**; the precise impression is not established. The printed heading is *Bateau ivre*; *Le Bateau ivre* is a familiar discovery title. The Michigan scan prints `vos lyres`, `Sons songer`, `mufle`, `Planche folle` and `des Maelstroms`; the separate [Gallica-derived Wikisource transcription](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Poésies_(Rimbaud)/éd._Vanier,_1895/Le_Bateau_ivre) has `nos lyres`, `Sans songer`, `muffle`, `Plante folle` and `les Maelstroms`. These examples do not establish why the readings differ or prove separate impressions. The selected scan's potentially erroneous readings are retained, not silently replaced. [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/topic/The-Drunken-Boat) and the [Poetry Foundation](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/poets/arthur-rimbaud) discuss the individual poem; the [BnF catalogue](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb11946581b) identifies its earlier publication in *Lutèce* in 1883.
  - Mistral's [*Desolación* (New York, 1922), pp. 118–120](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu?page=140), prints three numbered sonnets beneath one title. All three are included and counted as **one cycle**; its displayed line count includes the three Roman numeral headings. The third sonnet crosses the final two scan pages. The reading adapts the scan-checked Spanish Wikisource contributor transcriptions, pinned to revisions [1604047](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Página:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu/140&oldid=1604047), [1604046](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Página:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu/141&oldid=1604046) and [1604045](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Página:Desolación_(colección_de_Mistral,_1922).djvu/142&oldid=1604045), under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). The [Poetry Foundation](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/poets/gabriela-mistral) discusses the cycle's 1914 prize and reception; its modern commentary and translation are not reproduced.
  - The 1895 French and 1922 U.S. printings are public domain in the United States. Copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, including Chile, so check local law. The scans are linked, not republished; spacing and typography are normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-atlantic.py --check` reproduces the records from the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-atlantic-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks the two historical scan hashes named in the importer. Hashes and duplicate screens preserve reviewed inputs but cannot prove transcription accuracy, identify every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Four individually documented, complete works add **Akiko Yosano**'s *君死にたまふことなかれ*, **John Keats**'s *La Belle Dame Sans Mercy*, and **Paul Laurence Dunbar**'s *We Wear the Mask* and *Sympathy*. The source dates identify printings, not necessarily composition dates; associated countries do not assert composition locations. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Yosano's poem first appeared in *Myōjō* in 1904. This selection follows [Aozora Bunko's transcription](https://www.aozora.gr.jp/cards/000318/files/2086_15764.html) of the 1905 *恋衣* printing, based on the Japanese Museum of Modern Literature's 1980 photographic reprint. Its [catalogue record](https://www.aozora.gr.jp/cards/000318/card2086.html) credits inputter 武田秀男 and proofreader kazuishi and dates this file's registration and update to 2 June 2004; Aozora's [usage terms](https://www.aozora.gr.jp/guide/kijyunn.html) permit reuse of expired-copyright works. It uses modern kanji with historical kana. Ruby readings and indentation are omitted here; the prefatory line and all five eight-line stanzas are retained. We did **not** inspect the historical scan directly. The 1904 and later printings differ; they are not combined or counted as additional works. The [Asahi Shimbun](https://www.asahi.com/ajw/articles/14626592) discusses the poem's reception, not permission to reuse a modern translation.
  - Keats's twelve-stanza reading is transcribed from [*The Indicator*, 10 May 1820, p. 248](https://johnkeats.uvic.ca/images/facsimiles/laBelleDameIndicator.jpg); the page's `Caviare` signature is not treated as verse. The familiar alternative title *La Belle Dame sans Merci* is used for discovery, with the historical title also shown. The printing reads `wretched wight` twice, `sedge is wither'd`, and `thy cheek`. [Mapping Keats's Progress](https://johnkeats.uvic.ca/1819-04-21.html) discusses the first printing and a different surviving version; the origin of the textual differences is uncertain. Some online transcriptions presented as the 1820 printing mix readings, so the scanned page, not such a composite, is the witness. These versions are one work.
  - Dunbar's *We Wear the Mask* follows [*Lyrics of Lowly Life* (1896), printed p. 167](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Lyrics_of_Lowly_Life_Dunbar_(1896).djvu?page=193). Its complete three-stanza reading adapts the scan-checked [English Wikisource page, revision 15845393](https://en.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Page%3ALyrics_of_Lowly_Life_Dunbar_%281896%29.djvu%2F193&oldid=15845393), with contributor credit under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). *Sympathy* follows [*Lyrics of the Hearthside* (1899), pp. 40–41](https://collections.libraries.indiana.edu/wyliehouse/items/show/743); Indiana University's museum record joins images of the two excerpts. Both readings rejoin hanging wraps. [Wright State](https://webapp2.wright.edu/web1/newsroom/2023/06/28/wright-state-special-collections-and-archives-conserves-rare-books-by-paul-laurence-dunbar/) identifies the two poems among Dunbar's best-known works and notes the connection between *Sympathy*'s final line and Maya Angelou's autobiography; the later book is not included or counted as a poem here.
  - The historical originals are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-readings.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-readings.txt); adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the four witness files and hashes named in the importer. Hashes and duplicate screens cannot establish transcription accuracy, exclude every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Six individually documented works follow historical printings: **Maria Konopnicka**, *Rota* (1915); **Adam Mickiewicz**, *Romantyczność* (1899); **Rudyard Kipling**, *If—* (1910); **Matthew Arnold**, *Dover Beach* (1897); **César Vallejo**, the title poem *Los heraldos negros* (1918 title-page date; distribution reported in 1919); and **Garcilaso de la Vega**, sonnet XXIII (1911). These are six works, not six editions or translations. Each reader links a source witness and a separate source discussing the individual work. Print dates do not establish composition dates, and associated countries are not claims about composition locations.
  - *Rota* follows the [1915 critical edition's main text](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Maria_Konopnicka-Poezye_T._7_020.jpg), which ends after three six-line stanzas. Its [apparatus](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Maria_Konopnicka-Poezye_T._7_246.jpg) records an additional stanza under `A`, defined as an autograph on the [abbreviations page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Maria_Konopnicka-Poezye_T._7_244.jpg). Other versions have four stanzas; neither that variant nor a musical adaptation is counted as another work. The [Konopnicka Museum](https://muzeumzarnowiec.pl/rota-polska-piesn-patriotyczna/) describes the poem's public life; accounts of the first publication differ.
  - *Romantyczność* spans the 1899 printing's [pp. 85–87](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Poezye_Adama_Mickiewicza._T._1._(1899)_097.jpg). Its English Shakespeare epigraph, attribution and Polish rendering are retained as printed, before the complete ballad; the following *Świteź* is excluded. The displayed line count includes the six epigraph and attribution lines, and the four English lines are marked with `lang="en"`; they are not a translation of the Polish ballad. The linked Polish educational resource discusses this ballad as a Romantic manifesto.
  - *If—* follows the 1910 [pp. 181–182](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Rewards_and_Fairies_(Kipling,_1910).djvu?page=207), rejoining the third stanza over a page break. *Dover Beach* follows [pp. 213–214](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:The_poetical_works_of_Matthew_Arnold,_1897.djvu?page=251) of the 1897 printing and reads `moon-blanched sand`; the linked Poetry Foundation comparison reads `moon-blanched land`. These editions are not silently mixed. The four Polish and English readings adapt scan-checked, pinned Wikisource page transcriptions under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); each reader credits the contributors and links all revisions.
  - Vallejo's [title poem](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Los_heraldos_negros.pdf?page=14) occupies printed pp. 3–4, not the whole same-named book. The scan prints `mas`, `talvez`, `caidas`, `Esos` and a comma after `Muerte`, with unpaired exclamation marks; these are retained rather than silently replaced by a modern version. The [University of Yucatán essay](https://www.revistauniversitaria.uady.mx/pdf/274/ru274-8.pdf) discusses the opening poem and the distinction between the 1918 imprint and 1919 distribution. The original Spanish poem was transcribed from the scan; no modern translation or commentary is reproduced.
  - Garcilaso's [1911 sonnet](https://archive.org/details/obra00vega/page/n258/mode/1up) is on printed p. 231, and its note is not part of the verse. The edition's editor Tomás Navarro Tomás says his text follows Herrera with spelling changes. Its fourth line is `enciende el corazón y lo refrena`; [another circulated reading](https://www.poesi.as/gvsone23.htm) has `con clara luz la tempestad serena` and an extra `de` in the opening line. The comparison is not substituted or counted as a second work. Stanza breaks are editorial. [Project Gutenberg eBook 68131](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/68131), credited to Ramón Pajares Box and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team, is a comparison: its transcription modernizes spelling, and the historical scan is the reading witness. The editor died in 1979; potential edition-related rights outside the United States require separate assessment.
  - These historical printings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary by jurisdiction, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished; no translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications were added. Typography and indentation are normalized. `python3 scripts/import-recognized-global.py --check` reproduces the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/recognized-global-readings.txt); optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` checks the historical PDF and page-image hashes listed in the importer. Hashes and normalized duplicate screens cannot prove a transcription, rule out every variant or translation, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.

- Thirteen individually documented works add a Spanish-language poem by Filipino writer **José Rizal**, three Russian lyrics by **Alexander Pushkin**, and nine English works by **William Wordsworth**, **Percy Bysshe Shelley**, **W. B. Yeats**, **Emma Lazarus**, **Robert Frost** and **Langston Hughes**. These are complete works, not additional editions or translated duplicates; one separately sourced English translation is attached to Rizal. Each reader links its edition and an individual reception or contextual source. Associated countries do not assert composition places, and printing dates are not necessarily composition dates.
  - Rizal's farewell is known as *Mi último adiós*; the [National Library of the Philippines](https://web.nlp.gov.ph/nlp-heritage-month-ep-3-mi-ultimo-adios/) notes that its manuscript had no title. The selected [1903 Barcelona printing](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Noli_me_t%C3%A1ngere_(1903).pdf?page=11) calls it *Mi último pensamiento* and gives fourteen five-line stanzas on scan pp. 11–13. Its last stanza prints `an el`, an apparent typographical error retained in this reading. Other witnesses differ substantially: [Retana's 1907 printing](https://archive.org/download/vidayescritosde00unamgoog/vidayescritosde00unamgoog.pdf#page=442) says its text came from Paciano Rizal, acknowledges variants and appears to print `sangro` in stanza 3; it is comparison evidence, not silently combined with the 1903 text. The manuscript is undated, and the printing's account of composition is not treated as proof of an exact date.
  - Charles E. Derbyshire's *My Last Farewell* is separately credited and printed in Camilo Osias's [*Rizal and Education* (1921), appendix A](https://archive.org/details/ahz9301.0001.001.umich.edu/page/n79/mode/1up), printed pp. 76–80. Its complete fourteen-stanza English verse interpretation is attached to the Spanish work, not counted as another poem or described as a literal translation of this particular 1903 printing. The Spanish original and this independently dated translation are public domain in the United States; local terms and moral rights differ. Printed line wraps and typography are normalized; no modern translation is reused.
  - The three Pushkin readings follow P. A. Efremov's [1903 volume 2](https://archive.org/details/sochinenijaaspushkinat2liricheskies82): *К А. П. Керн* spans PDF pp. 37–38, *Я вас любил* is on p. 193, and *Зимнее утро* spans pp. 211–212. Pre-reform Cyrillic spelling is modernized for reading, with punctuation and typography normalized; the historical printed wording is retained. The printed dates and footnotes are not verse. The edition and original texts are public domain in the United States; check local law.
  - Wordsworth's 1815 *Daffodils* is the expanded four-stanza reading with `shew`; Shelley's 1819 *Ozymandias* retains `desart`. Lazarus's 1889 *The New Colossus* spans two printed pages, not just its famous excerpt. Yeats's *The Lake Isle of Innisfree* follows the 1920 New York anthology *The Year's at the Spring*; a modern reception page's attribution to an 1889 edition is not used as the source date. Frost's two readings follow *New Hampshire* (1923). Hughes's three readings follow *The Weary Blues* (1926), including *I, Too*, printed there as *Epilogue* with `I'll sit at the table`, not the wording of a later edition. These pre-1931 originals and printings are public domain in the United States; in particular, later Frost and Hughes editions and non-US terms require separate assessment.
  - The English poems adapt pinned, scan-checked English Wikisource contributor transcriptions under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), credited in each reader; the other readings were transcribed from the cited historical printings. Scans are linked, not republished. Long wraps, indentation and some typography are normalized, and transcription error remains possible. Reception links document discussion of the individual works, not permission to reproduce modern commentary, illustrations or recordings.
  - `python3 scripts/import-world-classics.py --check` reproduces the selection from the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/world-classics-readings.txt). The optional `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the exact two PDFs and scan images identified by filename and hash in the importer. These checks preserve reviewed inputs but do not prove every transcription, settle local rights, or exclude every translated or variant duplicate.

- Three individually documented **Rabindranath Tagore** works follow the 1916 *Kabyagrantha* printings: *Naibedya* 72 on [volume 7, printed p. 91](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:কাব্যগ্রন্থ_(সপ্তম_খণ্ড).pdf?page=115), *Banglar Mati Banglar Jol* on [volume 10, p. 187](https://archive.org/download/in.ernet.dli.2015.356788/2015.356788.Kabya-GranthaVol10.pdf#page=198), and *Ami Chini Go Chini Tomare* on [volume 10, p. 105](https://archive.org/download/in.ernet.dli.2015.356788/2015.356788.Kabya-GranthaVol10.pdf#page=116). Each is a complete work bounded by a number or separators. Their catalogue titles use the openings; romanized titles aid discovery. India and Bangladesh are cultural associations, not claims about composition locations.
  - Both title pages print **1916**: [volume 7](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:কাব্যগ্রন্থ_(সপ্তম_খণ্ড).pdf?page=11) and [volume 10](https://archive.org/download/in.ernet.dli.2015.356788/2015.356788.Kabya-GranthaVol10.pdf#page=10). Commons metadata labels the volume 7 copy 1920; the printed date, not that catalogue label or the evidence filename, identifies this witness. Printed years are not asserted as composition dates. The historical Bengali readings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. Scans are linked, not republished.
  - The selected texts and boundaries were checked against these scans; punctuation, spacing and some spellings are normalized. The corresponding Bengali Wikisource page transcriptions were **not proofread** and were consulted and corrected against the scans, not accepted as verified text. Each reader credits its pinned contributor revision under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). The Commons volume 10 page numbering is one higher than the linked Archive PDF's physical page. No transcription method or hash guarantees error-free reading or an exhaustive variant collation.
  - *Naibedya* 72 includes `নিজ হস্তে নির্দয় আঘাত করি’ পিতঃ / ভারতেরে সেই স্বর্গে কর জাগরিত` in this printing. Tagore's English *Gitanjali* 35, printed by Macmillan in **1913**, is a free self-reworking of that earlier Bengali poem, not a translation made from the 1916 printing; its country-awakening ending differs. The complete English reading was checked against printed pp. [27](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Gitanjali.djvu?page=57)–[28](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Gitanjali.djvu?page=58), and the consulted English Wikisource revisions are credited in the translation's rights note. The 1913 English text is independently public domain in the United States; check local law. The self-reworking is attached to the Bengali poem, not counted as another work.
  - Individual reception is documented for [*Chitto Jetha Bhayshunyo*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitto_Jetha_Bhayshunyo), [*Banglar Mati Banglar Jol*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banglar_Mati_Banglar_Jol), adopted as West Bengal's state song by its assembly in 2023, and [*Ami Chini Go Chini Tomare*](https://www.jiosaavn.com/song/ami-chini-go-chini-tomare-from-charulata/MQMbHBh2VHg), sung by Kishore Kumar in Satyajit Ray's 1964 film [*Charulata*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charulata). The linked recording is evidence of circulation, not reused or licensed here. The five-stanza *Jana Gana Mana* is not included in this batch because one word in the 1916 scan needs further verification; its anthem excerpt must not be counted separately.
  - `python3 scripts/import-tagore-1916.py --check` reproduces the records from the [reading snapshot](../scripts/sources/tagore-1916-readings.json). Adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` verifies the two historical PDFs and the two English scan images against the filenames and hashes in the importer. Normalized duplicate screening does not exclude every variant or translation already in the catalogue, and none of these checks settles rights in other jurisdictions.

- Three individually documented Bengali songs by **Rabindranath Tagore** are directly transcribed from the Majumdar Library [*Baul* scan](https://archive.org/details/baul_20200913). *Amar Sonar Bangla*, *O Amar Desher Mati* and *Ekla Chalo Re* begin on printed pp. 9, 12 and 18, or physical PDF pages [8](https://archive.org/download/baul_20200913/10804-baul%20%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%B2.pdf#page=8), [11](https://archive.org/download/baul_20200913/10804-baul%20%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%B2.pdf#page=11) and [17](https://archive.org/download/baul_20200913/10804-baul%20%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%B2.pdf#page=17), respectively; their endings are on PDF pages 10, 12 and 18. The index, each complete song and adjacent work boundaries were inspected. The printed headings are সোনার বাংলা, দেশের মাটি and একা; familiar romanized titles are discovery labels. India and Bangladesh are cultural associations, not claims about composition locations.
  - Archive catalogues the book as **1905**, but its title page has no printed year. A [library stamp](https://archive.org/download/baul_20200913/10804-baul%20%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%B2.pdf#page=3) reads 1907, and independent song bibliographies describe a Majumdar Library *Baul* in Bengali year 1312 with the same [printed pages 9–11](http://www.onushilon.org/corpus/robi/songinfo/sodesh/sodesh1.htm), [12–13](http://www.onushilon.org/corpus/robi/songinfo/sodesh/sodesh2.htm), and [18–19](http://www.onushilon.org/corpus/robi/songinfo/sodesh/sodesh3.htm). This supports an early printing but is not a printed date on this copy. The historical texts are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere. The scan is linked, not republished. No translations or recordings are included.
  - The edition's [printed refrain for *Ekla*](https://archive.org/download/baul_20200913/10804-baul%20%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%B2.pdf#page=18) repeats at the end and uses চলরে, unlike the later [Wikisource comparison, revisions 1696736](https://bn.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E0%A6%AA%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%3A%E0%A6%97%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BF%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A8.djvu%2F%E0%A7%A9%E0%A7%AB%E0%A7%AE&oldid=1696736) and [1696738](https://bn.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E0%A6%AA%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%3A%E0%A6%97%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%AC%E0%A6%BF%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A8.djvu%2F%E0%A7%A9%E0%A7%AB%E0%A7%AF&oldid=1696738). *Desher Mati* reads মিছে কাজে rather than the later বৃথা কাজে. In *Sonar Bangla*, the early কিন্ব না তোর is normalized here to কিনব না তোর, not silently replaced by the later কিনব না আর, মা, তোর. Punctuation, spacing and some other spellings are normalized, including printed পল্লিবাটে as পল্লীবাটে; this is not a diplomatic facsimile. The transcriptions do not incorporate the later comparison's wording, and human transcription errors remain possible.
  - Individual reception is documented for [*Amar Sonar Bangla*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amar_Sonar_Bangla), whose opening excerpt became Bangladesh's national anthem, [*O Amar Desher Mati*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Amar_Desher_Mati), including its appearance in a 1972 film, and [*Ekla Chalo Re*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekla_Chalo_Re). An anthem excerpt is not counted separately from the complete song; the inclusion of the other songs is based on individual evidence, not merely the fame of the collection.
  - `python3 scripts/import-tagore-baul.py --check` reproduces these records from the [transcription snapshot](../scripts/sources/tagore-baul-readings.json). Save the linked historical PDF as `tagore-baul-1905-discovery.pdf` and add `--evidence-dir <directory>` to verify the reviewed PDF hash. Hashes preserve the inputs but do not prove every transcription, identify every variant or translation already in the catalogue, or establish rights in every jurisdiction.

- Two individually documented **Hafez** ghazals introduce Persian discovery. The first follows John Richardson's [*A Specimen of Persian Poetry* (1802)](https://archive.org/details/aspecimenpersia00revigoog), revised by S. Rousseau. Its [title page](https://archive.org/download/aspecimenpersia00revigoog/aspecimenpersia00revigoog.pdf#page=8) dates that edition; the complete seven-couplet Persian/Arabic ode spans physical PDF pages [24](https://archive.org/download/aspecimenpersia00revigoog/aspecimenpersia00revigoog.pdf#page=24)–[25](https://archive.org/download/aspecimenpersia00revigoog/aspecimenpersia00revigoog.pdf#page=25). The second follows the [1902 *Dīwān-i Ḥāfiẓ* scan](https://archive.org/details/dli.ernet.286749), whose [title page](https://archive.org/download/dli.ernet.286749/286749-Diwan%20Hafiz.pdf#page=1) prints 1902. The nine-couplet *Shirazi Turk* is on printed p. 6, [physical PDF page 31](https://archive.org/download/dli.ernet.286749/286749-Diwan%20Hafiz.pdf#page=31), between the final couplet of the preceding poem and the start of the next. Some scanned page pairs are in reverse physical order; printed and PDF page numbers are not interchangeable. These are two works, not multiple editions or fragments. The print years do not establish composition dates; Iran is an associated place, not a documented composition location.
  - Both printings are public domain in the United States. The original texts were transcribed from these scans, not copied from a modern edited text. Arabic and Persian letter forms, spacing, vowel marks and typography are normalized; stanza breaks separate the printed couplets. Copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The scans are linked, not republished. The first and last lines of the 1802 ghazal are Arabic, while its other lines are Persian; the reader marks those lines and its Arabic opening-line title accordingly. The Arabic phrase `عفاک الله` within the 1902 Persian reading is also marked in the reader.
  - Hafez's transmitted texts differ among witnesses; the selected readings do not establish a definitive manuscript text. The 1802 first ode prints `چه جای عیش`, while the independently linked [1941 Qazvini–Ghani comparison, revision 143728](https://fa.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%D8%A8%D8%B1%DA%AF%D9%87%3A%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8_%D9%82%D8%B2%D9%88%DB%8C%D9%86%DB%8C_%D8%BA%D9%86%DB%8C.pdf%2F%DB%B1%DB%B3%DB%B2&oldid=143728) has `چه امن عیش` and reverses the third and fourth couplets. The 1902 *Shirazi Turk* has `بدم گفتی و خرسندم عفاک الله نکو گفتی`; the 1941 text begins that couplet `اگر دشنام فرمایی و گر نفرین دعا گویم` and orders its last interior couplets differently. That comparison spans revisions [143730](https://fa.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%D8%A8%D8%B1%DA%AF%D9%87%3A%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8_%D9%82%D8%B2%D9%88%DB%8C%D9%86%DB%8C_%D8%BA%D9%86%DB%8C.pdf%2F%DB%B1%DB%B3%DB%B3&oldid=143730) and [143731](https://fa.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%D8%A8%D8%B1%DA%AF%D9%87%3A%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8_%D9%82%D8%B2%D9%88%DB%8C%D9%86%DB%8C_%D8%BA%D9%86%DB%8C.pdf%2F%DB%B1%DB%B3%DB%B4&oldid=143731); it is evidence of variations, not the source of the readings here. These examples are not an exhaustive collation. The earlier attribution of the first poem's opening Arabic line is also [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al%C4%81_y%C4%81_ayyoha-s-s%C4%81q%C4%AB).
  - In [his analysis of the Qazvini–Ghani version](https://irajbashiri.com/Hafiz/Hafiz.html), Iraj Bashiri argues that the advice and insult-response couplets may be later additions. That is a scholarly hypothesis, not proof about the different insult-response line in the 1902 printing. The low-resolution 1902 scan also leaves the reading `کاین` versus `کین` and the beginning of `به آب و رنگ` uncertain; the transcription records our tentative reading rather than silently substituting another edition.
  - Richardson's complete seven-stanza English **paraphrase**, as printed on PDF pages [25](https://archive.org/download/aspecimenpersia00revigoog/aspecimenpersia00revigoog.pdf#page=25)–[27](https://archive.org/download/aspecimenpersia00revigoog/aspecimenpersia00revigoog.pdf#page=27), is attached to the first poem and not counted as a separate work. The 1802 edition is public domain in the United States; check local law. It is a free literary interpretation, not a literal translation: it adds Leila and a female beloved where the Persian does not specify one. Long-s letterforms, punctuation, indentation and spacing are normalized; historical contractions remain. The mark after `look'd` is unclear in the scan. No modern English translation, recording or scholarly commentary is reproduced.
  - The [first ode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al%C4%81_y%C4%81_ayyoha-s-s%C4%81q%C4%AB) and [*Shirazi Turk*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirazi_Turk) each have separate discussions of their textual histories and individual reception. Their interpretations are contested; neither a religious denomination nor a devotional theme is inferred from the poet's identity. The 1802 garden ghazal is excluded: its ten-couplet reading includes a couplet not in the 1941 edition and omits another found there, so that difference needs further review rather than silent splicing.
  - `python3 scripts/import-hafez-persian.py --check` reproduces the records from the [reviewed transcriptions](../scripts/sources/hafez-persian-readings.json). To verify the exact historical witnesses too, save the 1802 and 1902 PDFs as `hafez-richardson-1802-research.pdf` and `hafez-divan-dli-1902-metadata-only.pdf` and add `--evidence-dir <directory>`. Hashes and line counts preserve the reviewed inputs, but cannot prove a transcription, establish a definitive text, exclude every variant or translation already in the catalogue, or settle rights in every jurisdiction.
