- Seven individually circulating **Kabir-attributed dohas** follow Shyamsundar Das's [*Kabir Granthavali* (1928)](https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.442452). Each reader links its page of the historical PDF and the exact Hindi Wikisource contributor revision used; the PDF's physical page is one greater than the linked Wikisource scan-page number. The opening line supplies the title. The date identifies the edition, not the composition, and India is an associated place rather than a claimed composition location.
  - Kabir's orally transmitted corpus has many variants and [disputed attributions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabir#Authenticity); these are traditional attributions, not a claim that each can be independently authenticated. The readers, page metadata and poet page treat Kabir as an attribution rather than asserting sole authorship of every couplet. The historical mixed North Indian register is often called Hindavi or Sadhukkari. **Hindi** and the `hi` language tag are broad discovery and accessibility labels here, not a claim that the texts are in standardized modern Hindi. Scholarship does not agree on every language label.
  - Each selected two-line couplet has its own number in the 1928 edition; thematic group headings, footnotes and neighboring couplets are not included. A [doha can be an independent, self-contained couplet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doha_(Indian_literature)). Each selected work also appears separately among the early individual entries in [Hindwi's popularity-sorted Kabir listing](https://www.hindwi.org/poets/kabir/dohe?sort=popularity-desc); its ranking method is not stated and does not establish equal or worldwide popularity. The separately grouped textbook selections on that page are not counted as individual works here. Hindwi's text, commentary and recordings are not reused as the source.
  - The 1928 printing is public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The readings adapt the linked Hindi Wikisource contributors' transcriptions under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), independently of the software license; pinned revision histories provide attribution. The historical PDF is linked, not republished. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - Each selected couplet and its boundaries were visually checked against the scan, with spacing and punctuation normalized; this does not establish an exhaustive collation of other printings or oral variants. The page-106 contributor transcription is marked **not proofread** on Wikisource; its selected couplet was checked against the scan. On page 117 the scan has `आसा`, not the linked transcription's `प्रासा`; on page 153 it has `नांहिं`, not `नंहिं`; and on page 156 it has `आया`, not `पाया`. The scan's number for *कबीर यहु घर प्रेम* is 19, while its transcription gives 16; numbers are omitted from the verse. These scan-based corrections are also disclosed in the readers.
  - Hindwi's modern comparison for *माया मुई* ends `यौं कहै दास कबीर`, while the selected printing has `यौं कहि गया कबीर`; its *कबीर यहु घर प्रेम* has `पैठे`, versus `पैसै` in 1928. Its *काबा फिर कासी* has `बैठ कबीर`, while the historical printing has `बैठि कबीरा`. The reasons for differences are not established, and modern wording is not substituted into the historical readings.
  - `python3 scripts/import-kabir-hindavi.py --check` reproduces the records from the [pinned contributor excerpts](../scripts/sources/kabir-hindavi-revisions.json). To verify the reviewed historical witness too, save the linked PDF as `kabir-das-1928-ia442452.pdf` and add `--evidence-dir <directory>`. The excerpts were compared with the cited live revisions; hashes preserve those inputs and the reviewed PDF but do not prove transcription, authorship, work boundaries or rights in every jurisdiction. Normalized duplicate screening cannot exclude every variant or translation elsewhere in the catalogue.

- Eleven individually documented original works add two French poems by **Paul Verlaine**, two German poems by **Rainer Maria Rilke**, two English poems by **W. B. Yeats**, four English poems by **Sarojini Naidu**, and Bengali *Gitanjali* song 21 by **Rabindranath Tagore**. These are eleven works, not eleven editions or translations. Each reader links its historical scan and credits every pinned Wikisource contributor revision used. The historical originals and printings predate 1931 and are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The transcriptions are adapted from the respective Wikisource contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), separately from the software license; linked revision histories provide attribution. Scans are linked, not republished. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are added.
  - The selected readings and their boundaries were checked against the linked page images, while indentation, spacing and some typography were normalized. A printing year is not necessarily a composition date, and an associated country does not establish where a poem was written. Verlaine's [*Poèmes saturniens* cover](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Verlaine_-_Po%C3%A8mes_saturniens,_Lemerre,_1866.djvu?page=11) says **1867**, while its [title page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Verlaine_-_Po%C3%A8mes_saturniens,_Lemerre,_1866.djvu?page=15) says **1866**; both dates are disclosed. Rilke's *Der Panther* follows *Neue Gedichte* (1907), not the separate 1920 printing; annotations on the scan are not treated as verse. *Archaïscher Torso Apollos* follows the 1918 printing.
  - Yeats's [Cuala title page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Michael_Robartes_and_the_Dancer_(1920).djvu?page=1) bears a **1920** imprint; [publication histories](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/articles/70114/william-butler-yeats-easter-1916) describe the book's release as **1921**. *The Second Coming* begins after another poem and ends before the next on its two scan pages. This printing reads `a waste of desert sand` and `Wind shadows`; a [later circulated reading](https://poets.org/anthology/yeats-5) has `somewhere in sands of the desert` and `Reel shadows`. In *Easter, 1916*, the printed date after the verse is excluded; a stanza continues across the page turn. These examples are not an exhaustive comparison of editions.
  - Naidu's 1905 *Indian Weavers* scan has a comma after `solemn and still`, where the linked transcription has a period. The 1905 *Coromandel Fishers* scan says `morning light,`, while its transcription says `morning lights`; both readings here follow the scans. Hanging continuation lines in *Palanquin-Bearers* and *Coromandel Fishers* are rejoined. *Bangle-sellers* follows the 1912 *Bird of Time* and spans two pages. *In the Bazaars of Hyderabad* is not added separately from the numbered *Songs of my City* cycle.
  - Bengali song 21 is a complete numbered reading from the third-edition scan, dated Bengali year 1320 (1913–1914). The opening line supplies its title; the printed Bengali date beneath the verse is excluded. [Gitabitan](https://gitabitan.io/songs/aaji-jhorer-raate/) identifies Tagore's English *Gitanjali* no. 23 as a self-reworking of this song. That English work is neither added nor counted separately; the collections' numbers do not generally correspond. Gitabitan and [the Bengali Wikipedia article](https://bn.wikipedia.org/wiki/আজি_ঝড়ের_রাতে_তোমার_অভিসার) document the song's individual circulation, not rights to modern recordings or translations.
  - Individual reception is also documented for Verlaine's [*Chanson d'automne*](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanson_d%27automne) and [*Mon rêve familier*](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_rêve_familier), [Rilke's *Panther*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Panther_(poem)) and [*Torso*](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaïscher_Torso_Apollos), [both Yeats poems](https://www.poetryfoundation.org/articles/70241/william-butler-yeats-selections), and [the four Naidu poems](https://timesofindia.indiatimes.com/life-style/books/web-stories/8-classic-poems-by-sarojini-naidu-and-their-iconic-themes/photostory/114284217.cms). Reception sources are not the source texts or evidence that all works are equally popular.
  - `python3 scripts/import-recognized-international.py --check` reproduces the reviewed readings and credits and compares their wordings with the [pinned contributor revisions](../scripts/sources/recognized-international-revisions.json), allowing the disclosed `morning light` correction and normalizing case, punctuation, line breaks and spacing for that comparison. Adding `--evidence-dir <directory>` also checks the 18 reviewed page-image hashes listed in the importer. For verification, save the linked historical pages using those filenames, including both pages for each page-spanning poem; scans are not distributed in this repository. Hashes and exact or containment duplicate checks preserve reviewed evidence but cannot prove a transcription, exclude every textual variant or translation, or establish rights in every jurisdiction.

- Fifty-nine Polish originals introduce three writers: 55 separately titled short poems by **Maria Pawlikowska-Jasnorzewska**, three works by **Adam Mickiewicz** and one by **Maria Konopnicka**. The Pawlikowska selections follow [*Pocałunki* (1926)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Maria_Pawlikowska-Jasnorzewska_-_Pocałunki.djvu), printed under the name Maria Pawlikowska; [*Wachlarz* (1927)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Maria_Pawlikowska-Jasnorzewska_-_Wachlarz.djvu) reprints the earlier volume. Mickiewicz's *Stepy akermańskie* follows the [1826 *Sonety*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Adam_Mickiewicz-Sonety_Adama_Mickiewicza_028.jpeg); *Oda do młodości* and *Śmierć pułkownika* follow [*Poezye*, vol. I (1899)](https://pl.wikisource.org/wiki/Poezye_Adama_Mickiewicza._T._1._(1899)). Konopnicka's *A jak poszedł król na wojnę* follows [*Pieśni i piosenki* (1903)](https://pl.wikisource.org/wiki/Poezye_w_nowym_uk%C5%82adzie._Tom_III,_Pie%C5%9Bni_i_piosenki/A_jak_poszed%C5%82). Each reader links to its first scan page and credits the pinned Polish Wikisource transcription; its rights note links all transcriptions used for the reading and, for *Pocałunki*, its 1927 comparison.
  - These pre-1931 originals and printings are public domain in the United States. Copyright and moral rights differ by jurisdiction; check local law. The transcriptions are adapted from Polish Wikisource contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), independently of the software license. Scans are linked, not republished. No translations, recordings or inferred religious classifications are included. Poland is an associated place, not a claim about where a poem was written; a printing year is not a composition date.
  - The 1926 *Pocałunki* [printed index](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Maria_Pawlikowska-Jasnorzewska_-_Pocałunki.djvu?page=45) lists individual titles across four book sections. The selected readings each have their own printed heading and four complete lines; they are not the numbered sections of *Kobieta w morzu* or *Oczy na skrzydłach*, which are excluded. Several, such as [*Miłość*](https://wolnelektury.pl/katalog/lektura/pawlikowska-jasnorzewska-milosc/) and [*Ogród*](https://wolnelektury.pl/katalog/lektura/pawlikowska-jasnorzewska-ogrod/), are also catalogued individually by Wolne Lektury. These facts do not establish that every selection is equally popular. The 1927 book reprints *Pocałunki* as a section: its separate page transcriptions are comparison evidence, not additional works or independent proof of popularity.
  - The Pawlikowska readings use Wikisource page transcriptions marked validated. Selected scan pages, headings and boundaries were inspected; OCR-assisted comparisons helped locate discrepancies but do not establish a line-by-line independent collation. The 1926 and 1927 transcriptions have the same words for the chosen readings after case, spacing and punctuation normalization; this is not an exhaustive variant comparison. The familiar modern spellings *Fotografia*, *Ofelia* and *Żurawie* are editorial display titles alongside the historical titles, not substitutions in the verse. The unselected *Syreny* illustrates why a validated page is not conclusive: the scan appears to read `rozpienione`, while the [transcription](https://pl.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Strona%3AMaria_Pawlikowska-Jasnorzewska_-_Poca%C5%82unki.djvu%2F7&oldid=1984525) reads `rozplenione`. The unverified *Wiersz ukradziony*, multipart pieces, differing readings and scan page 43 are excluded.
  - The separately titled 14-line *Stepy akermańskie* is one sonnet within the Crimean sequence, not a count of the sequence and sonnet twice; the [1826 scan file 028](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PL_Adam_Mickiewicz-Sonety_Adama_Mickiewicza_028.jpeg) shows printed p. 29, not p. 28. Its editorial footnote is excluded from the verse. The 1899 *Oda* runs from printed pp. 237–239 and *Śmierć pułkownika* from pp. 256–257. In the latter, the first scan page reads `przed chatą leśnika`, while the credited transcription has `przed chatką leśnika`; the scan reading is used and the difference disclosed in the reader. Other changes to the credited texts normalize spacing, indentation and typography; this is not an exhaustive collation of variants. Konopnicka's three-stanza 1915 and 1916 printings of *Rota* are excluded because another widely circulated version has four stanzas; the 1915 critical supplement presents an additional stanza as a variant, not as part of that main reading.
  - Konopnicka's song appears without a separate title in the 1903 printing, whose title page identifies Warsaw and Kraków; the opening line supplies the catalogue title. It spans printed pp. 85–86, has six stanzas, and also appears in the [1887 third series](https://pl.wikisource.org/wiki/Poezye._Serya_trzecia/Piosenki_i_pie%C5%9Bni/XII) and separately titled in the [1915 critical edition](https://pl.wikisource.org/wiki/Strona:PL_Maria_Konopnicka-Poezye_T._3_031.jpg). The 1903 printing reads `liliowe`, while 1915 has `liljowe`; the respective texts are not counted twice. The 19th-century era reflects the earlier printing, not an asserted composition date.
  - `python3 scripts/import-polish-classics.py --check` reproduces the selected records from the [pinned page-revision snapshot](../scripts/sources/polish-classics-revisions.json). To check the nine historical scan witnesses as well, save the linked *Pocałunki* DjVu as `pawlikowska-pocalunki-1926.djvu`, the 1826 sonnet page as `mickiewicz-sonety-1826-028.jpg`, 1899 files 249, 251, 268 and 269 as `mickiewicz-poezye-1899-<number>.jpg`, and 1903 files 005, 095 and 096 as `konopnicka-piosenki-1903-<number>.jpg`; add `--evidence-dir <directory>`. Hashes and comparison checks preserve the reviewed inputs; they cannot prove transcription accuracy, completeness, absence of every variant or translated duplicate, or rights in all jurisdictions.

- Seven complete Ukrainian originals by **Taras Shevchenko**, **Lesya Ukrainka**, **Mykhailo Petrenko**, **Leonid Hlibov** and **Mykhailo Starytskyi** follow individually cited historical printings. Five readings come from Oleksa Kovalenko's *Ukrainska Muza*, Kyiv, 1908: Shevchenko's *Заповіт* and *Вечір* and Petrenko's *Дивлюся на небо* from [issue 2](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Украінська_Муза_(Випуск_2).pdf); Hlibov's *Журба* from [issue 3](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Украінська_Муза_(Випуск_3).pdf); and Starytskyi's *Виклик* from [issue 4](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Украінська_Муза_(Випуск_4).pdf). Ukrainka's *Contra spem spero!* and *Надія* follow [*На крилах пісень*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Леся_Українка._На_крилах_пісень._1892.pdf), whose title page prints **1892**; some publication histories give 1893. These records retain the uncertainty instead of using either as a definite numeric year. Each reader links to its first scan page and credits the pinned Ukrainian Wikisource page transcription; the rights note links every page revision used.
  - The historical originals and pre-1931 printings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The transcriptions are adapted from Ukrainian Wikisource contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), independently of the software license. Scans are linked, not republished; no translations are included. Ukraine is an associated place, not a claim that each work was composed there. A date attached to a printing is not a composition date. These are 19th-century works, even when the selected printing appeared in 1908; selection from a well-known poet or anthology does not establish equal individual popularity.
  - Several selections have documented reception as songs, including [*Дивлюсь я на небо*](https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/Дивлюсь_я_на_небо,_та_й_думку_гадаю…), [*Журба*](https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/Стоїть_гора_високая) and [*Виклик*](https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ніч_яка_місячна). Song versions can differ and are not the source of these readings; no recordings or musical settings are included.
  - Each selected text and boundary was compared visually with its source scan and the cited page transcription. The readings retain historical spellings and stress marks but normalize spacing, indentation and some punctuation. The [1876 *Kobzar*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Кобзарь_(1876).djvu?page=546) separately prints a full *Заповіт* with its middle passage, although it reflows lines and has `Були видні, було чути` where 1908 has `Було видно, було чути`. Its separately titled *Вечір* begins on [p. 212](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Кобзарь_(1876).djvu?page=244) and ends on the next page. The 1908 *Заповіт* transcription corrects the final punctuation; the early 1859 printing is not assumed complete.
  - The *На крилах пісень* printing of *Contra spem spero!* spans pp. 13–14 and contains 36 lines, including two stanzas absent from the 28-line [1908 anthology printing](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Украінська_Муза_(Випуск_6).pdf?page=12). The reason for this difference is not established here. *Надія* is separately titled and ten lines in the selected printing and in the [1908 comparison](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Украінська_Муза_(Випуск_6).pdf?page=22); wordings differ. These editions are not counted as additional works.
  - The untitled 1908 *Дивлюся на небо* continues onto printed p. 179 and retains the apparent duplication `У в світі` from the scan. Scholarship in [*Поет-романтик Михайло Петренко*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Poet_romantyk_Mykhaylo_Petrenko_Tvory_ta_materialy.pdf) identifies its separately titled 1841 appearance, *Недоля*, and its later inclusion in the three-poem *Небо* cycle; the catalogue title combines that earlier title and the opening line, and the single poem and later cycle are not counted separately here. No modern editorial text from that study is reproduced. The 1908 *Журба* has `любе літечко` and `бідне серденько`, while the [1922 *Струни* comparison](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Струни._Антольоґія_української_поезії._Т._1.djvu?page=218) reads `тепле літечко` and omits `бідне`. The 1908 *Виклик* retains `місяшна`; its familiar modern spelling `місячна` is added to the catalogue title for discovery, not substituted in the verse. The separate 1922 printing begins on [p. 236](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Струни._Антольоґія_української_поезії._Т._1.djvu?page=246). These examples are not an exhaustive census of textual variants.
  - Reproduce the readings with `python3 scripts/import-ukrainian-classics.py --check`. To verify the scan files too, save the four linked PDFs as `ukrainian-muse-issue2-1908.pdf`, `ukrainian-muse-issue3-1908.pdf`, `ukrainian-muse-issue4-1908.pdf` and `ukrainka-na-krylah-1892.pdf`, then add `--evidence-dir <directory>`. Hashes and line counts preserve reviewed inputs but cannot establish transcription accuracy, completeness, absence of all duplicate versions or rights in every jurisdiction.

- Eleven additional complete readings bring Greek into the catalogue: eight by **C. P. Cavafy** and three Russian poems by **Anna Akhmatova**. No English translation is included or counted as another work. Cavafy's associated country is Egypt, where the periodicals appeared; it is not a claim that each poem was written there. The date on each record identifies its printing, not necessarily composition or first publication.
  - *Ithaka* follows the 1911 *Grámmata* [facsimile, pp. 286–287](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Ithaka_by_Kavafis,_Grammata_magazine_vol_9-10,_1911.pdf). Its last three lines continue onto p. 287. The print retains forms including “τ’ ὡραῖο”, “Ἄλλα”, “ἡ Ἰθάκες” and “ταξείδι”, unlike the linked later Wikisource comparison. *The God Abandons Antony* follows a [different 1911 *Grámmata* page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Γράμματα_Αρ.3_(1911)_-_65.jpg). Six other works are individually titled and complete in the December 1926 and January 1927 *Alexandriní Téchnē* issues. Their linked Greek Wikisource page transcriptions are credited under CC BY-SA 4.0; they were compared with the periodical scans, with historical spellings retained and spacing or punctuation normalized. In *Candles*, the 1926 “μιὰ θλιβερὴ σειρὰ” differs from the later [Centre for the Greek Language](https://www.greek-language.gr/digitalResources/literature/tools/concordance/browse.html?cnd_id=9&text_id=794) reading “μια θλιβερή γραμμή”; in *The Windows*, 1926 “Πλὴν τὰ παράθυρα” differs from “Μα τα παράθυρα” in the [Onassis Archive's 1963 edition](https://www.onassis.org/el/initiatives/cavafy-archive/the-canon/the-windows). These later versions are comparison evidence, not copied into the readings. No modern English Cavafy translation is reused. These printings predate 1931 and are public domain in the United States; check copyright and moral rights elsewhere.
  - The Akhmatova poems are transcribed from her [*Evening* (1912)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Akhmatova_Vecher_1912.pdf), pp. 19, 25–26 and 67, with pre-1918 letters and final hard signs modernized, some historical spelling retained, and spacing or punctuation normalized. The linked Russian Wikisource readings are later comparisons, not the transcription source. The 1912 *Song of the Last Meeting* reads “Я обманут, слышишь, унылый” and “шопот”, unlike the comparison's “Я обманут моей унылой” and “шёпот”; *She Clasped Her Hands* has “до пьяна”, while *The Grey-Eyed King* retains “ея”. These are examples, not an exhaustive census of variants. The 1912 originals are public domain in the United States, but Akhmatova died in 1966 and copyright may persist elsewhere. Check local law. No later translations are included.
  - Reproduce the records with `python3 scripts/import-greek-russian-classics.py --check`; `--evidence-dir <directory>` additionally verifies the six linked scan files against their SHA-256 hashes. Save the original scan files as `cavafy-ithaka-1911.pdf`, `cavafy-antony-1911.jpg`, `cavafy-1926-01.jpg`, `cavafy-1926-02.jpg`, `cavafy-1927-01.jpg` and `akhmatova-vecher-1912.pdf` respectively. Scans are linked from the records rather than republished. Hashes alone cannot establish accuracy, completeness or rights in every jurisdiction. A published anthology or famous author is not evidence that every selected poem is equally popular.

- Fourteen additional works follow three independently identified printed sources. They are eleven Russian poems by **Alexander Pushkin**, **Mikhail Lermontov** and **Nikolai Nekrasov**, two separately numbered anonymous Chinese *Shijing* odes, and Goethe's *Erlkönig*. Their translations are attached to works, not counted as additional poems. A well-known anthology or poet is not evidence that every selected poem is equally popular.
  - The Russian readings follow Semeonoff and Tillyard's [*Russian Poetry Reader* (1917)](https://archive.org/details/semeonoff-tillyard__russian_poetry_reader); its [title page](https://archive.org/download/semeonoff-tillyard__russian_poetry_reader/Semeonoff%20A.%20E.%2C%20Tillyard%20J.%20H.%20J.%20W.%20%28eds.%29%20%20Russian%20poetry%20reader.pdf#page=5) names the editors, publishers and date. Each reader links to its first physical PDF page and to a separately identified, pinned Russian Wikisource comparison. The 1917 printed texts are public domain in the United States; check local law. Scans are linked, not republished. The title-page year is not a composition date; Russia is an associated place, not a claim about composition.
  - Each selected Russian text and boundary was visually checked against its 1917 scan and compared with a separately identified complete edition. Pre-1918 letters and endings are modernized, pedagogical stress marks removed, and spacing, indentation, ellipses and punctuation normalized; some historical spellings remain. Four-line stanza breaks in *Выхожу один я на дорогу* are editorial: the anthology prints it continuously. In *Зимний вечер* the anthology reads `путник запоздалой`, while the comparison has `путник запоздалый`. In *Школьник* the anthology has `Скоро ты узнаешь`, `Тут уж` and `пустых`, against the comparison's `Скоро сам узнаешь`, `Там уж` and `тупых`. The 1917 *Выхожу один я на дорогу* has `Я ищу`, `навеки так заснуть` and `дремали`, against the linked 1843 edition's `И ищу`, `на веки там заснуть` and `дрожали`. The readers disclose these and other variants; this is not an exhaustive collation. The anthology silently shortens Pushkin's *Осень* and *Зимняя дорога* and explicitly extracts a song from a longer work: none is included in this selection.
  - Pushkin's *Памятник* is a distinct Russian imitation, not a literal translation of Horace's ode; its Latin epigraph quotes Horace. A [Bryn Mawr Classical Review discussion](https://bmcr.brynmawr.edu/2010/2010.01.36) describes similarities and differences from Horace's *Carmen* 3.30, including the Russian landscape and political dimension. Neither Horace's poem nor another version is added as a separate work in this batch.
  - Lermontov's *Ангел* includes Babette Deutsch and Avrahm Yarmolinsky's separately dated English version, *The Angel*, from [*Modern Russian Poetry* (1921), p. 18](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Modern_Russian_Poetry.djvu?page=44). The complete English text was checked against the 1921 page; indentation is normalized. This 1921 publication is public domain in the United States. [Deutsch died in 1982](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Author:Babette_Deutsch) and [Yarmolinsky in 1975](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Author:Avrahm_Yarmolinsky), so protection may persist elsewhere; check local law. Their `heavenly gong` and `unuttered Word` elaborate the original: it is a literary interpretation, not a certified literal rendering. No denomination is inferred from the poet's identity.
  - The Chinese *關雎* and *蒹葭* follow the bilingual [*Chinese Classics*, vol. IV, part I (1871)](https://archive.org/details/chineseclassics41legg_0), whose [title page](https://archive.org/download/chineseclassics41legg_0/chineseclassics41legg_0.pdf#page=9) names James Legge. Both the original Chinese and Legge's English versions were checked against their complete printed texts, beginning at PDF pages [205](https://archive.org/download/chineseclassics41legg_0/chineseclassics41legg_0.pdf#page=205) and [399](https://archive.org/download/chineseclassics41legg_0/chineseclassics41legg_0.pdf#page=399). The Chinese readings were compared to separately linked Wikisource versions; Legge prints `淒淒` in *蒹葭*, where the comparison has `萋萋` and records `淒淒` as a variant. Horizontal line breaks and Chinese punctuation are editorial, and English indentation is normalized. Authorship, exact composition dates and places are not established; China is an associated place. These 1871 texts and Legge's translations are public domain in the United States; check local law. His gendered and social descriptions are historical interpretations, not the only possible readings. The commentary and scans are not republished.
  - Goethe's complete *Erlkönig* follows no. 42 in Hatfield's [*German Lyrics and Ballads* (1900), pp. 49–50](https://archive.org/download/germanlyricsball00harfuoft/germanlyricsball00harfuoft.pdf#page=83), compared with the linked German Wikisource reading. The anthology prints `gethan` where that comparison has `getan`. Historical spelling remains; quotation marks and spacing are normalized. This 1900 printed original is public domain in the United States; check local law. Germany and the printing year do not establish a composition location or date. No translation is included.
  - Reproduce the records with `python3 scripts/import-international-classics.py --check`. To verify the exact PDF witnesses as well, save the linked Russian, Chinese and German PDFs as `russian-poetry-reader-1917.pdf`, `shijing-legge-vol4pt1.pdf` and `german-lyrics-1900.pdf` in one directory and run `python3 scripts/import-international-classics.py --check --evidence-dir <directory>`. The importer pins those PDFs and the reviewed transcriptions, but hashes cannot prove visual collation, completeness, or rights in every jurisdiction. Normalized exact and containment screening cannot exclude every variant or translation already in the catalogue.
- Twenty-one individual Bengali songs, numbered 1–20 and 120, follow the Bengali Wikisource contributors’ validated page transcriptions of Rabindranath Tagore’s *Gitanjali*, third edition. The [title page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:%E0%A6%97%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%9E%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%9C%E0%A6%B2%E0%A6%BF_-_%E0%A6%B0%E0%A6%AC%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A8%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%A6%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%B0%E0%A6%A8%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A5_%E0%A6%A0%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%95%E0%A7%81%E0%A6%B0.djvu?page=1) gives Bengali year ১৩২০, spanning 1913–1914; the exact Gregorian year of this printing is not established here. These original Bengali readings are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere. Each record links to its first pinned transcription and the beginning of the scan, and its rights note lists the revision histories for **all** pages of that song. The transcriptions are adapted under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), separately from the software license. Scans are linked, not republished.
  - The [printed index](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:%E0%A6%97%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A4%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%9E%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%9C%E0%A6%B2%E0%A6%BF_-_%E0%A6%B0%E0%A6%AC%E0%A7%80%E0%A6%A8%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%A6%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%B0%E0%A6%A8%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A5_%E0%A6%A0%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%95%E0%A7%81%E0%A6%B0.djvu?page=5) and numbered poems identify these as distinct songs, not fragments. *Gitanjali* is a celebrated collection; selection from it does not establish equal individual popularity for each song. The opening line supplies each catalogue title. Verse is extracted from the marked poem blocks, page-spanning readings are rejoined, and indentation and spacing are normalized. Dates printed separately after songs are excluded; the transcription places the date after no. 16 inside its poem block, so it is explicitly removed. For no. 120, three reversed Bengali vowel-sign sequences on scan page 151 are corrected for display.
  - The selection relies on community transcriptions marked validated, not an independently claimed line-by-line collation of every scan. The title page, index, songs 1, 5, 11, 12, 16, 20 and 120, and selected neighbouring boundaries were inspected. Wikisource's separate work page for **no. 109**, not part of this selection, omits the continuation on scan page 137: a page-quality flag does not establish complete work boundaries. The [pinned revision snapshot](../scripts/sources/tagore-gitanjali-bengali-revisions.json) and `python3 scripts/import-tagore-gitanjali.py --check` reproduce the selected page ranges and readings offline; neither the snapshot nor the checks prove every transcription correct or identify all variants.
  - The English prose text alongside Bengali no. 120 is Tagore’s own free reworking, English *Gitanjali* no. 11, as printed in [Macmillan’s 1913 edition, pp. 8–9](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Gitanjali.djvu?page=38). The English text is separately public domain in the United States and was checked against that scan; it is not a literal line-by-line translation, and the two collections’ numbering is not generally aligned. No other English/Bengali pairings are assumed.
- Twenty-five German-language works introduce Goethe, Heine, Eichendorff, Schiller and a traditional song. Each reading credits and links to an individually pinned **German Wikisource** revision; the linked historical scan starts the poem. The source editions are Goethe’s *Schriften* (1789) and *Werke*, Ausgabe letzter Hand (1827); Eichendorff’s *sämmtliche Werke* (1864) and *Deutscher Dichterwald* (1813); Schiller’s *Gedichte*, second part (1808); *Schlesische Volkslieder mit Melodien* (1842); and Heine’s *Buch der Lieder* (1827). These pre-1931 German originals are public domain in the United States; copyright and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. The transcriptions are adapted from German Wikisource contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), separately from the software license; linked revision histories provide attribution. Scans are linked, not republished, and no translations are added.
  - This selection relies on the community transcriptions, marked complete by Wikisource, rather than claiming an independent line-by-line collation of every scan. Spacing, indentation, markup and typography are normalized; source spellings remain. Each chosen Heine lyric is separately numbered in the [1827 collection index](https://de.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Buch_der_Lieder&oldid=2516357); the index is not a claim that every poem in the collection is individually famous. Their titles are opening lines except the familiar editorial display titles. Publication dates and Germany as associated place do not establish dates or places of composition.
  - The complete 1808 *An die Freude* retains all eight chorus sections, with eight chorus headings counted among its 104 displayed lines; it is not the shortened Beethoven adaptation or the earlier 1786 version. Goethe’s two *Wandrers Nachtlied* poems are distinct works on the same [1827 page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:De_Goethe_Werke_LH_01_099.jpg). The 1827 *Ich grolle nicht* has eight printed lines; later versions or settings can differ. The 1842 *Die Gedanken sind frei* contains five stanzas, and Wikisource corrects the printed `Geadnken` to `Gedanken`. Eichendorff’s *Sehnsucht* is printed on pages [267](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Sehnsucht_(Eichendorff)_1.jpg)–[268](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Sehnsucht_(Eichendorff)_2.jpg), although the Wikisource page markers call them 268–269. These are examples, not an exhaustive collation or census of variants.
  - The [pinned revision snapshot](../scripts/sources/german-wikisource-revisions.json) and `python3 scripts/import-german-classics.py --check` reproduce the selection, source revisions, line counts and readings offline. These checks preserve the input and boundaries but cannot independently prove the transcription, identify every variant or translation, or establish rights in all jurisdictions. The associated scan samples for *Sehnsucht*, *Die Lorelei*, *Ich grolle nicht*, the two *Nachtlied* works and *Die Gedanken sind frei* were also inspected; the remaining scans were not independently collated line by line.
- Six complete works in five original languages follow individually cited historical printings: Robert Frost's *The Road Not Taken* in [*Mountain Interval* (1916), p. 9](https://archive.org/download/mountaininterv00frosrich/mountaininterv00frosrich.pdf#page=15); Arthur Rimbaud's *Voyelles* and *Le Dormeur du val* in Vanier's *Poésies complètes* (1895), [pp. 7](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Rimbaud_-_Po%C3%A9sies_compl%C3%A8tes,_Vanier,_1895.djvu/29) and [84](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Rimbaud_-_Po%C3%A9sies_compl%C3%A8tes,_Vanier,_1895.djvu/106); Antonio Machado's *A un olmo seco* in [*Poesías completas* (1917), pp. 182–183](https://archive.org/download/poesascompletas00machgoog/poesascompletas00machgoog.pdf#page=190); Fernando Pessoa's *O menino da sua mãe* in [*Contemporânea* (May 1926), p. 47](https://hemerotecadigital.cm-lisboa.pt/OBRAS/CONTEMPORANEA/1926/N1/N1_master/Contemporanea_N01_Mai1926.pdf#page=52); and Masaoka Shiki's persimmon hokku in [*獺祭書屋俳句帖抄* (1902), p. 57](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/pid/875216/1/46). The linked PDF pages are physical page numbers. The Machado poem is not attributed to the original 1912 *Campos de Castilla* printing.
  - These pre-1931 originals and printings are public domain in the United States; terms and moral rights vary elsewhere, so check local law. Frost died in 1963 and his poem may remain protected outside the United States. Scans are linked, not republished; no translations or modern commentary are reproduced. The [National Diet Library catalogue](https://ndlsearch.ndl.go.jp/books/R100000039-I875216) dates Shiki's book 1902, and its [IIIF manifest](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/875216/manifest.json) marks the digitized item public domain. Associated countries identify the poets, not independently established places of composition. Printed dates are not silently treated as composition dates: Machado's page places `Soria, 1912` beneath the poem, Rimbaud's *Dormeur* page prints `7 octobre 1870`, and Shiki's book groups the hokku under autumn 1895.
  - Each complete poem and its boundary were visually transcribed against the linked scans; OCR helped locate pages but is not independent proof. Spacing and typography are normalized. Pessoa's historical spellings are retained; Machado's joins and stanza breaks across the page turn are editorial. Shiki's otherwise untitled verse is shown with a modern-spelling display title for search, while its text retains `柹くへば` and uses three editorial phrase lines instead of the print's single vertical column. Its printed headnote is quoted in context, not counted as another poem. The modern spelling is not claimed to reproduce the 1902 page.
  - *Le Dormeur du val* prints `un petit aval`; Verso makes the disclosed editorial correction to `un petit val`, also found in [French Wikisource contributors' comparison transcription, revision 13688916](https://fr.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Page:Rimbaud_-_Po%C3%A9sies_compl%C3%A8tes,_Vanier,_1895.djvu/106&oldid=13688916). The reading was transcribed from the scan, not copied from Wikisource. *Voyelles* retains the print's `bombillent` and `plein de strideurs`; [a comparison of different witnesses](https://www.arthur-rimbaud.eu/p/voyelles) gives `bombinent` and `plein des strideurs`. These examples are not an exhaustive collation, and comparison sites are not substituted for the historical pages.
  - To check the source files and reviewed text hashes, save the Frost, Machado and Pessoa PDFs as `frost-1916.pdf`, `machado-1917.pdf`, and `pessoa-1926.pdf`; save the Rimbaud page images as `rimbaud-1895-p07.jpg` and `rimbaud-1895-p84.jpg` using the `Image` link on each cited Wikisource page; and save [NDL image 46](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/875216/R0000046/full/full/0/default.jpg) as `shiki-1902-p57.jpg`. Run `python3 scripts/check-popular-multilingual.py <evidence-directory>`. Hashes and line counts preserve reviewed evidence but cannot prove the transcription, completeness, rights, or absence of variants and translations elsewhere in the catalogue.
- Thirteen complete Spanish poems by Colombian poet **Agripina Montes del Valle** follow her [*Poesías*, tomo I](https://babel.banrepcultural.org/digital/api/collection/p17054coll10/id/4352/download), printed in Bogotá in 1883. The [Banco de la República catalogue](https://babel.banrepcultural.org/digital/collection/p17054coll10/id/4352) identifies the author, publisher, date and Biblioteca Nacional de Colombia source. The linked file may download rather than open in the browser; each reader supplies both the printed page and the PDF's physical page. Its title page is PDF p. 1, its index pp. 204–205, and its errata p. 206. The book's date is not a claim about a poem's composition date or place; Colombia is the poet's associated country.
  - These 1883 Spanish originals are public domain in the United States; the Colombian catalogue labels the book `Dominio público`. Copyright and moral rights differ by jurisdiction. The historical scan is linked, not republished; no translation or modern commentary is reproduced. The [readings](../scripts/sources/montes-del-valle-1883-readings.txt), individual page boundaries and index were visually checked against the scan. Historical wording is retained while spacing, indentation, ellipses, some accents and punctuation are normalized. Printed dedications are identified in the reader's context rather than counted as verse lines.
  - *A Magdalena Giraldo de A. en la tumba* prints `Se evaporó su aroma bendecida`; the book's errata directs `esencia` in place of `aroma`, and Verso follows the correction. The heading *Una azucena* appears as `A una azucena` in the index. *Apólogo* ends in a comma in this printing. A date `1865` appears beneath *Adiós*, but its meaning is not stated. These notes do not claim an exhaustive collation of other editions.
  - The scan jumps from printed p. 84 to 87 and from p. 154 to 157; works affected by missing pages, the prose, prologue and other unreviewed material are excluded. To reproduce the reviewed selection, save the linked PDF as `poesias-1883.pdf` and run `uv run --no-project python scripts/import-montes-del-valle-1883.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins the PDF, readings, page numbers and displayed line counts; hashes cannot prove transcription or completeness, and normalized duplicate screening cannot rule out every variant or translation.
- Thirteen complete Spanish poems by Uruguayan poet **Delmira Agustini** follow the Montevideo 1913 printing of [*Los cálices vacíos*](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9c/Los_c%C3%A1lices_vac%C3%ADos_-_IA_losclicesvac00agusuoft.pdf). The [title page](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9c/Los_c%C3%A1lices_vac%C3%ADos_-_IA_losclicesvac00agusuoft.pdf#page=15) names Agustini, publisher O. M. Bertani and 1913; the [printed index](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9c/Los_c%C3%A1lices_vac%C3%ADos_-_IA_losclicesvac00agusuoft.pdf#page=151) lists the selected works and continues on the next page. Each reader links to its own poem's first PDF page. The year is the printing's publication date, not a claim about composition date or place; Uruguay is the author's associated country.
  - The originals as printed in 1913 are public domain in the United States. [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Los_c%C3%A1lices_vac%C3%ADos_-_IA_losclicesvac00agusuoft.pdf) marks this Internet Archive / University of Toronto scan public domain; the images are linked, not republished. Copyright terms and moral rights vary by jurisdiction. No modern translations, prologue, photographs or commentary are reproduced. The [Uruguayan National Library](https://catalogoarchivoliterario.bibna.gub.uy/index.php/agustini-delmira) gives the poet's birth year as 1886; [autores.uy](https://autores.uy/autor/30) uses 1886 but records a reference giving 1887. Verso follows 1886 while noting the disagreement.
  - The [readings](../scripts/sources/agustini-1913-readings.txt), their boundaries and the index were visually checked against the scan; an [Internet Archive higher-resolution scan](https://archive.org/download/losclicesvac00agusuoft/losclicesvac00agusuoft_jp2.zip) helped read the obscured final lines of *Otra estirpe*. Historical spellings are retained, while spacing, ellipses, some accents and punctuation are normalized; indentation and italics are not reproduced. The stanza join across the page break in the first *Nocturno* is editorial. OCR is a locator and cross-check, not proof of readings. The book's two distinct *Nocturno* poems begin `Fuera, la noche` and `Engarzado en la noche`; they are counted separately and identified by opening line. Section headings, the prose prologue, excerpts and unreviewed works are not counted as poems.
  - The second *Nocturno* prints `la sima de la Vida`, while [Ciudad Seva](https://ciudadseva.com/texto/nocturno-agustini/) gives `la cima de la Vida`. *Tu boca* prints `labor sobre la roca` where [LeerCuentos](https://leercuentos.online/poemas/tu-boca) has `labor de roca`; *En silencio...* prints `desfloca`, while [Archiletras](https://www.archiletras.com/poemassentidos/delmira-agustini-toda-alma-toda-cuerpo/) prints `desfleca` and titles it `El silencio`. *La ruptura* prints `Esfinje` and ends with a comma; [LeerCuentos](https://leercuentos.online/poemas/la-ruptura) has `Esfinge` and a period. The poem heading is plural `Inextinguibles...`, but the 1913 index gives singular `Inextinguible`. These are examples, not an exhaustive collation; the reasons for differences are unknown. Comparison sites do not replace the 1913 witness.
  - To reproduce, save the [PDF](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9c/Los_c%C3%A1lices_vac%C3%ADos_-_IA_losclicesvac00agusuoft.pdf) as `calices-1913.pdf` and run `uv run --no-project python scripts/import-agustini-1913.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins the scan and reviewed readings, selection, page spans and displayed line counts. Hashes preserve the evidence but cannot prove collation or work boundaries. Normalized duplicate screening cannot rule out all variants or translations already in the catalogue.
- Seven complete French poems by Haitian poets **Virginie Sampeur** (three) and **Oswald Durand** (four) follow Louis Morpeau's [*Anthologie d'un siècle de poésie haïtienne*](https://manioc.univ-antilles.fr/omeka-s/files/original/3530/PAP11095.pdf). The [title page](https://manioc.univ-antilles.fr/omeka-s/files/original/3530/PAP11095.pdf#page=9) says Paris, Éditions Bossard, **1925**; the [copyright page](https://manioc.univ-antilles.fr/omeka-s/files/original/3530/PAP11095.pdf#page=10) says 1924. Verso labels the dated printing 1925, not an asserted composition or first-publication date. Each reader links to the beginning of its own text. [Île en île](https://ile-en-ile.org/sampeur/) identifies Sampeur and lists her three selected titles; [Montclair State](https://digitalcommons.montclair.edu/phareshatiens/author/durand/) identifies Durand. Haiti is the poets' associated country, not an inferred composition place.
  - The 1925 French originals are public domain in the United States. Local copyright terms and moral rights may differ; check the relevant jurisdiction. The scan is linked, not republished, and its image-reuse terms are not established here. No translations, modern commentary or photographs are added; the modern English translations linked from Montclair have not been cleared for reuse.
  - The [reading transcriptions](../scripts/sources/haiti-morpeau-1925-readings.txt) and printed boundaries were visually checked against the anthology. They preserve wording and punctuation while normalizing spacing, indentation and apostrophes, and supplying accents on initial capitals. The printed line splits in *Orfèvrerie* and *Les Papillons* are retained, so the displayed line counts include the split fragments; *Le Fils du Noir* includes both numbered headings in its count. The anthology prints dates beneath four poems; their meaning is not established and they are not used as composition dates.
  - *L’Abandonnée*'s anthology reading `n’a-t-il pu se défendre` differs from the [Montclair comparison](https://digitalcommons.montclair.edu/phareshatiens/author/sampeur/1/), `n’a-t-il su se défendre`. For *Le Fils du Noir*, Morpeau prints `mon cœur est plein de sève encor` and `nos bruns maïs`, while the [1896 scan](https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?title=File:Durand_-_Rires_et_pleurs,_po%C3%A9sies._Premi%C3%A8re_partie_-_Po%C3%A8mes,_%C3%A9l%C3%A9gies,_satires,_odelettes,_1896.djvu&page=57) has `mon cœur est jeune encor` and `nos blonds maïs` ([next page](https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?title=File:Durand_-_Rires_et_pleurs,_po%C3%A9sies._Premi%C3%A8re_partie_-_Po%C3%A8mes,_%C3%A9l%C3%A9gies,_satires,_odelettes,_1896.djvu&page=58)). These are examples, not an exhaustive list; the reasons for differences are unknown. Morpeau's two sonnets under one heading are counted as **one** work. His *Les Papillons* begins `Je regardais`; Durand's [1896 same-titled poem](https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?title=File:Durand_-_Rires_et_pleurs,_po%C3%A9sies._Premi%C3%A8re_partie_-_Po%C3%A8mes,_%C3%A9l%C3%A9gies,_satires,_odelettes,_1896.djvu&page=37) starts `Te souvient-il` and is not included. The anthology's *Si !* omits an entire stanza present in the 1896 volume and is excluded, as are other fragments and unreviewed items. Comparison websites are not the source transcriptions, and their relationship to Morpeau's edition is not established.
  - To reproduce, save the [Manioc PDF](https://manioc.univ-antilles.fr/omeka-s/files/original/3530/PAP11095.pdf) as `morpeau-1925.pdf` and run `uv run --no-project python scripts/import-haiti-morpeau-1925.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins the PDF and reviewed readings, selection, page spans and displayed line counts. Hashes preserve the evidence but do not prove that collation or work boundaries are correct; normalized duplicate screening cannot rule out all variants or translations in the catalogue.
- Twenty-eight complete poems by **Alfonsina Storni** follow the Buenos Aires 1918 printing of [*El dulce daño*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:El_Dulce_Da%C3%B1o_-_Alfonsina_Storni.pdf). The [title page](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b4/El_Dulce_Da%C3%B1o_-_Alfonsina_Storni.pdf#page=1) identifies author, publisher and year; the [printed index](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b4/El_Dulce_Da%C3%B1o_-_Alfonsina_Storni.pdf#page=146) lists individual works. Each reader links to the start of its complete poem in the Circe library scan, supplied to Commons through a Buenos Aires library agreement. The four-page *Tú me quieres blanca* is **one** poem; book sections and other unreviewed entries are not counted as additional works.
  - The 1918 originals are public domain in the United States. Commons marks its scan public domain; the images are linked, not republished. Storni died in 1938, but local copyright terms and moral rights vary; check the relevant jurisdiction. These are Spanish originals, not translations. Argentina identifies the Swiss-born poet's literary association, not her birthplace or a claimed place of composition. The year is publication, not a claim about when a poem was written.
  - The [reading transcriptions](../scripts/sources/storni-1918-readings.txt) and complete poem boundaries were visually compared with the 1918 pages and index. Historical wording, spelling and punctuation are retained while title case and accents, spacing, indentation and ellipses are normalized; joins between stanzas across page breaks may be editorial. Obvious scan specks are not treated as punctuation. OCR helped locate and compare lines, but did not establish readings; its `habernos`, `qne` and `do menos`, for example, were checked against the images and read as `habemos`, `que` and `lo menos`.
  - The printing of *Sábado* begins `Levanté temprano` and has `Comedor en sombras`, while [Ciudad Seva](https://ciudadseva.com/texto/sabado/) has `Me levanté temprano` and `Comedor en sombra`. In *Tú me quieres blanca*, the print begins `Tu me quieres alba` and has `y lévate al alba`, while [Ciudad Seva](https://ciudadseva.com/texto/tu-me-quieres-blanca/) gives `Tú me quieres alba` and `y límpiate al alba`. In *¿Qué diría?* the print has `extra fantasía` and `cubriendo las aceras`; the [2017 Biblioteca del Congreso anthology](https://bcn.gob.ar/uploads/PoemasAlfonsinaStorni.pdf#page=18) has `ultra fantasía` and `cubriendo en las aceras`. The print readings are retained and disclosed in the readers; the cause of these differences is not established. These examples are not an exhaustive census of variants, and the comparison texts do not replace the 1918 witness. The Wikisource book transcription is marked incomplete and was not used as the primary source.
  - To reproduce, save the [Circe PDF](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b4/El_Dulce_Da%C3%B1o_-_Alfonsina_Storni.pdf) as `circe-1918.pdf`, then run `uv run --no-project python scripts/import-storni-1918.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins that PDF and the reviewed readings, selection, page spans and line counts. Hashes preserve the evidence but cannot prove visual collation correct. Normalized exact, containment and shared-line duplicate screens cannot exclude all versions or translations already in the catalogue.
- Twelve complete poems by Nicaraguan modernist **Rubén Darío** follow the 1896 Buenos Aires first edition of [*Prosas profanas y otros poemas*](https://archive.org/details/prosasprofanasyo00daro), including *Sonatina*. The [title page](https://archive.org/download/prosasprofanasyo00daro/prosasprofanasyo00daro.pdf#page=9) names Darío and Pablo E. Coni é Hijos, 1896; the [printed index](https://archive.org/download/prosasprofanasyo00daro/prosasprofanasyo00daro.pdf#page=197) lists the individual titles. Each reader links to the first PDF page of its complete poem. *Sonatina*'s eight stanzas form **one** poem, not eight works; the other book entries and internal sections are not included in this batch.
  - The originals and the pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The linked Toronto scan is not republished, and its image-reuse terms are not established here. These are Spanish originals, not translations. Nicaragua identifies the poet's country; the 1896 publication year and Buenos Aires printing are not claims about the date or place of composition.
  - Each selected text and its boundaries were visually checked against the 1896 pages. The [readings](../scripts/sources/dario-1896-readings.txt) preserve historical spellings and punctuation while normalizing spacing, quotation marks and indentation; italics are not reproduced. Printed dedications to the countess of Peralta, to Reynaldo de Rafael, and `In memoriam` are described in context rather than included as verse. OCR helps locate and compare words but is not independent proof of a reading.
  - For example, the printing has `pavos-reales` in *Sonatina*; the linked [Spanish Wikisource comparison](https://es.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Sonatina&oldid=1321030) has `pavos reales` and labels a 1917 edition in its header. *Del campo* prints `ginete`, versus `jinete` in [Ciudad Seva](https://ciudadseva.com/texto/del-campo/); *Para una cubana* prints `finje`, versus `finge` at [Poesi.as](https://www.poesi.as/rd06pp009.htm). The print titles the last selected poem `Ite misa est` and has `convalesciente`; [Ciudad Seva](https://ciudadseva.com/texto/ite-missa-est/) gives `Ite, missa est` and `convaleciente`. To aid discovery, the modern title is displayed alongside the original and both can be searched. The 1896 text is retained; these are examples, not an exhaustive census of variants. The comparison texts are not substituted for the 1896 readings.
  - To reproduce, save the [1896 PDF](https://archive.org/download/prosasprofanasyo00daro/prosasprofanasyo00daro.pdf) as `prosas-1896.pdf` and its [OCR text](https://archive.org/download/prosasprofanasyo00daro/prosasprofanasyo00daro_djvu.txt) as `prosas-1896-ocr.txt`, then run `python3 scripts/import-dario-1896.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins both files and the reviewed readings, selection, page spans and line counts; hashes do not prove visual collation correct. Normalized exact, containment and shared-line screening cannot exclude every variant or translation already in the catalogue.
- Forty-five complete, individually numbered poems by **José Martí** add *Versos sencillos* I–XXXVIII and XL–XLVI. The [1891 title page](https://www.poesi.as/JoseMartiVersosSencillos-09foto.htm) identifies the New York printing by Louis Weiss & Co.; its [two-page index](https://www.poesi.as/JoseMartiVersosSencillos-83foto.htm) identifies the 46 numbered pieces. Each new reader links to the first facsimile image of its complete poem, and adjacent images can be reached through that page's navigation. The hosting site describes this as a Digital Library of the Caribbean facsimile of an inscribed copy; the relationship of this digitization to other copies is not independently established. The images are linked, not republished, and their image-reuse terms are not assumed. The year is publication, not a claim about the exact date or place of composition.
  - The historical poems and 1891 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The reading transcriptions adapt **Spanish Wikisource contributors'** individually linked revisions and are distributed under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), separately from the software license. They were altered after comparison with the 1891 printing; the associated revision links provide attribution histories. The [pinned Wikisource snapshot](../scripts/sources/marti-1891-wikisource.json) contains the credited comparison text, including XXXIX, but only the 45 other works are newly published here. No scan images or translations are added.
  - Each complete text, number and boundary was visually compared with the printed pages; OCR aided comparison but is not proof. Accents and punctuation are modernized or normalized, some historical spellings remain, and indentation is not reproduced: these are reading editions, not diplomatic transcriptions. The numbering and opening phrases in the display titles are editorial. For example, I reads `alcaide` rather than Wikisource's `alcalde`; XXVII has `la niña` rather than `la luna`; XXX has `con su vida` rather than `con su sangre`; and XXXV has `alado` rather than `al lado`. The printing of XLVI has `charme`, possibly a printing error, where the comparison has `echarme`; it is retained and disclosed. These are examples, not a complete list of differences.
  - The existing `rosa-blanca-marti` is XXXIX and remains unchanged, not a new work. Its modern `ortiga` differs from `oruga` in the [1891 printing](https://www.poesi.as/JoseMartiVersosSencillos-72foto.htm). The 1891 index says p. 18 for IV, although the poem actually starts on p. 19. Neither discrepancy is silently used to create an extra record.
  - To reproduce, save the facsimile images numbered 9 and 17–84 as `page-09.jpg`, `page-17.jpg`, …, `page-84.jpg`. For example, [image 17](https://www.poesi.as/cuadros/JMAVS00001_00017.jpg) is the first page of I; substitute the five-digit image number in that URL. Run `python3 scripts/import-marti-1891.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The [reviewed manifest](../scripts/sources/marti-1891-reviewed.json) pins the images and the committed Wikisource snapshot; the importer checks selection, image spans, comparison revisions and complete line counts. Hashes and OCR comparisons preserve evidence but cannot independently prove that editorial collation was correct. Normalized exact and containment screening cannot rule out every textual variant or translation already present elsewhere.
- A further 35 complete *waka*, traditionally **Ogura Hyakunin Isshu** nos. 66–100, follow the same [1900 Keichū printing](https://archive.org/details/hyakuninkeichu) described below. Each reader links to its poem's PDF page. [Japanese Wikisource revision 240235](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E5%B0%8F%E5%80%89%E7%99%BE%E4%BA%BA%E4%B8%80%E9%A6%96&oldid=240235) is a numbered attribution and modern-text comparison, not the historical source edition. These historical originals and the pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translations are included. Five phrase breaks, some character-form normalization, expanded repetition marks and supplied voicing are editorial. The titles are opening phrases, not historical titles; exact composition dates and places are not inferred. Anthology attributions are not independent proof of authorship.
  - In no. 74 the 1900 printing includes `よ` after `山おろし`, absent from the comparison; no. 77 ends `あはんこそ思ふ` rather than `逢はむとぞ思ふ`; and no. 85 reads `明やらぬ` rather than `明けやらで`. These are disclosed in the affected readers and are not an exhaustive list of variants. No religious denomination or devotional theme is inferred from a poet's identity.
  - Reproduce the records from the same pinned evidence files using `python3 scripts/import-ogura-1900-final.py <evidence-directory> --check`; see the [reviewed manifest](../scripts/sources/ogura-1900-final-reviewed.json). Hash, numbering, page, boundary and comparison-attribution checks do not independently establish the readings. Normalized exact and containment screening cannot rule out every textual variant or translation of an existing work.
- A further 25 complete *waka*, traditionally **Ogura Hyakunin Isshu** nos. 41–65, follow the same [1900 Keichū printing](https://archive.org/details/hyakuninkeichu) described below. Each text links to its own scan page; [Japanese Wikisource revision 240235](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E5%B0%8F%E5%80%89%E7%99%BE%E4%BA%BA%E4%B8%80%E9%A6%96&oldid=240235) is a numbered attribution and modern-text comparison, not the source edition. The historical texts and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translations are included. Five phrase breaks, character-form normalization, expanded repetition marks and supplied voicing are editorial. The titles are opening phrases, not historical titles; exact dates and places of composition are not inferred.
  - The 1900 text is not silently replaced with the familiar modern card text: no. 43 reads `昔は物も`, versus `昔はものを`; no. 47 `さびしさに`, versus `さびしきに`; no. 49 `よるはもえて`, versus `夜はもえ`; no. 56 `あふよしもがな`, versus `逢ふこともがな`; and Murasaki Shikibu's no. 57 ends `よはの月影`, versus `夜半の月かな`. The affected readers describe these differences; this list is not exhaustive. Names and court titles follow the anthology attribution, not independent proof of authorship.
  - Reproduce these records from the same pinned evidence files using `python3 scripts/import-ogura-1900-sequel.py <evidence-directory> --check`; see the [reviewed manifest](../scripts/sources/ogura-1900-sequel-reviewed.json). The importer verifies evidence hashes, numbers, pages, five-line boundaries and linked comparison attributions; these checks do not independently establish the readings. The 25 complete texts were screened against the existing catalogue and each other for normalized exact and containment matches, which cannot rule out all variant or translated duplicates.
- Forty complete *waka*, traditionally nos. 1–40 in the widely known **Ogura Hyakunin Isshu**, follow the Japanese text printed in [Keichū's *Hyakunin Isshu Kaikanshō*](https://archive.org/details/hyakuninkeichu). Its [title page](https://archive.org/download/hyakuninkeichu/hyakuninkeichu_text.pdf#page=8) credits Keichū and a preface by Kimura Masakoto; the [colophon](https://archive.org/download/hyakuninkeichu/hyakuninkeichu_text.pdf#page=176) gives Meiji 33 (1900) and names Miyoshi Nakao as editor/publisher. Each reader links to the PDF page containing its poem. The historical poems and this pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; the Archive describes the scan with a public-domain mark. Check local law. Images and commentary are not republished, and no translations have been added.
  - The original print uses one vertical column per poem. Verso supplies five phrase breaks, expands repetition marks, normalizes some character forms and clarifies voicing where needed; the result is a reading transcription, not a diplomatic facsimile. The first phrase supplies each otherwise untitled poem's display title. Each complete verse was checked visually against its cited scan page, not accepted from OCR; the scan prints attribution headings in some cases on the preceding page.
  - The numbered ordering and attributions were compared with [Japanese Wikisource revision 240235](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E5%B0%8F%E5%80%89%E7%99%BE%E4%BA%BA%E4%B8%80%E9%A6%96&oldid=240235), not used as the primary text. Its [discussion](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E3%83%88%E3%83%BC%E3%82%AF:%E5%B0%8F%E5%80%89%E7%99%BE%E4%BA%BA%E4%B8%80%E9%A6%96&oldid=144306) describes a modern competitive-card text and adjustments from another card edition; it is **not** claimed to reproduce the 1900 scan or an original medieval manuscript. Early poems and poet attributions may vary across anthologies and manuscripts; an anthology attribution is not proof of authorship. Keichū notes that the Kokin Wakashū treats no. 5 as anonymous and questions the traditional attribution of no. 27. His no. 6 commentary mentions other poems with related imagery, not necessarily alternate attributions of this poem. The associated country is Japan, not an asserted place of composition; exact dates are not inferred.
  - Material differences are retained and disclosed in the affected readers: no. 13's 1900 `なりける` versus `なりぬる`, no. 20's `あはんこそ思ふ` versus `逢はむとぞ思ふ`, and no. 33's `花の散らん` versus `花の散るらむ` in the modern comparison. No. 25 supplies voicing in `しられで` for the print's `しられて`, as Keichū's commentary explains the negative sense; nos. 27 and 36 similarly supply voicing in `いづみ川` and `いづこ`. These examples are not a complete catalogue of variants. Traditional spellings, including printed `ん`, and additional kanji/kana choices also differ.
  - To reproduce, save the [primary PDF](https://archive.org/download/hyakuninkeichu/hyakuninkeichu_text.pdf) as `keichu-1900-text.pdf` and the [Wikisource API response for revision 240235](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/api.php?action=parse&oldid=240235&prop=wikitext&format=json) as `wikisource-ja-revision-240235.json`, then run `python3 scripts/import-ogura-1900.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The [reviewed manifest](../scripts/sources/ogura-1900-reviewed.json) and importer pin both inputs, ordering, pages, complete line counts and the comparison's author links. These checks preserve review evidence; they do not independently prove that collation or attribution is correct. The 40 complete poems were screened against the prior catalogue and one another for normalized exact and containment matches; this does not rule out all variant or translated duplicates.
- Seven complete English versions by **Shigeyoshi Obata** are attached to seven existing Li Bai works. They follow [*The Works of Li-Po*, London and Toronto, 1923](https://archive.org/details/worksoflipochine00libauoft), whose [title page](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=7) names Obata. The earlier [New York 1922 printing](https://archive.org/details/workslipochines00conggoog) has its own [title](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=10) and [copyright](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=11) pages. The pre-1931 English texts are public domain in the United States. [Obata died in 1971](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Author:Shigeyoshi_Obata); they may remain protected in jurisdictions using life plus 70 years, so check local law. The Chinese working edition is not established here; these are historical interpretations, not certified literal matches to the displayed originals. The scans are linked, not republished.
  - The three already-catalogued `清平調` lyrics (`tang-316`–`tang-318`) correspond in order to Obata's numbered nos. 6–8, *Lady Yang Kuei-fei at the Imperial Feast of the Peony—I–III*, printed pp. 31–33 ([1923 PDF pp. 57–59](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=57); [1922 PDF pp. 58–60](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=58)). Obata's heading identifies Lady Yang and an imperial feast; the displayed poems do not name either, and this historical occasion is not independently established here. His first rendering rearranges the wind-and-dew images and adds an earthly-garden encounter; the second elaborates the cloud-and-rain allusion with a dreamer king and fairy; the third rearranges the lines and interprets longing as love. The three numbered renderings are translations of existing records, not additional original works.
  - `玉階怨` (`tang-255`), no. 18, *The Sorrow of the Jewel Staircase*, printed p. 46 ([1923 PDF p. 72](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=72); [1922 PDF p. 73](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=73)): “silken shoes” interprets `羅襪`, usually understood as silk stockings or socks.
  - `下江陵` (`tang-269`), no. 47, *The River Journey from White King City*, printed p. 76 ([1923 PDF p. 102](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=102); [1922 PDF p. 103](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=103)): the 1923 margin names the work `早發白帝城`; the shared sequence of dawn, White King City, Chiang-ling, cries and the boat identifies the same poem. “Monkeys” renders `猿`, often understood as apes or gibbons, and “scarcely ceased” differs from displayed `啼不住` (calls do not stop). The final letter of “stream” is obscured in the 1923 scan and its OCR reads `streanf`; the 1922 scan clearly reads `stream`, which is retained here.
  - `渡荊門送別` (`tang-101`), no. 51, *Parting at Ching-men*, printed p. 80 ([1923 PDF p. 106](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=106); [1922 PDF p. 107](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=107)): the explicit “you” and the speaker's farewell interpret the parting; the displayed Chinese verse does not identify a separate addressee.
  - `贈孟浩然` (`tang-100`), no. 58, *To Meng Hao-jan*, printed p. 88 ([1923 PDF p. 114](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=114); [1922 PDF p. 115](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf#page=115)): “love of beauty” interprets `風流`, and the explicit reference to the soul elaborates the closing image.
  - Both dated scans were visually checked for complete English texts and boundaries. They are separate print witnesses with very similar page layouts; they are not independent translations and their typesetting relationship is not established. Punctuation follows the 1923 reading, spaces are normalized, and indented print wraps are joined into reading lines; prose notes and marginal Chinese titles are not part of the translations. OCR locates and compares normalized words, with explicit corrections for marginal noise; it does not prove punctuation or replace visual review.
  - To reproduce, save the [1923 PDF](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf) as `obata-li-po-1923-london.pdf` and [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft_djvu.xml) as `obata-li-po-1923-ocr.xml`, plus the [1922 PDF](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog.pdf) as `obata-li-po-1922-new-york.pdf` and [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/workslipochines00conggoog/workslipochines00conggoog_djvu.xml) as `obata-li-po-1922-new-york-ocr.xml`. Run `python3 scripts/import-obata-li-bai.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins all four files, checks each primary OCR entry and reading hash, and checks the 1922 `stream` comparison; hashes cannot prove that the visual review or interpretation is correct.
- Two historical English versions are attached to Li Bai's existing `靜夜思` (`quiet-night-thought-li-bai`); they are translations, not new original works, and do not replace or independently review Verso's seven labeled AI-assisted reading aids. Both pre-1931 English publications are public domain in the United States; check local law. The Chinese working editions are not established, and the literary renderings are interpretations rather than certified literal matches to the Chinese text displayed here. The scans are linked, not republished.
  - L. Cranmer-Byng's *Thoughts in a Tranquil Night* follows [*A Lute of Jade*, London, 1909](https://archive.org/details/lutejade00cran), printed pp. 61–62 ([PDF pp. 65–66](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran.pdf#page=65)). His dream imagery and address to the fatherland elaborate the displayed Chinese. The book's appendix acknowledges drawing on earlier English and French translations, but does not identify the immediate source of this individual version. The separately transcribed [Gutenberg eBook 390](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/390) describes a New York 1909 printing; its normalized words agree, but it is a different edition witness and is not silently substituted for the London reading.
  - Shigeyoshi Obata's *On a Quiet Night* follows no. 27 in [*The Works of Li-Po*, London and Toronto, 1923](https://archive.org/details/worksoflipochine00libauoft), printed p. 55 ([PDF p. 81](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=81); [title page, PDF p. 7](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf#page=7)). His “mountain moon” differs from the displayed `明月` (bright moon) and is consistent with the `山月` variant attested by the [Hong Kong Education Bureau](https://www.edb.gov.hk/attachment/tc/curriculum-development/kla/chi-edu/resources/primary/lang/pdp/20250308_PDF.pdf). That comparison does not establish which Chinese witness Obata used. The Archive identifier includes `uoft`; despite a locally used 1922 filename during research, the title page of this scanned copy is explicitly 1923, and the credit follows the scan.
  - Both complete texts and boundaries were visually checked against their dated scans. Line breaks and punctuation follow the scanned readings with normalized spacing; spaces approximate the centered 1909 layout and do not reproduce its typography exactly. OCR is used only as an aid for boundary and normalized-word comparison, not independent proof of transcription or punctuation. The importer pins the scans, scan-derived OCR, separate Gutenberg comparison and manually reviewed texts; hashes cannot prove that visual review was correct.
  - To reproduce, save the [1909 PDF](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran.pdf) as `lutejade-1909-london.pdf`, its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran_djvu.xml) as `lutejade-1909-london-ocr.xml`, the [Gutenberg text](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/390/pg390.txt) as `gutenberg-390.txt`, the [1923 PDF](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft.pdf) as `obata-li-po-1923-london.pdf`, and its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/worksoflipochine00libauoft/worksoflipochine00libauoft_djvu.xml) as `obata-li-po-1923-ocr.xml` in one directory. Run `python3 scripts/import-quiet-night-translations.py <evidence-directory> --check`.
- Five English versions by **Basil Hall Chamberlain**, from [“Bashō and the Japanese Poetical Epigram,” *Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan*, vol. XXX (1902)](https://archive.org/details/in.gov.ignca.26037), are attached to five existing Japanese works; translations are not additional originals. The volume's [title page](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=4) prints 1902, and the [article's opening](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=270) credits Chamberlain and says it was read on 4 June 1902. The 1902 English versions are public domain in the United States; check local law. Chamberlain's Japanese working editions are not established here; the renderings are historical interpretations, not certified word-for-word matches to the displayed originals. No scan image is republished.
  - Bashō's `夏草や兵どもが夢の跡` (`summer-grass-basho`), no. 46, printed p. 305 ([volume PDF p. 332](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=332), [second copy PDF p. 34](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf#page=34)): Chamberlain's romanization prints `no` where the displayed Japanese reads `が` (`ga`); the cited Japanese edition itself notes a particle variant. “Haply” and “relic” are interpretive. The English title here is editorial.
  - Bashō's `年暮れぬ笠着て草鞋穿きながら` (`basho-nozarashi-28`), no. 74, printed p. 319 ([volume PDF p. 346](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=346), [second copy PDF p. 41](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf#page=41)): “pilgrim's hat” interprets `笠` (hat); the English title here is editorial and does not imply a religious affiliation.
  - Buson's `春雨やものかたりゆく簑と傘` (`buson-1926-12`), no. 179, printed p. 351 ([volume PDF p. 378](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=378), [second copy PDF p. 57](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf#page=57)): Chamberlain's romanization reads `monogatari`; the displayed 1926 edition has `ものかたり` (`monokatari`). His “rain-coat” renders `簑`, a straw rain cape. The English title here is editorial.
  - Buson's `遲き日のつもりて遠きむかしかな` (`buson-1926-13`), no. 176, printed p. 350 ([volume PDF p. 377](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=377), [second copy PDF p. 56](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf#page=56)): “Memories of the Past” is Chamberlain's printed English heading; the displayed Japanese text has no heading.
  - Chiyo-ni's `百生や蔓一すじの心より` (`chiyo-ni-1927-46`), no. 170, printed p. 348 ([volume PDF p. 375](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf#page=375), [second copy PDF p. 55](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf#page=55)): Chamberlain explicitly calls his two lines an approximation and notes an alternative gourd reading. He proposes a Buddhist interpretation; this is attributed to him rather than established as the poet's intent or used to infer a religious label. The 1927 anthology prints the headnote `三界唯心` above the original. The English title here is editorial.
  - Both separately digitized copies corroborate the five English texts and boundaries, but they are copies of the **same edition**, not independent editions.
