The article's numerals identify complete individual hokku; prose commentary is not included in the translations. Each English version retains the two printed lines, with punctuation normalized to ordinary spacing. OCR serves only to locate the readings and check boundaries; each line was visually transcribed from the scans because OCR is unreliable, and the reproducible importer explicitly stores those corrections. The [Haiku Foundation catalogue](https://thehaikufoundation.org/omeka/items/show/6080) identifies the article as public domain; its direct PDF was inaccessible during review, so the separately hosted public copy linked above was used without bypassing access controls.
  - To reproduce, save the [1902 volume PDF](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037.pdf) as `chamberlain-1902-transaction-vol30.pdf`, its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/in.gov.ignca.26037/26037_djvu.xml) as `chamberlain-1902-transaction-vol30-ocr.xml`, and the [other digitized copy](https://archive.org/download/basho-and-the-japanses-poetical-epigram/87b3b0e22e3c341d29c3f58aa3666d5c.pdf) as `chamberlain-1902-haiku-public-copy.pdf` in one directory. Run `python3 scripts/import-chamberlain-translations.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins all three files and checks numbered boundaries, OCR line positions and the hashes of the manually reviewed readings; it does not claim the OCR independently proves the transcription.
- Seven English versions from **Florence Ayscough and Amy Lowell**, [*Fir-Flower Tablets*, Boston and New York, 1921](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/48222), are attached to seven existing Chinese works; they are translations, not additional originals. The [title page](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=13) credits Ayscough's translations from Chinese and Lowell's English versions; the [copyright page](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=14) names both in 1921. These historical English texts are public domain in the United States; check local law. Their Chinese working editions are not established here, and these interpretive versions are not asserted to be literal matches for the displayed Chinese. No scan image is republished.
  - Wang Wei's `青谿` (`tang-015`), [*The Blue-Green Stream*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#THE_BLUE-GREEN_STREAM), printed p. 123 ([scan PDF p. 229](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=229)): the phrase “clean and white as silk” elaborates the displayed description of the heart.
  - Wang Wei's `渭川田家` (`tang-016`), [*Farm House on the Wei Stream*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#FARM_HOUSE), printed p. 124 ([scan PDF p. 230](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=230)): “upon the heights” is not explicit in the displayed Chinese, and “Dwindled and Shrunken” renders the song title `式微`.
  - Qiu Wei's `尋西山隱者不遇` (`tang-022`), [*Seeking for the Hermit of the West Hill; Not Meeting Him*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#SEEKING_FOR_THE_HERMIT_OF_THE_WEST), printed p. 125 ([scan PDF p. 231](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=231)): “Nothing-Beyond Peak” and “red grass” are not explicit in the displayed first line; “Man of Wisdom” interprets the absent person.
  - Qiwu Qian's `春泛若耶溪` (`tang-023`), [*Floating on the Pool of Jo Ya. Spring*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#FLOATING_ON_THE_POOL_OF_JO_YA_SPRING), printed p. 126 ([scan PDF p. 232](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=232)): the version says “The serrated hills face the Southern Constellation,” whereas the displayed `隔山望南斗` describes a view of the Southern Dipper across a mountain. These are material editorial differences, not proof of the Chinese source edition used.
  - Li Bai's `送友人` (`tang-102`), [*Saying Good-Bye to a Friend*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#SAYING_GOOD-BYE_TO_A_FRIEND), printed p. 50 ([Michigan PDF p. 156](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=156), [Boston PDF p. 154](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablets00aysc_0/firflowertablets00aysc_0.pdf#page=154)): the version's “water-plants” renders `孤蓬`, often understood as a lone drifting plant; the right angle and repeated horse-call are interpretive.
  - Li Bai's `登金陵鳳凰臺` (`tang-175`), [*Fêng Huang T’ai*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#FENG_HUANG_TAI), printed p. 21 ([Michigan PDF p. 127](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=127), [Boston PDF p. 125](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablets00aysc_0/firflowertablets00aysc_0.pdf#page=125)): “silver-crested love-pheasants” is this version's rendering of the mythical `鳳凰`, not an asserted zoological identification; the book's own note 35 describes the birds as mythical. “Green Heaven” differs from displayed `青山外` (beyond the green mountains). The translation's heading and subtitle are combined in its displayed title; the two printed stanzas and the final deliberate indent are retained, with two spaces approximating the indent.
  - Li Bai's `送孟浩然之廣陵` (`tang-268`), [*At the Yellow Crane Tower, Taking Leave of Mêng Hao Jan on His Departure to Kuang Ling*](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html#YELLOW_CRANE_TOWER), printed p. 93 ([Michigan PDF p. 199](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf#page=199), [Boston PDF p. 197](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablets00aysc_0/firflowertablets00aysc_0.pdf#page=197)): “flower-smelling mist” and the “jade-coloured distance” are interpretive renderings.
  - The identifications use complete sequences, not titles alone: `tang-015` moves from the Yellow Flower River and winding stream through pines and water-plants to sitting and fishing; `tang-016` moves from the village, herds and wheat through farmers' conversation to the closing song; `tang-022` follows the visitor from the mountain hut, absent host and fishing conjecture through rain, pines and the descent; `tang-023` follows the evening boat and flower path through the western valley, constellation, mist and moon to the wish to fish. `tang-102` follows the parting by hills and water through the wandering clouds, sunset and horse-call; `tang-175` moves from the deserted bird terrace through the Wu and Jin remains to the hills, island, clouds and Chang'an; `tang-268` moves from the Yellow Crane Tower and third-month journey to a distant sail and the flowing river. These correspondences do not certify every translated phrase.
  - The complete texts and boundaries were visually checked against the linked 1921 printing. Text follows the [Gutenberg transcription](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/48222), which credits David Edwards, Christopher Wright, and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team; its credited scans came from Google Print. The Michigan Google scan and Gutenberg are therefore **not assumed to be independent witnesses**. A separately digitized [Boston Public Library copy](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablets00aysc_0/firflowertablets00aysc_0.pdf#page=227) of the same 1921 edition also agrees in the words and boundaries of all seven poems; it is not a separate edition. The edition's indented print wraps are joined into reading lines; the Gutenberg `_li_` markers representing print italics are removed while retaining the word, punctuation is retained, and interword spacing is normalized. The deliberate final indent in `tang-175` is approximated with two spaces. OCR is a comparison aid, not a substitute for visual collation. Paul Pelliot's [1922 review](https://altaica.ru/LIBRARY/Pelliot/Pelliot_Rev.%20on%20F.%20Ayscough,%20Fir-Flower%20Tablets%201922.pdf) criticizes unspecified Chinese editions and several interpretations elsewhere in the book; its specific examples do not establish errors in these seven poems.
  - To reproduce, save the [Gutenberg HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/48222/pg48222-images.html) as `gutenberg-48222.html`, the [Michigan PDF](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog.pdf) as `firflower-1921-michigan.pdf`, and its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/firflowertablet02lowegoog/firflowertablet02lowegoog_djvu.xml) as `firflower-1921-michigan-ocr.xml` in one directory; run `python3 scripts/import-fir-flower-translations.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer pins all three files, headings, line counts and reviewed text hashes, and checks the scan-derived OCR after explicit corrections. It normalizes punctuation and diacritics **only for the OCR comparison**, not the published text; it does not assert that OCR itself proves punctuation or edition independence.
- Four historical translations are attached to **three existing Chinese works**, not counted as additional originals. Each was published before 1931 and is public domain in the United States; check local law. The translators' Chinese working editions are not established here and the renderings are not claimed to match the particular Chinese edition displayed by Verso word for word. The scans are linked, not republished.
  - Two French translations by **Léon d’Hervey de Saint-Denys** are from [*Poésies de l’époque des Thang*, Paris, 1862](https://archive.org/details/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC), title page [PDF p. 4](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC.pdf#page=4). The complete *Le poète attend son ami Ting-Kong dans une grotte du mont Nié-Chy* ([PDF p. 325](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC.pdf#page=325)) corresponds to Meng Haoran's `宿業師山房待丁大不至`; its particular cave and mountain, and the precise relationship of Ting-Kong to `丁大`, are not established by the displayed title. A short mark between `soleil` and `a` in the scan is read as a hyphen by the OCR; our reading normalizes it to a space. *Visite à un ami dans sa maison de campagne* ([PDF p. 323](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC.pdf#page=323)) corresponds to Meng's `過故人莊`. His `riz` means rice, whereas the displayed `黍` is millet. His [following notes](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC.pdf#page=324) explain `重陽` as the ninth day of the ninth month and identify the friend as Wang Wei; the latter identification is not independently established here. Footnote markers and notes are not part of the translated verse.
  - **L. Cranmer-Byng**, *A Friend Expected*, follows the London 1909 [*A Lute of Jade*](https://archive.org/details/lutejade00cran), printed pp. 44–45 ([PDF pp. 48–49](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran.pdf#page=48)); the same original `宿業師山房待丁大不至` also has the separately credited French version above. His appendix says the book drew largely on earlier English and French translations, especially those of Herbert Giles and Hervey de Saint-Denys. His poem resembles Hervey's version, but the book does not explicitly identify the source for this individual rendering. His `lure him on` is interpretive; the displayed Chinese waits with a zither. The separately transcribed [Gutenberg eBook 390](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/390) describes a New York 1909 witness; it agrees in normalized words, but prints a final period where the London scan has none. A small mark after `peaks` in the London scan is ambiguous: its OCR reads a period, but our reading omits it, as does Gutenberg. We do not conflate the two print witnesses or silently mix them.
  - **Herbert A. Giles**, *The Grass-Widow’s Song*, follows [*Gems of Chinese Literature*, London and Shanghai, 1884](https://archive.org/details/gemsofchineselit00gilerich), printed p. 113 ([PDF p. 131](https://archive.org/download/gemsofchineselit00gilerich/gemsofchineselit00gilerich.pdf#page=131)). The poem corresponds to Li Bai's `子夜吳歌（秋歌）`. Giles's `crescent`, `Kansuh` and `Tartars` are not explicit in the displayed Chinese; the historical ethnonym is retained as printed, not endorsed. Cranmer-Byng's separate *Under the Moon* shares distinctive details with Giles's earlier version, but is not added to this reader and its exact source is not established.
  - The complete texts and boundaries were visually checked against these dated scans. The [reading file](../scripts/sources/early-tang-translation-readings.txt) preserves reading lines and stanzas while joining print-wrapped lines, omitting footnote markers and indentation, and normalizing typography and spacing; it is not diplomatic. The [importer](../scripts/import-early-tang-translations.py) pins the scans, their derived OCR, the Gutenberg comparison and reviewed readings; it checks selection order, stanza counts, hashes, source boundaries and exact documented OCR corrections. OCR is a comparison aid, not independent proof of collation. Save the following files in one directory and run `python3 scripts/import-early-tang-translations.py <evidence-directory> --check`:
    - [1862 PDF](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC.pdf) as `hervey-thang-1862.pdf` and its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC/bub_gb_4vQSL2JfTZMC_djvu.xml) as `hervey-thang-1862-ocr.xml`.
    - [1884 PDF](https://archive.org/download/gemsofchineselit00gilerich/gemsofchineselit00gilerich.pdf) as `giles-gems-1884.pdf` and its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/gemsofchineselit00gilerich/gemsofchineselit00gilerich_djvu.xml) as `giles-gems-1884-ocr.xml`.
    - [1909 London PDF](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran.pdf) as `lutejade-1909-london.pdf` and its [OCR XML](https://archive.org/download/lutejade00cran/lutejade00cran_djvu.xml) as `lutejade-1909-london-ocr.xml`; save the [Gutenberg text](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/390/pg390.txt) as `gutenberg-390.txt`.
- Three English translations by **Arthur Waley** are attached to existing Chinese works, not counted as new original poems. The translations are independently identified and are public domain in the United States as pre-1931 publications; check local law. Waley's working Chinese editions have not been identified here, and these literary renderings are not asserted to be literal translations of the particular editions displayed in Verso. No scan image is republished.
  - His translations of Tao Yuanming's `飲酒` 其九 and 其五 are numbered (2) and (7) in [*A Hundred and Seventy Chinese Poems*, London, 1918, Gutenberg eBook 42290](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/42290). Gutenberg credits Henry Flower and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team. The separately linked [Cornell copy](https://archive.org/details/cu31924023430097) identifies the same 1918 London printing; the two complete translations and their next numbered boundaries were visually checked on printed pp. 71–72 and 76–77 (PDF pp. [91–92](https://archive.org/download/cu31924023430097/cu31924023430097.pdf#page=91) and [96–97](https://archive.org/download/cu31924023430097/cu31924023430097.pdf#page=96)). The relationship between this physical copy and Gutenberg's source images is not established. Gutenberg's page-72 marker appears one line earlier than the printed page turn, without changing the verse. Waley supplies numbers rather than individual English titles; our titles are the opening lines. In 其五, his `summer hills` differs from the displayed Ming reading `南山` (southern mountain); neither text has been silently altered.
  - Waley's [*More Translations from the Chinese*, New York, 1919, Gutenberg eBook 16500](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/16500), identifies *Drinking Alone by Moonlight* as three poems. His complete poem I corresponds to the existing Li Bai `月下獨酌`; II and III are not attached to this reader. Gutenberg credits David Starner, Jonathan Niehof and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team. The separately scanned [1919 New York copy](https://archive.org/details/moretranslation00paigoog), printed p. 27 ([PDF p. 34](https://archive.org/download/moretranslation00paigoog/moretranslation00paigoog.pdf#page=34)), was visually checked for the full translation, its boundary and Waley's note that the Cloudy River is the Milky Way. The relationship between this copy and Gutenberg's transcription is not established. His `shadow as slave` is an interpretive addition not explicit in the displayed Chinese.
  - The [importer](../scripts/import-waley-translations.py) pins both Gutenberg HTML transcriptions and both comparison PDFs, enforces the numbered/group boundaries and expected line counts, removes a footnote marker without adding it to the verse, and checks each reviewed translation hash. The reading text preserves verse lines and Gutenberg's punctuation but normalizes spacing and typographic detail and omits indentation; it is not diplomatic. Save the [1918 HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/42290/pg42290-images.html) as `gutenberg-42290.html`, the [1919 HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/16500/pg16500-images.html) as `gutenberg-16500.html`, the [1918 PDF](https://archive.org/download/cu31924023430097/cu31924023430097.pdf) as `waley-1918-london.pdf` and the [1919 PDF](https://archive.org/download/moretranslation00paigoog/moretranslation00paigoog.pdf) as `waley-1919-ny.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-waley-translations.py <evidence-directory> --check`. Hashes preserve the reviewed inputs, not proof that visual collation or a translator's interpretation is correct.
- Twenty-one complete five-character quatrains attributed to Li He (李賀) follow *箋註評點李長吉歌詩* in a [Zhejiang University / CADAL *Siku quanshu* facsimile](https://archive.org/details/06049046.cn). The heading [馬詩二十三首](https://archive.org/download/06049046.cn/06049046.cn.pdf#page=88) explicitly identifies twenty-three poems; this selection comprises 其二–其十三 and 其十五–其二十三, each counted once. The other two are excluded because their Wikisource verse contains `SKchar` placeholders not resolved for this batch. Each selected verse and its following numbered boundary, or the next work after 其二十三, was checked visually in PDF pp. 88–100. The number and opening words in the titles are editorial, not separate historical titles.
  - The historical poems are public domain in the United States; check local law. The facsimiles are linked, not republished, and their reproduction dates and image-reuse terms have not been established. The readings adapt [Chinese Wikisource revision 790637](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E7%AE%8B%E8%A8%BB%E8%A9%95%E3%B8%83%E6%9D%8E%E9%95%B7%E5%90%89%E6%AD%8C%E8%A9%A9%20%28%E5%9B%9B%E5%BA%AB%E5%85%A8%E6%9B%B8%E6%9C%AC%29%2F%E5%8D%B72&oldid=790637), credited to contributors including 維基小霸王 and Wmr-bot, and are distributed under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), separately from the software license. No translation or scan image is supplied. The transcription identifies the same *Siku* work, but its relationship to the scanned copy is not established. The later compilation is not an autograph or proof of attribution, and the witnesses may share antecedents. China is associated with the poet, not asserted as a place of composition; exact composition years are unknown.
  - The separate [Peking University / CADAL Wang Qi facsimile, PDF p. 10](https://archive.org/download/02099632.cn/02099632.cn.pdf#page=10), and [*昌谷集* revision 740547](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E6%98%8C%E8%B0%B7%E9%9B%86%20%28%E6%9D%8E%E8%B3%80%2C%20%E5%9B%9B%E5%BA%AB%E5%85%A8%E6%9B%B8%E6%9C%AC%29%2F%E5%8D%B72&oldid=740547), credited to Wikisource contributors including 維基小霸王 and Wmr-bot, were consulted for selected comparisons, not blended into the reading. In 其五 the selected *Siku* text reads `大漠山如雪`, while both comparisons read `大漠沙如雪`; the alternative is explicitly labeled in the display title so either form can be searched without silently replacing the verse. In 其九 the two transcriptions read `飂叔` and `颼叔`, respectively; in 其十六 the selected `颴風` differs from *昌谷集*'s `飃風`, and the *Siku* commentary records `飈` as an alternative. The *Siku* commentary on 其四 suggests `精` for the second `星`, but the verse retains `星`. In 其六 the *Siku* has `朱色`; the separate [*李賀歌詩編* revision 1755495](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E6%9D%8E%E8%B3%80%E6%AD%8C%E8%A9%A9%E7%B7%A8%2F%E5%8D%B7%E7%AC%AC%E4%BA%8C&oldid=1755495), credited to contributor Shiuh bee, reads `珠色`; that transcription's print provenance is not established here. These are selected comparisons, not a complete variant census.

- Twenty-five separately titled English poems by Christina Rossetti follow [Project Gutenberg eBook 19188](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/19188), *Poems*, whose title page identifies a 1906 Boston Little, Brown printing and whose facing page identifies the author's revised and enlarged 1876 edition. Gutenberg credits Steven desJardins, Jeffrey Johnson and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. The historical poems and pre-1931 editions are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translation or scan image is republished.
  - The selections and printed pages are *Dream-land* (24–25), *At Home* (25–26), *From Sunset to Star Rise* (26–27), *Love from the North* (27–28), *Winter Rain* (29–30), *A Dirge* (30), *Spring* (34–35), *The Lambs of Grasmere, 1860* (36–37), *A Birthday* (37), *Remember* (38), *After Death* (39), *An End* (40), *Song* beginning “O roses for the flush of youth” (43), *A Summer Wish* (46–47), *An Apple Gathering* (47–48), *Song* beginning “Two doves upon the selfsame branch” (48), *Maude Clare* (49–51), *Echo* (51), *Winter: My Secret* (52–53), *Another Spring* (53–54), *Fata Morgana* (55–56), *No, Thank You, John* (56–57), *May* (57–58), *Song* beginning “When I am dead, my dearest” (67) and *Up-Hill* (77). These are complete individual works in this printing, not stanzas or a claim about every version. The three *Song* titles are retained; their opening lines distinguish them in the catalogue.
  - The Gutenberg text was compared line by line, in order and without punctuation, against OCR of the Library of Congress [1888 Roberts Brothers scan](https://archive.org/details/poems00ross5); headings, boundaries, page turns and OCR discrepancies were checked visually. The 1888 title page and facing leaf name that year and the 1876 revised/enlarged edition respectively. This comparison is not established as the same copy or impression as Gutenberg's 1906 source, and is not an exhaustive punctuation collation or a diplomatic transcription. Indentation is omitted. In *May*, Gutenberg's adjacent preformatted blocks split the poem after eight lines; the editorial stanza break follows the 1888 scan. In *Dream-land*, the scan has `charmèd`, which its OCR drops; the Gutenberg accent is retained. In *Up-Hill*, Gutenberg reads `other`, whereas the 1888 scan appears to show `otner`, possibly damaged type or a printing error; the Gutenberg reading is retained. These are not a complete census of variants. England is associated with Rossetti, not asserted as the place of composition; exact composition years are unknown. Religious language or the poet's faith alone is not classified as devotional.

The scans have similar page layout and may be related, so they are not independent proof of textual descent or authorship. The Tokyo facsimile reads `瀟瀟風雨夜`, whereas that comparison reads `蕭蕭風雨夜` and records additional alternate characters. The facsimile reads `懶起粧`, while the comparison has `嬾起妝` and notes `寄李憶員外` as another title. These are not a complete variant census. Punctuation, reading lines and some character forms are normalized; the readings follow the facsimile, not a silent blend of witnesses, and are not diplomatic transcriptions.

The historical poems and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The NCL describes the resource as open access for online reading; its scan is linked, not republished. No translation is supplied.
  - The selections, using the reader's page numbers, are *送友人* and *聽僧吹蘆管* (14), *酬郭簡州寄柑子* (14–15), both poems under *上川主武元衡相國二首* and *憶荔枝* (15), *送姚員外* and *酬祝十三秀才* (16), both under *賦凌雲寺二首* (19), *上王尚書* (20), *酬辛員外折花見遺* and the first of *贈遠二首* (25), and *柳絮* (26). Each is a complete four-line work in this printing; all three *二首* headings explicitly identify pairs, and the numbers and opening words added to their catalogue titles are editorial. The second *贈遠* poem is omitted pending review of an uncertain variant-note placement; a nearby mountain poem with an unclear glyph is also omitted.
  - Each selected text and its boundaries were visually checked in the linked reader; use its page control to reach the cited pages. The later 1906 collection is not an autograph or proof of every attribution. These readings adapt the separately linked Chinese Wikisource transcription of a **different** *Siku quanshu* collection, revision 679301, credited to its contributors including 維基小霸王 under CC BY-SA 4.0. Punctuation, reading lines and some character forms are normalized; this is aided comparison, not a diplomatic transcription. China identifies an association with Xue Tao, not an established composition location; exact composition years are unknown.
  - Significant differences include 1906 `聊一弄` versus Wikisource `勞一弄` in *聽僧吹蘆管*; `明月` and `珠簾下` versus `朗月` and `珠簾上` in the first *上川主* poem; `移尊` versus `移樽` in the second; `榜下看` versus `榜上看` in *酬祝十三秀才*; and `一任南飛` versus `一晌南飛` in *柳絮*. The 1906 heading names `武元衡相國` and notes a copy without `元衡`; the comparison heading omits those characters. The 1906 print also records alternate characters beside the main readings of *送友人*, *酬祝十三秀才*, the first *賦凌雲寺* poem, and *柳絮*. The main readings are retained, not silently blended with alternatives.
  - The [reviewed readings](../scripts/sources/xue-tao-1906-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-xue-tao-1906.py) pin the reading file, each work, the selected headings and the comparison revision. Save the [raw Wikisource revision](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E8%96%9B%E6%BF%A4%E6%9D%8E%E5%86%B6%E8%A9%A9%E9%9B%86%20%28%E5%9B%9B%E5%BA%AB%E5%85%A8%E6%9B%B8%E6%9C%AC%29&oldid=679301&action=raw) as `wikisource-xue-li-oldid679301.txt` and run `python3 scripts/import-xue-tao-1906.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer checks the documented comparison adjustments and rejects other character differences, but it does not download, pin or automatically verify the NCL scan; hashes do not prove the accuracy of visual collation.
- Twenty separately headed English poems by Walt Whitman follow the HTML transcription in [Project Gutenberg eBook 27494](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/27494), *The Patriotic Poems of Walt Whitman*, published by Doubleday, Page & Company in 1918. Gutenberg credits K. Nordquist, Carla Foust and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. The historical poems and pre-1931 edition are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translation or scan image is republished.
  - The 1918 selections are *Thick-Sprinkled Bunting* (p. 3), *Beat! Beat! Drums!* (4–5), *City of Ships* (6), *A March in the Ranks Hard-Prest, and the Road Unknown* (7–8), *Come Up from the Fields Father* (9–11), *A Twilight Song* (12–13), *A Sight in Camp in the Daybreak Gray and Dim* (14–15), *Year That Trembled and Reel'd Beneath Me* (16), *Old War-Dreams* (41), *Delicate Cluster* (42), *To a Certain Civilian* (43), *Adieu to a Soldier* (44), *Long, Too Long America* (45), *Weave In, My Hardy Life* (49), *Spirit Whose Work Is Done* (51–52), *O Captain! My Captain!* (76–77), *Hush'd Be the Camps To-Day* (78), *I Hear America Singing* (87), *To Foreign Lands* (141) and *For You O Democracy* (143). These are complete individual works in this printing, not stanzas counted separately or claims about every witness.
  - Gutenberg's HTML was compared in verse order with punctuation-insensitive OCR of the Internet Archive [1918 scan](https://archive.org/details/patrioticpoemsof00whitiala), with selected visual checks. Text extents were also compared, using OCR and selected visual checks, against an identified [1891/92 *Leaves of Grass* scan](https://archive.org/details/grasssands00whitrich). The 1918 scan and Gutenberg transcription represent the same edition, not necessarily the same physical copy; the anthology is not an independent manuscript witness. This is aided comparison, not exhaustive character-by-character punctuation collation or a diplomatic transcription. Indentation and typographic detail are omitted; printed wraps are rejoined. The United States identifies an association with Whitman, not an established composition location; exact composition years are not asserted. The 1918 headings date *Spirit Whose Work Is Done* “Washington City, 1865” and *Hush'd Be the Camps To-Day* “May 4, 1865”. Religious imagery alone is not classified as devotional.
  - In *Beat! Beat! Drums!* the 1918 print and Gutenberg read `Not the peaceful farmer`, while the 1891/92 print reads `Nor the peaceful farmer`. In *A March in the Ranks*, the 1918 `odour of blood` is `odor of blood` in the 1891/92 print. These examples are not a complete variant census. The 1918 anthology says one selection is incomplete but does not name it; *Faces* prints only section 5 of the five-part work in the 1891/92 witness, so it is excluded. *How Solemn as One by One* is also excluded because an initial glyph is damaged or incomplete in the available 1918 scan. No witness is silently completed from another.
  - The [manifest](../scripts/sources/whitman-1918.json) and [importer](../scripts/import-whitman-1918.py) pin source files, headings, page ranges, stanza line counts and reading hashes. Save the [Gutenberg HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/27494/27494-h/27494-h.htm) as `pg27494-h.htm`, the [1918 PDF](https://archive.org/download/patrioticpoemsof00whitiala/patrioticpoemsof00whitiala.pdf) as `whitman-1918.pdf`, and the [1891/92 comparison PDF](https://archive.org/download/grasssands00whitrich/grasssands00whitrich_bw.pdf) as `grasssands00whitrich_bw.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-whitman-1918.py <evidence-directory> --check`. In the HTML, *Beat! Beat! Drums!* has a physical wrap inside one verse span, while *Weave In* has two actual verses inside one span; the extractor explicitly handles both and rejects undocumented internal breaks. Hashes protect reviewed inputs, not the accuracy of collation. Normalized first lines and pairs of lines were screened against all 2,071 prior originals and translations; this cannot rule out every variant or translated duplicate.
- Nine complete English poems by John Keats follow the HTML transcription and page images in [Project Gutenberg eBook 23684](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/23684), *Keats: Poems Published in 1820*, edited by M. Robertson and printed at Oxford in 1909. Gutenberg credits Jonathan Ingram, Lisa Reigel and the Project Gutenberg Online Distributed Proofreading Team and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. The historical poems and pre-1931 edition are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translation or page image is republished.
  - The nine individually headed works are *Ode to a Nightingale* (pp. 107–112), *Ode on a Grecian Urn* (113–116), *Ode to Psyche* (117–121), *Fancy* (122–127), *Ode* beginning “Bards of Passion and of Mirth” (128–130), *Lines on the Mermaid Tavern* (131–132), *Robin Hood* (133–136), *To Autumn* (137–139) and *Ode on Melancholy* (140–142). The incipit distinguishes the generically titled *Ode*; *Robin Hood* has the printed subtitle “To a Friend”. Numbered stanzas are not separate works. *Hyperion*, which the edition expressly calls unfinished, is not included. Completeness is in this printing, not a claim about every version.
  - Gutenberg's HTML verse was compared in order against punctuation-insensitive OCR of its own 36 linked page images, and OCR mismatches, printed headings, work boundaries and page turns were visually checked. This is aided collation, not an independently sourced witness or a diplomatic re-transcription: punctuation was not exhaustively checked character by character. Indentation, typographic detail, printed stanza numbers and Robertson's line numbers are omitted; verse lines wrapped in print are rejoined, including `ways` in *Nightingale* (p. 109), `pain` in *Psyche* (p. 120) and `tongue` in *Melancholy* (p. 142). The HTML retains `Provençal`, `Phœbe` and `grenè`, while Gutenberg's plain-text format reads `Provencal`, `Phoebe` and `grene`; these are format differences, not evidence of separate historical versions. Robertson's preface says the edition reprints an 1820 British Museum copy, adding line numbers and correcting a few misprints; that 1820 copy was not independently checked. England identifies an association with Keats, not an established composition location; exact composition years are not asserted.
  - The [manifest](../scripts/sources/keats-1909.json) and [importer](../scripts/import-keats-1909.py) pin the source archive, printed page ranges, headings, stanza lengths and reading hashes. Save the [Gutenberg HTML archive](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/23684/23684-h.zip) as `pg23684-h.zip` and run `python3 scripts/import-keats-1909.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The archive contains the HTML and page images; [p. 107](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/23684/23684-h/images/107.png) through [p. 142](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/23684/23684-h/images/142.png) are also individually linked. Pinned hashes guard the reviewed files, not the accuracy of the collation.
- Twelve complete English poems by William Blake follow the Metropolitan Museum of Art's individual plate images from *Songs of Innocence and of Experience*, copy Y, printed about 1825. The Met identifies the plates as public domain and credits the Rogers Fund, 1917; the historical poems and print are public domain in the United States. Check local law. No translation or plate image is republished; each reader links to its Met object.
  - Each poem's text, separately titled beginning and ending were visually checked against its plate. Completeness is in this copy, not a claim about all witnesses or Blake's final intentions. Plate 43 has three separately titled poems: *My Pretty Rose Tree*, *Ah! Sun-Flower* and *The Lilly*. They are three works, not three sections of one. *The Fly* is read down the left column, then down the right. In *The Lilly*, `bright` wraps beneath its verse line; in *The Garden of Love*, `rounds` does likewise. Both are rejoined, not counted as extra lines. The alternate display title *The Lily* aids discovery without changing the engraved title.
  - [Project Gutenberg eBook 1934](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/1934), credited to David Price, was a comparison aid. Its [earlier HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1934/old/sinex10h.htm) identifies the source as the 1901 R. Brimley Johnson edition, not the Met's copy Y. The engraved *Tyger* reads `Tyger` and `sieze`, whereas Gutenberg has `Tiger` and `seize`; *The Lamb* has `Dost` and `wooly`, where Gutenberg has `Does` and `woolly`; *Infant Sorrow* has `swadling`, where Gutenberg has `swaddling`. The copy-Y readings are retained; these examples are not an exhaustive list of variants. The reading transcription normalizes spacing, punctuation, some apostrophes, contractions and typographic details; it is not diplomatic. In *The Garden of Love*, the plate's `flow'rs` is expanded to `flowers` in the tomb-stones line. England is associated with Blake, not asserted as a composition location; the plate dates and estimated printing date do not establish exact composition years.
  - The [reviewed readings](../scripts/sources/blake-copy-y-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-blake-copy-y.py) pin all ten plate-image hashes, the comparison text, selections and stanza line counts. For reproduction, save the linked original-resolution Met images under these names: [plate 8](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816581.jpg) as `met-347905-lamb.jpg`; [25](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816666.jpg) as `met-347934-infant-joy.jpg`; [39](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816680.jpg) as `met-347973-sick-rose.jpg`; [40](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816704.jpg) as `met-347974-fly.jpg`; [42](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816703.jpg) as `met-347983-tyger.jpg`; [43](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816691.jpg) as `met-347980-rose-sunflower-lilly.jpg`; [44](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816692.jpg) as `met-347987-garden-love.jpg`; [46](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816693.jpg) as `met-347997-london.jpg`; [48](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816695.jpg) as `met-348004-infant-sorrow.jpg`; and [49](https://images.metmuseum.org/CRDImages/dp/original/DP816701.jpg) as `met-348006-poison-tree.jpg`. Save the [Gutenberg UTF-8 text](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1934/1934-0.txt) as `pg1934.txt`, then run `python3 scripts/import-blake-copy-y.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The hashes guard the reviewed inputs; they do not themselves establish that a transcription is accurate. Six initially saved JPEGs had different file bytes from the 2026-11-11 downloads but identical decoded RGB pixels; the pinned hashes are for the latter. Future byte changes require review, not a silently bypassed guard.
- Ten individually numbered poems from Tao Yuanming's *Drinking Wine* follow the [National Central Library's scanned *Tao Yuanming ji*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:NCL-09399_%E9%99%B6%E6%B7%B5%E6%98%8E%E9%9B%86.pdf), catalogued on Commons as an Yichun Zhang printing from the Ming Chongzhen reign (1628–1644). The historical poems and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The scan is linked, not republished. Nos. 5 and 9 have separately credited Waley translations, described above.
  - The print labels each selected poem separately: nos. 1–3, 5–6, 8–9, 14 and 16–17. Their verse and numbered beginnings and following boundaries were checked against the linked scan, including the page turn in no. 5. Completeness is in this printing, not every witness. The cycle's shared prose preface is not another poem. The other numbered pieces are not imported in this selection; no. 10 in particular has an unresolved incipit glyph. China identifies an association with the poet, not a documented composition place; the exact years are not established. Although the comparison page assigns 417 to the series, that claim is not adopted here.
  - These scan-checked reading texts adapt the [Chinese Wikisource revision](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E9%A3%B2%E9%85%92%E4%BA%8C%E5%8D%81%E9%A6%96&oldid=2692638), credited to its contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). Reading lines, punctuation and some character forms are normalized; this is not a diplomatic edition. The adapted text uses `荊` and `遊` in nos. 14 and 16 where that Wikisource revision has `荆` and `游`. In no. 5 the Ming print reads `悠然見南山`; its commentary reports `望` in other texts. Other witnesses differ, and this is not an exhaustive variant census.
  - The [readings](../scripts/sources/tao-drinking-ming-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-tao-drinking-ming.py) pin the scan, comparison revision, numbered selections, page ranges and couplet counts. Save the [scan PDF](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3e/NCL-09399_%E9%99%B6%E6%B7%B5%E6%98%8E%E9%9B%86.pdf) as `tao-ming.pdf` and the [raw Wikisource revision](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E9%A3%B2%E9%85%92%E4%BA%8C%E5%8D%81%E9%A6%96&oldid=2692638&action=raw) as `wikisource-drinking-oldid2692638.txt`; run `python3 scripts/import-tao-drinking-ming.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer verifies the cited character-form adjustments and rejects other undocumented differences from that comparison revision.
  - Eight further separately numbered poems, nos. 4, 7, 11–13, 15 and 18–19, use the same scan and credited CC BY-SA 4.0 Wikisource revision. Their complete verse and numbered boundaries were checked individually in the Ming print; no. 12 crosses PDF pp. 63–64. Punctuation, reading lines and some character forms are normalized; these are aided reading transcriptions, not diplomatic editions. The former ten original texts and their URLs are unchanged. No. 10's incipit remains unresolved, and no. 20's first glyph is obscured in this scan; neither is counted here.
  - The Ming no. 4 reads `厲響思清晨，遠去何所依，自植孤生松`, versus the comparison's `厲響思清遠，去來何依依，因值孤生松`; the comparison's `托` is represented as `託`. No. 11 reads `長飢至于老`, versus `長飢至於老`. No. 15 has `歲月相從過` rather than `歲月相催逼`; no. 18 has `載醪祛所感` rather than `載醪袪所惑`; no. 19 has `拂衣歸田里` rather than `終死歸田里`. These are print/comparison differences, not a complete census of variants. The scan and Wikisource revision do not establish independent manuscript descent or a single final text.
  - The [additional readings](../scripts/sources/tao-drinking-ming-more-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-tao-drinking-ming-more.py) pin the same evidence, poem boundaries and the exact documented comparison-to-print substitutions. With the two files saved as above, run `python3 scripts/import-tao-drinking-ming-more.py <evidence-directory> --check`; it rejects undocumented differences. Fresh automated requests to both source URLs returned HTTP 403 on 2026-11-11, so this check used previously saved files matching the pinned hashes; those access restrictions were not bypassed. Traditional and simplified forms of several openings and internal lines were screened against the existing catalogue, but that does not exclude every possible textual or attribution variant.
- Twelve complete Chinese song lyrics attributed to Li Qingzhao follow the National Library of China's scanned [*Shuyu ci*, Siyinzhai edition](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:NLC892-312002087162-387332_%E6%BC%B1%E7%8E%89%E8%A9%9E.pdf), which Commons catalogues as 1881 and marks public domain. The historical originals and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The scan is linked, not republished, and no translation is supplied.
  - Each selection's verse, printed beginning and following boundary were checked against the scan, including page turns; completeness means in this printing, not in every witness. Punctuation, reading lines, stanza breaks and some character forms are normalized. The tune names represented by `又` in the print are expanded, and incipits are added to distinguish works sharing a tune. `浣溪沙` and `一剪梅` are alternate display spellings of printed `浣溪紗` and `一翦梅`. This is a scan-based reading transcription, not a diplomatic edition. Printed lacunae in other lyrics are not silently filled or counted as complete works.
  - [Project Gutenberg eBook 25367](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/25367), credited to Christine Zeng and the Zhong Hua Publishing House, was consulted as a transcription aid; it does not identify its print edition. The 1881 scan reads `江梅已過`, `暖雨晴風` and `人比黃花瘦`, whereas Gutenberg reads `江海已過`, `暖日晴風` and `人似黃花瘦`; the scan's own note also identifies `似` as an alternative. The scan's `淚濕羅衣脂粉滿` has a marginal alternative `淚搵征衣脂粉暖`. These examples are not a complete census of variants. In `天接雲濤`, the historical character `䢜` is normalized to `歸`; Taiwan's [character-variants dictionary](https://dict.variants.moe.edu.tw/dictView.jsp?ID=22549&q=1) documents that equivalence.
  - Li's early song-lyric collection was lost and later attributions differ. Ronald Egan's [2019 introduction](https://library.oapen.org/bitstream/handle/20.500.12657/23528/1006618.pdf) treats his first 23 songs, to which these twelve correspond, as among the most reliable early attributions; that is a scholarly assessment, not proof of sole authorship. His translation and commentary are CC BY-NC-ND and are not reproduced. Other anthologies attribute `常記溪亭日暮` to Su Shi, Lü Dongbin or an unknown writer. A version of `小院閒窗春色深` is [attributed elsewhere to Zhou Bangyan or Ouyang Xiu](https://www.xuges.com/mj/z/zby/py/187.htm), and a reconstructed [*Fushan ji*](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E6%B5%AE%E5%B1%B1%E9%9B%86_(%E5%9B%9B%E5%BA%AB%E5%85%A8%E6%9B%B8%E6%9C%AC)/%E5%8D%B703&oldid=787928) attributes a related `淡蕩春光寒食天` to Zhong Bing. These alternatives are disclosed, not silently resolved; the latter online transcription was not used as the text or rights basis.
  - [BnF](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb119129450) gives Li's death as 1155; [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Li-Qingzhao) says after 1155. The records use the twelfth century rather than assert an exact death year. China identifies an association with the poet, not a composition location; composition years are not asserted.
  - The [reviewed readings](../scripts/sources/li-qingzhao-1881-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-li-qingzhao-1881.py) pin the exact scan and Gutenberg hashes, PDF page ranges and reading-line counts. Save the linked scan as `shuyu-1881.pdf` and the [Gutenberg UTF-8 file](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/25367/25367-0.txt) as `pg25367.txt`; run `python3 scripts/import-li-qingzhao-1881.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The comparison eBook and scholarly study do not supply silently substituted readings.
  - Six further distinct lyrics attributed to Li use the same 1881 scan: *鷓鴣天* (`寒日蕭蕭上鎖窗`, PDF p. 9), *臨江仙* (`庭院深深深幾許`, pp. 9–10), *好事近* (`風定落花深`, p. 10), *訴衷情* (`夜來沈醉卸妝遲`, p. 10), *壺中天慢* (`蕭條庭院`, p. 11) and *聲聲慢* (`尋尋覓覓`, p. 11). Each complete printed text, heading, following boundary and relevant page turn was checked against the scan, aided by [Gutenberg eBook 25367](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/25367); completeness is in this printing, not a claim about every witness. Punctuation, reading lines, stanza breaks and some character forms are normalized, not diplomatically transcribed. The display title `念奴嬌` is a comparison tune name for printed `壺中天慢`. The earlier twelve records are unchanged; no translations are added.
  - Egan's introduction treats his lyrics 3.16, 3.19, 3.21 and 3.22 among the earliest attributed group, and 3.29 and 3.30 among a later Southern Song group he also regards as relatively reliable. This is evidence, not proof of sole authorship. `庭院深深深幾許` is also the opening of an earlier lyric now commonly attributed to Feng Yansi and another Li-attributed lyric: compare full texts, not only the incipit, before treating them as duplicates. No text of Egan's CC BY-NC-ND translation is republished.
  - The 1881 readings include `酒闌愛喜團茶苦` and `人老建康城`, versus Gutenberg's `酒闌更喜團茶苦` and `人客遠安城`; Egan has `人客建康城`. A note in the print questions `是` in `正是傷春時節`, which the main text retains; the print reads `玉樽`, `青釭` and `啼鴂`, while Gutenberg has `玉尊`, `青缸` and `啼鴃`. The print's `酒醒熏破惜春夢遠又不成歸` differs from Gutenberg and Egan's `酒醒熏破春睡夢遠不成歸`; the print's editor questions the metre and suggests a copying addition or different historical form. The 1881 `清露晨梳` and `煙歛` differ from Gutenberg's `清露晨流` and `煙斂`; Egan also reads `晨流`. The 1881 `晚來風急` agrees with Gutenberg, while Egan reads `晚來風力`. The printed window glyph in `聲聲慢` is normalized to `窗`; Taiwan's [variant-character dictionary](https://dict.variants.moe.edu.tw/dictView.jsp?ID=-31863) identifies `牎` and `牕` as window variants. These examples are not a complete census of variants.
  - The [additional readings](../scripts/sources/li-qingzhao-1881-more-readings.txt) and [importer](../scripts/import-li-qingzhao-1881-more.py) pin the same source files, selection, page ranges, line counts and per-lyric hashes. With the scan and Gutenberg text saved as above, run `python3 scripts/import-li-qingzhao-1881-more.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The importer also rejects undocumented character differences from that Gutenberg comparison, ignoring punctuation and lineation. Hashes and comparison checks protect reviewed inputs, not the accuracy of collation. The print's lacunose `歸鴻聲斷殘雲碧` and `莫許杯深琥珀濃`, the uncertain opening glyph after `草際鳴` in *行香子*, and `湖上風來波浩渺` with its apparent missing character are excluded rather than silently completed from other witnesses.
- Ten complete English poems by Edgar Allan Poe follow Project Gutenberg's transcription of the 1900 George Bell/Macmillan [*Poems of Edgar Allan Poe*](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/76996). Gutenberg credits Chris Curnow, Turgut Dincer, Linda Cantoni and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team; it says the source images came from the Internet Archive and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. The originals and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translation or illustration is reproduced.
  - The verse, printed beginnings and endings were compared with OCR and visual checks of the University of California [1900 scan](https://archive.org/details/poemsillustrated00poeerich), including page turns. Gutenberg does not identify its source copy, so we do not claim it is the scanned copy linked here. They are the same edition, not independent manuscript witnesses; completeness is in that edition. This is OCR-assisted collation, not an exhaustive diplomatic transcription or a claim to Poe's final authorized text. Gutenberg says it moved full-page illustrations to nearby paragraph breaks; illustration captions are removed, including captions split across lines. Indentation and italics are omitted, and other typographic details may differ from the printed scan.
  - *The Raven* is one 18-stanza work and *The Bells* one work with four numbered parts, not separate additions. *The Bells* has 113 verse lines; Verso's 117-line count includes its four retained part headings. The printed verse begins on p. 15, after an illustrated title leaf; Gutenberg's blank at the p. 15/16 turn within part II is removed. The 1900 *Raven* reads `darkness gave no token`, `not an instant` and `sign of parting`; the Academy of American Poets' [1849 Examiner comparison](https://poets.org/poem/raven) reads `stillness gave no token`, `not a minute` and `sign in parting`. These examples are not an exhaustive variant census. The United States identifies an association with the poet, not a claim that every poem was composed there; exact composition dates are not asserted. References to religious imagery alone do not establish a devotional classification.
  - The [source manifest](../scripts/sources/poe-1900.json) pins both input hashes, printed pages, PDF leaves, boundaries, section lengths and reading hashes. Save the [Gutenberg text](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/76996/76996-0.txt) as `pg76996.txt` and the [scan PDF](https://archive.org/download/poemsillustrated00poeerich/poemsillustrated00poeerich.pdf) as `poe-1900.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-poe-1900.py <evidence-directory> --check`. Offline guard tests run with `python3 -m unittest discover -s tests -p test_poe_import.py`. The scan is comparison evidence rather than a source of silently substituted verse.
- Eighteen Spanish romances by Federico García Lorca follow [Project Gutenberg's transcription](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/72665) of *Primer romancero gitano*, Madrid: Revista de Occidente, 1928, from images supplied by the Biblioteca Nacional de España. Its [scanned 1928 copy](https://archive.org/details/romancerogitano_202401) gives the publisher and copyright year on the title and copyright leaves; Gutenberg credits Ramón Pajares Box and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. The historical original and 1928 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The scan is linked, not republished, and no translation is supplied.
  - The 1928 contents number 18 romances, not their internal sections. The texts and boundaries were compared in order with OCR from the 1928 scan's text layer; exceptional OCR matches, printed section headings and selected page turns were checked visually. Reverse comparison found no unrepresented verse rows. This is OCR-assisted collation, not a diplomatic re-transcription of every character or an exhaustive comparison of editions. Gutenberg and the scan derive from the same BNE source, so they are not independent historical witnesses. Completeness means complete as printed in this edition. *Martirio de Santa Olalla*'s three titled sections and *Burla de don Pedro a caballo*'s printed *laguna* and *sigue* headings are retained within their respective works, not counted as additional poems. The latter's subtitle calls it a “romance con lagunas”; those named gaps are part of the printed work. Headings retained in the text are included in Verso's nonblank-line counts.
  - Gutenberg says it corrected printing errors, including the 1928 `horzas` to `orzas` and `llemas` to `yemas`; these corrections remain in the reader. The scan prints `la cuatro luces` in *Muerto de amor*, where Gutenberg reads `las cuatro luces`. The printed `II` headings in [*San Rafael*](https://archive.org/download/romancerogitano_202401/romancerogitano.pdf#page=69) and *San Gabriel* are retained within their respective romances. *Burla* restores the scan's `SEGUNDA LAGUNA` from Gutenberg's `SECUNDA LAGUNA`. On [1928 p. 131](https://archive.org/download/romancerogitano_202401/romancerogitano.pdf#page=133), part of `Una ciudad lejana` is obscured; Gutenberg's reading also appears on [p. 131 of a separately printed Chilean seventh edition](https://es.wikisource.org/wiki/P%C3%A1gina:Romancero_gitano.djvu/135). That edition's publication date and textual descent are not established here; it is comparison evidence, not the source of the reproduced verse or its rights basis. These examples do not establish every variant.
  - Indentation and italics are omitted, printed bullet separators become blank lines, and other paragraph spacing follows Gutenberg. The separately printed dedications are identified from Gutenberg in each record's context, not added to the verse. The scan prints `Creselles` in [*San Rafael*'s dedication](https://archive.org/download/romancerogitano_202401/romancerogitano.pdf#page=65), where Gutenberg reads `Croselles`; Gutenberg accents `Aladrén` in *El emplazado*'s dedication, printed `Aladren` in 1928. The latter's verse heading is `Romance del emplazado`, while its title leaf and contents say `El emplazado`. The 1928 volume groups its last three works under `Tres romances históricos`. Spain identifies an association with the poet, not an established composition place; exact composition dates are not asserted. Angelic or biblical figures alone do not establish a devotional classification.
  - The [source manifest](../scripts/sources/lorca-romancero-1928.json) pins the Gutenberg HTML, 1928 PDF, Chilean comparison-page image, ranges, dedications, line counts and reading hashes. Save the [Gutenberg HTML](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/72665/pg72665-images.html) as `gutenberg-72665.html`, the [1928 PDF](https://archive.org/download/romancerogitano_202401/romancerogitano.pdf) as `bne-1928.pdf`, and the [Chilean comparison image](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Romancero_gitano.djvu/page135-1280px-Romancero_gitano.djvu.jpg) as `chile-p131.jpg`. Run `uv run --no-project --with beautifulsoup4 python scripts/import-lorca-romancero-1928.py <evidence-directory> --check` to reproduce the records; the image is comparison evidence, not silently substituted verse.
- One complete Arabic poem by Ahmad Shawqi, *Āyat al-ʿaṣr fī samāʾ Miṣr*, is transcribed from [*al-Shawqiyyāt li-l-madāris*](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=6), printed pp. 8–12. The [title page](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=2) names Shawqi, dates the edition 1345 AH/1927 and reserves printing rights to the author. The original as published before 1931 is public domain in the United States; check local law. No translation is supplied. The [BnF authority](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb13485136d) gives Shawqi's dates as 1868–1932 and associates him with Egypt, not necessarily with the poem's place of composition.
  - We checked the poem's 58 couplets, in order and across the printed section breaks, against the school scan and the separately printed [*al-Shawqiyyāt (al-ṣaḥīḥa)*, vol. II, pp. 3–6](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-137947/137947%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20-%20%28%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B5%D8%AD%D9%8A%D8%AD%D8%A9%29.pdf#page=5); a new heading follows in each. The latter is undated in its Archive record, and its title page calls the author deceased; it is used for comparison, not as evidence of pre-1931 publication. The matching boundaries show completeness in these printings, not in every tradition or independent textual descent. The school edition's *Ayyuhā al-ʿummāl* and *Qum li-l-muʿallim* omit verses found in separately printed collected editions, so their school selections are not included as complete works. Its heading dates the aviators' arrival to 1914; that occasion does not independently establish the exact composition date or place.
  - The school edition prints `الأنس` where the posthumous volume has `الإنس`. The school scan also appears to read `حطنا الدهر`, whereas [posthumous vol. II, p. 5](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-137947/137947%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20-%20%28%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B5%D8%AD%D9%8A%D8%AD%D8%A9%29.pdf#page=7) appears to read `حظنا الدهر`; the school readings are retained. A [Wikisource transcription of an *al-Bayān* article labelled 30 April 1914](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?oldid=217507) reproduces a poem with matching boundaries, but reads `في يمين الله` where the school edition has `في عين الله`. That online text has apparent transcription errors and its magazine scan was not verified here; whether this difference is a historical variant or a transcription error remains unresolved. The online transcription was used to flag lines for visual rechecking, not as evidence of the 1914 printing's wording or rights.
  - The reading follows the 1927 wording rather than blending editions. Vowel marks, footnotes and decorative dividers are omitted; some spelling, spacing and punctuation are normalized, each printed half-line becomes a line, and blank lines mark the two printed section breaks. For example, the school scan reads `للعفاء`, `البلا` and `ميناء` near the end. This is a reading transcription, not a fully vocalized diplomatic edition. Both scans are linked, not republished. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/shawqi-schools-1927.json) pins their SHA-256 hashes, page ranges, 23/18/17 section lengths and the reviewed text hash. Save the two linked PDFs as `schools-unknown.pdf` and `correct-137947.pdf`, then run `python3 scripts/import-shawqi-schools-1927.py <evidence-directory> --check`.
  - Two further complete works follow the same 1927 school edition: [*Bank Miṣr*](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=59), printed pp. 114–115 (17 couplets), and [*Baʿd al-manfā*](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=16), printed pp. 28–32 (60 couplets). Their readings and boundaries were compared in order with the separately printed collected *al-Shawqiyyāt*, vol. I: [pp. 229–230](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-101217/101217%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA.pdf#page=253) and [pp. 54–58](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-101217/101217%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA.pdf#page=78), respectively. The Archive catalogues that comparison as 1939, but its date was not established from the scan; it is comparison evidence, not the rights basis. Separately printed does not establish independent manuscript descent, and matching boundaries establish completeness only in these printings.
  - Both readings follow the school scan; vowel marks, explanatory footnotes and decorative ornaments are omitted, and spelling, spacing and punctuation are partly normalized. Each printed half-line becomes a line; the four printed divisions in *Baʿd al-manfā* remain blank lines within one poem, not four extra works. The school scan and collected comparison read `نثرت الدمع`, while a [modern Hindawi transcription](https://www.hindawi.org/books/70706142/5/) reads `فنثري الدمع`. The school scan prints `بينكمو` in *Bank Miṣr*, where [Hindawi](https://www.hindawi.org/books/70706142/37/) has `بينكم`. The school readings are retained. Hindawi was used as a transcription aid, not accepted as evidence of the historical wording or rights. The school edition describes both works as recited in 1920, the latter after return from exile; that claim about an occasion is not independent proof of composition dates or places. No devotional category is inferred from references to God. No translations are supplied.
  - The [additional manifest](../scripts/sources/shawqi-schools-more-1927.json) pins the two PDF hashes, printed pages, PDF leaves, section lengths and reading hashes. Save the school PDF as `schools-1927.pdf` and the collected comparison PDF as `collected-101217.pdf`, then run `python3 scripts/import-shawqi-schools-more-1927.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The existing aviation poem and its source and URL remain unchanged.
  - Two more school-edition works are [*Anas al-wujūd*](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=18), pp. 33–36 (42 couplets), and [*Miṣr tujaddid majdahā*](https://archive.org/download/1525pdf_201912/1525%20%D9%83%D8%AA%D8%A7%D8%A8%20%D8%A7%D9%82%D8%B1%D8%A7%20%D8%A7%D9%88%D9%86%D9%84%D8%A7%D9%8A%D9%86%20%20%20%20pdf%20%20%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20%28%D9%84%D9%84%D9%85%D8%AF%D8%A7%D8%B1%D8%B3%29.pdf#page=31), pp. 58–61 (43 couplets). Their verse and boundaries were compared in order with [*al-Shawqiyyāt (al-ṣaḥīḥa)*, vol. II, pp. 53–58](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-137947/137947%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA%20-%20%28%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B5%D8%AD%D9%8A%D8%AD%D8%A9%29.pdf#page=55), and the separately printed [collected vol. I, pp. 102–105](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-101217/101217%20-%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%B4%D9%88%D9%82%D9%8A%D8%A7%D8%AA.pdf#page=126). Neither comparison's publication date is established from its scan. They show completeness of the verse in these printings, not independent manuscript descent or universal boundaries. The comparison to *Anas al-wujūd* has a prose dedication before the verse which the school poem does not print; only the latter's complete verse is reproduced.
  - The school and comparison of *Anas al-wujūd* print `بالسعد والنحس`; [al-Diwan's modern transcription](https://www.aldiwan.net/poem63710.html) has `بالسعد والنخس`. The school heading says the poem was presented to former president Roosevelt on a 1912 Egyptian visit, but a [contemporary newspaper](https://nebnewspapers.unl.edu/lccn/sn95070058/1910-03-25/ed-1/seq-1/) and [his published addresses](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/13930) document a 1910 visit. The reason for the discrepancy and the poem's exact composition date are unresolved; neither visit nor the poem establishes a composition place.
  - In *Miṣr tujaddid majdahā* the school scan prints `عامت بناته`, while the collected comparison has `علمت بناته` and [Hindawi](https://www.hindawi.org/books/70706142/14/) has `علت ببناته`. The school also reads `عندهمو`, `أقبلن ... المآثر` and `المحسنات`; the modern aid has `عندهم`, `أقبل ... المنائر` and `المحصنات`. We retain the 1927 readings. The school heading dates the women's gathering to 1920; [a later account](https://www.hindawi.org/books/94053925/5/) gives 1924. We do not resolve the occasion's year or infer a composition date from it.
  - Modern transcriptions were aids rather than historical or rights evidence; both readings were checked against the school scan, not blended with them. Vowel marks, footnotes, ornaments and some punctuation are omitted; spacing and spelling are partly normalized. Each complete couplet appears on one line, rejoining words split across printed half-lines, with school-edition divisions retained as blank lines: 16/19/7 and 27/16. These are reading transcriptions, not fully vocalized diplomatic editions. No translation or inferred devotional category is supplied. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/shawqi-schools-historical-1927.json) pins the scans, page ranges, section counts and reading hashes. Save the school and collected PDFs as above, and the comparison vol. II as `correct-137947.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-shawqi-schools-historical-1927.py <evidence-directory> --check`.
- Thirty Italian poems by Dante Alighieri follow Project Gutenberg's transcription of [*La vita nuova*](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/71218), Roux e Viarengo, 1903, with each reader linked to the corresponding pages in an independently scanned [1903 second edition](https://archive.org/details/lavitanuova04aliggoog). Gutenberg credits Barbara Magni and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team, says its source images were supplied by the University and City Library of Cologne, and marks the eBook public domain in the United States. Dante's originals and the historical printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translations are supplied. Gutenberg's catalogue metadata says 1902, but its transcribed title page says **1903**; the separate scanned copy's title page says **second edition, 1903**. We do not assert that the Cologne source and the scanned copy are the same physical copy or impression.
  - Gutenberg's printed index lists 31 poems. The [independent *Vita Nova* incipit index](https://the-orb.arlima.net/encyclop/culture/lit/italian/da_e_rin.htm) identifies the works and their traditional chapters. *Sì lungamente m'ha tenuto Amore* is excluded: [Treccani](https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/si-lungiamente-m-ha-tenuto-amore_(Enciclopedia-Dantesca)/) describes it as an interrupted canzone. *Era venuta nella mente mia* has two alternative opening quatrains followed by ten shared verses; the selected record uses the second opening and the shared verses once. The first opening remains visible in the source and scan, not counted as another poem. See [Treccani's discussion](https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/era-venuta-ne-la-mente-mia_(Enciclopedia-Dantesca)/) of the alternative beginnings and their uncertain chronology.
  - *Quantunque volte* is presented in Dante's prose as two stanzas for two voices, and [Treccani identifies the piece as a two-stanza canzone](https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/quantunque-volte-lasso-mi-rimembra_(Enciclopedia-Dantesca)/); both stanzas are included together as one work. The collection also contains two 20-line **sonetti doppi**, a complete 44-line ballata, and three longer canzoni. *Donna pietosa* is one 84-line poem even though Gutenberg's HTML splits it across two containers and the scan has inserted plates; *Gli occhi dolenti* includes its six-line closing congedo. These works are not counted by stanza or section.
  - All selected verses were matched in order with OCR of the separate 1903 scan; PDF leaf mappings, selected OCR mismatches, the two openings and the longer poems' page turns were checked visually. This is OCR-assisted collation, **not** an exhaustive diplomatic transcription of every accent and punctuation mark. The printed volume's pages 72–73 are absent from the linked scan, but the selected sonnet on p. 71 is present in full. Gutenberg says its proofreaders silently corrected minor typographic errors. Printed indentation is omitted, and Gutenberg's verse and stanza breaks are retained, including joining a line wrapped at the edge of scan p. 133. Modernized alternate titles aid discovery without replacing the selected historical wording. Italy identifies the poet's association, not an assertion of each poem's exact composition place or date.
  - [The importer](../scripts/import-dante-vita-nuova-1903.py) pins the selected readings, boundaries, page and leaf mappings, the Gutenberg text, and the second-edition scan. Save the [Gutenberg UTF-8 text](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/71218/71218-0.txt) as `gutenberg-71218.txt` and the [scan PDF](https://archive.org/download/lavitanuova04aliggoog/lavitanuova04aliggoog.pdf) as `la-vita-nuova-1903.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-dante-vita-nuova-1903.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The scan is collation evidence, not a source of silently substituted wording.
- Forty-eight Portuguese sonnets attributed to Luís de Camões follow Project Gutenberg's transcription of the 1843 Paris [*Obras completas*, vol. II](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/31509), edited by Barreto Feio and Gomes Monteiro, with each reader linked to the relevant page in the Internet Archive's [scanned copy](https://archive.org/details/obrascompletasd00montgoog). Gutenberg credits Pedro Saborano and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team and marks its eBook public domain in the United States. The original works and 1843 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. No translations are supplied.
  - Attribution of Camões's lyric poems is contested. Leodegário A. de Azevedo Filho's [*Os sonetos de Camões*](https://www.uc.pt/fluc/eclassicos/publicacoes/ficheiros/humanitas29-30/08_Azevedo_Filho.pdf), pp. 215–217, lists 48 sonnets as a provisional minimum under Emmanuel Pereira Filho's criteria. The additions correspond to these 48, with several differing incipits identified below; this corroborates the selection without claiming scholarly unanimity, and the study's later editorial text is not reproduced. The [PUC-Rio survey of sonnet editions](https://catedravieira-ic.letras.puc-rio.br/detalhe/32/ntroducao-aos-sonetos-de-camoes) and [Maurizio Perugi's 2020 critical edition](https://ciep-ge.ch/Sonetos.pdf) discuss different attribution criteria and witnesses. Even the [1595 *Rhythmas*](https://archive.org/details/rhythmas) contains disputed or non-Camões pieces, so its presence alone is not treated as proof. *Mudam-se os tempos* and *Todo animal da calma repousava* are deferred: Azevedo Filho discusses conflicting attributions and excludes them under his criterion. Their exclusion here is not a determination that Camões did not write them.
  - Verso's existing `amor-e-fogo-camoes` is the counterpart of Gutenberg sonnet LXXXI; it is outside that 48-item selection and remains unchanged. Each addition is one complete 14-line work in the 1843 printing, not an internal section or another version. The selected Gutenberg verse was matched in sequence to OCR of the 1843 scan with no intervening verse rows; numbered boundaries, selected exceptional OCR matches and the page mappings were checked visually. This is OCR-assisted collation, not an assertion that every accent, punctuation mark or witness was visually transcribed or exhaustively collated. Gutenberg says its proofreaders silently corrected some obvious typographical errors while retaining inconsistent historical spelling and accents, so the text is not a diplomatic transcription. Indentation and italics are omitted; stanza breaks are supplied and Gutenberg's `[~e]` and `[~u]` become `ẽ` and `ũ`. The final line of XLIII is in Spanish and is marked as such in the reader. Page anchors in Gutenberg precede each printed page, while the scan links target its PDF leaf.
  - A scan-based correction is explicit: on [1843 p. 25](https://archive.org/download/obrascompletasd00montgoog/obrascompletasd00montgoog.pdf#page=104), XLIX reads `Ah dura lei`, not Gutenberg's `Ali dura lei`; Verso restores `Ah`. On [p. 12](https://archive.org/download/obrascompletasd00montgoog/obrascompletasd00montgoog.pdf#page=91), the scan appears to number *Chara minha inimiga* `XXXIII` between XXII and XXIV; Gutenberg's `XXIII` is retained and the apparent misnumber disclosed. The 1595 scan gives *Pellos estremos raros* ([fol. 12](https://archive.org/download/rhythmas/cam-10-p_0000_capa-capa_t24-C-R0150_text.pdf#page=47)) where 1843 begins *Por os raros extremos*; it gives *Tempo he ja que minha confiança* ([fol. 14](https://archive.org/download/rhythmas/cam-10-p_0000_capa-capa_t24-C-R0150_text.pdf#page=51)) where 1843 begins *Ja he tempo, ja* and Azevedo Filho lists *Rezão é já*. The 1595 *Se algũa hora* ([fol. 13](https://archive.org/download/rhythmas/cam-10-p_0000_capa-capa_t24-C-R0150_text.pdf#page=49)) differs from 1843's *Se somente hora alguma*. Azevedo Filho's list has *Ficou-se o coração*, while 1843 has *Fiou-se*; Perugi's critical comparison identifies the latter as the same poem. Modernized or alternate display titles aid discovery without replacing the 1843 texts. The examples are not an exhaustive variant census.
  - [The importer](../scripts/import-camoes-sonnets-1843.py) pins the selection, pages, source hashes, disclosed correction and extracted readings. Save the [Gutenberg ISO-8859-1 plain text](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/31509/31509-8.txt) as `gutenberg-31509-latin1.txt`, the [1843 PDF](https://archive.org/download/obrascompletasd00montgoog/obrascompletasd00montgoog.pdf) as `obras-1843-vol2.pdf`, the [1595 PDF](https://archive.org/download/rhythmas/cam-10-p_0000_capa-capa_t24-C-R0150_text.pdf) as `rhythmas-1595.pdf`, and the linked study PDF as `azevedo-filho-48.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-camoes-sonnets-1843.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The 1595 and scholarly evidence inform selection and notes, not silently substituted verse. Portugal is an association with the poet, not an assertion of each poem's composition place or date. The Jacob sonnet has a biblical subject but is not classified as devotional solely for that reason.
- Seventy-four Spanish originals by Gustavo A. Bécquer follow the *Rimas* in the 1912 Madrid [*Obras escogidas*](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/53552), as transcribed by Project Gutenberg from the University of Toronto's [scanned copy](https://archive.org/details/obrasescogidas00bcqu). Gutenberg credits Josep Cols Canals, Ramon Pajares Box and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team; it marks the eBook public domain in the United States. Check local law before reuse. The historical poems and pre-1931 edition are also public domain in the United States. No translations are supplied.
  - The 1912 collection separately numbers 76 works. Its VII and LXXIII correspond to Verso's existing `castellana-095` and `castellana-096`, which retain their prior anthology texts, sources and URLs instead of importing duplicate variants. The 1912 VII has `arrancarlas` where the existing text has `arrancarla`; LXXIII has `duelo` where the existing text has `miedo`. The 74 additions use this edition's Roman numbering, not an assertion that every edition uses the same order. Reader titles combine that number with the opening line.
  - Gutenberg's transcription note says it silently corrected obvious printing errors and normalized inconsistent spellings; the resulting text is not claimed to be diplomatic. The selected lines were compared in order against OCR from the 1912 PDF, and exceptional OCR matches, dotted breaks and the three voice headings in LXXII were visually checked against the scan. Reverse comparison found no unmatched verse rows except OCR fragments of already represented lines. This is OCR-assisted collation, not a claim of visual re-transcription of every page or of agreement in every accent and punctuation mark. Indentation and italics are omitted, dialogue dashes normalized, and III's printed separator is represented by a blank line. Page turns and numbered boundaries establish completeness in this printing, not every manuscript or edition.
  - XXIX, XL, LXXI, LXXII and LXXVI contain printed rows of dots; those same breaks appear in the [1885 *Obras*, vol. III](https://archive.org/details/obrasdegustavoab03bc), although their significance and whether other witnesses differ are not established here. LXXII's three voice headings and its final two stanzas are retained as one numbered work, not counted separately. XXIX reads `entrambos` in 1912, versus `ambos` on [1885 p. 167](https://archive.org/download/obrasdegustavoab03bc/obrasdegustavoab03bc.pdf#page=173); the editions are not blended. These comparisons are not an exhaustive variant census. The PDF scans are linked, not republished, and the scan link on each reader opens the first page of the recorded range.
  - The [importer](../scripts/import-becquer-rimas-1912.py) pins source hashes, printed page ranges, numbering and extracted texts. To reproduce or check, save the [Gutenberg plain text](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/53552/53552-0.txt) as `gutenberg-53552.txt`, the [1912 PDF](https://archive.org/download/obrasescogidas00bcqu/obrasescogidas00bcqu.pdf) as `obrasescogidas-1912.pdf`, and the [1885 PDF](https://archive.org/download/obrasdegustavoab03bc/obrasdegustavoab03bc.pdf) as `becquer-obras-1885-vol3.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-becquer-rimas-1912.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The 1885 comparison informs notes only. Spain is an association with the poet, not a claim of each poem's composition place; exact composition dates are not asserted.
- Eleven Portuguese originals by the Brazilian writer and teacher Maria Firmina dos Reis are transcribed from two historical printings, without borrowing modern editorial text or translations. Two separately titled works, *Por ver-te* and *Minha vida*, occur under `M. Firmina dos Reis` in the [1861 *Parnaso maranhense*](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/documentos?id=220537), pp. 222–225 ([scan](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/midias/stream?id=147845&raw=1)); the next author's heading follows. Nine others follow the [1871 *Cantos à beira-mar*](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/documentos?id=195841) ([scan](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/midias/stream?id=155427&raw=1)), whose title page names Maria F. dos Reis and bears the date 1871. UFSC hosts a scan of the Maranhão public library copy; the two works from the earlier anthology are not counted as versions of the nine *Cantos* poems.
