  - The *Cantos* PDF skips printed pp. 1–2, 17–24 and 50–103; other gaps may exist. Its page offsets are not constant. Each selected text, page turn and following printed boundary was reviewed individually, using the explicit printed-page/PDF-leaf pairs in [the importer](../scripts/import-firmina-historical.py). None crosses a missing range. The opening *Uma lágrima*, whose beginning is absent in this PDF, and works interrupted by missing leaves are excluded. Completeness means the boundaries in the cited printing, not every witness. Separate dedications, the two `Guimarães...` markers in *Parnaso*, and the *Cuman* footnote `Praias de Guimarães` are described in context rather than treated as verse or proof of composition location. Ink obscures part of `tristesa` in the 1861 *Minha vida*; that reading is provisional and disclosed.
  - Historic spelling is retained, while indentation, spacing and some punctuation and accent placement are normalized. Modernized display titles support discovery without replacing source titles. The author and pre-1931 printings are public domain in the United States; check local law. Scans and comparison transcriptions are linked rather than redistributed. The [Memorial's biographical account](https://mariafirmina.org.br/novos-dados-biograficos-e-novos-documentos/) cites baptismal and 1847 documents for 1822; [UFSC's catalogue](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/autores?id=7077) gives 1825. Both are disclosed instead of silently picking one. A [2024 scholarly account](https://www.letras.ufmg.br/literafro/arquivos/3_Apresentao_Cantos__beira_mar_de_Maria_Firmina_autoria_de_Luciana_Diogo_-_Copia.pdf) warns that earlier editions can omit stanzas or exchange passages between poems; comparison editions are not assumed equivalent to the cited printings.
  - Material differences are identified rather than blended: *Uma tarde no Cuman* prints `phrases`, while the [2024 publisher excerpt](https://circulodepoemas.com.br/produto/cantos-a-beira-mar-e-outros-poemas) and [UFSC's modernized transcription](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/midias/stream?id=117651&raw=1) give `fases`; *Supplica* has `sem gozos` where a [2024 Biblioteca Pública do Paraná transcription](https://www.bpp.pr.gov.br/Candido/Noticia/POEMAS-Maria-Firmina-dos-Reis) gives `seus gozos`; *Amor* has `revelar-te` where a [2022 UFRGS selection](https://seer.ufrgs.br/index.php/brasilbrazil/article/download/126245/85723/535371) gives `rever-te`. The Memorial's [*Hosana!*](https://mariafirmina.org.br/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/HOSANA_MARIA-FIRMINA-DOS-REIS.pdf) and [*Uns olhos*](https://mariafirmina.org.br/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/UNS-OLHOS_MARIA-FIRMINA-DOS-REIS.pdf) transcriptions identify earlier newspaper appearances and differ from the 1871 texts; those earlier newspaper scans were not independently verified here. Only *Supplica*, *Hosana!* and *Scismar* are tagged Faith based on their texts, without asserting a denomination.
  - [Reviewed readings](../scripts/sources/firmina-historical-readings.txt), source-page mappings and evidence hashes are pinned by the importer. To reproduce, save the linked *Cantos* PDF as `firmina-cantos-source.pdf`, *Parnaso* PDF as `parnaso-maranhense-1861.pdf`, the two Memorial PDFs as `memorial-hosana.pdf` and `memorial-uns-olhos.pdf`, and the UFRGS PDF as `ufrgs-amor-2022.pdf`; then run `python3 scripts/import-firmina-historical.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The comparison PDFs inform notes only and do not supply the generated verse.
- Twenty-two Spanish originals by Sor Juana Inés de la Cruz—eighteen sonnets, one set of redondillas and three separately headed romances—are transcribed from the University of Seville's [1689 *Inundación castálida* scan](https://archive.org/details/A086A273), catalogued there as Madrid: Juan García Infanzón, and marked public domain. The selected works' words, first and last lines, intervening page turns, and following printed boundaries were checked in this copy; completeness is **in that printing**, not a claim about every manuscript or edition. The separately attributed prefatory poems by other writers, dramatic sections, and the component refrains and verses of larger villancico cycles are not counted as Sor Juana originals here. [UNAM SIBILA's contents record](https://sibila.iib.unam.mx/index.php/Detail/Object/Show/object_id/1077) was used for navigation and attribution, not as a source of modern editorial text. The offsets between printed pages and scan leaves differ across the two selected page ranges.
  - The seventeenth-century works and printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The transcriptions normalize long `s`, `u/v` and `i/j` type, expand abbreviations, regularize capitalization, spacing, accents and punctuation, and insert editorial stanza breaks. Some historical spelling is retained and some normalized. Reader titles use first lines; a few explicitly identified modernized display titles aid discovery. No modern translation is reproduced.
  - The [1901 anthology scan](https://archive.org/details/poesasescogida00juan/page/n97/mode/1up) prints `Son falsos silogismos` where the 1689 portrait sonnet has `con falsos sylogismos`; on its [p. 95](https://archive.org/details/poesasescogida00juan/page/n98/mode/1up), `muchas` differs from the 1689 `muchos` in *Fabio, en el ser de todos adoradas*. [Project Gutenberg's transcription of the 1873 *Obras selectas*](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/74087) was consulted for additional comparisons; Gutenberg credits Chuck Greif and the Online Distributed Proofreading Team. In *Hombres necios*, the 1689 print has `obre bien`, while the Gutenberg 1873 transcription has `obren bien`. Notable differences in *Feliciano*, *Miró Celia*, *Lucrecia*, *Pompeyo*, *Porcia* and *Vesme, Alcino* are disclosed on those records, not silently imported. The 1689 *En perseguirme* repeats `entendimiento` in lines 7–8; a [later teaching edition](https://college.holycross.edu/faculty/cstone/span312/sorjuanapoemas.html) uses `pensamiento`. These examples are not an exhaustive collation of all witnesses or a claim that a later reading is authorial.
  - The [Centro de Estudios de Filosofía Mexicana](https://filosofiamexicana.org/2025/04/17/sor-juana-ines-de-la-cruz-la-filosofa-a-330-anos-de-su-muerte/) describes the competing 1648 baptismal evidence and 1651 birth date from an early biography; the dates are presented as disputed. Mexico is a poet association, not an asserted place of composition. Although Sor Juana was a Catholic nun, only the four selections whose texts explicitly address the Incarnation, Joseph, Peter or Christ receive a Faith tag; the secular poems are not classified as devotional by authorship alone.
  - The independently prepared [1689 readings](../scripts/sources/sor-juana-1689-readings.txt), page selections and evidence hashes are pinned by [the importer](../scripts/import-sor-juana-1689.py). To reproduce them, download the [1689 PDF](https://archive.org/download/A086A273/A086A273.pdf) as `sor-juana-inundacion-sevilla-1689.pdf`, the [1901 PDF](https://archive.org/download/poesasescogida00juan/poesasescogida00juan.pdf) as `poesias-escogidas-1901.pdf`, and the [Gutenberg plain text](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/74087/pg74087.txt) as `gutenberg-74087.txt`, then run `python3 scripts/import-sor-juana-1689.py <evidence-directory> --check`. The scans and Gutenberg comparison are linked, not redistributed; the generated verses use the 1689 printing, not a blend of editions.
- Eighteen separately titled French poems by May Ziadé follow the scan-backed [Wikisource transcription of *Fleurs de rêve*](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Livre:Isis_Copia_-_Fleurs_de_r%C3%AAve,_1911.pdf). The printed title page names Isis Copia; the [Academy of American Poets](https://poets.org/poet/may-ziadeh) identifies that name as Ziadé's pseudonym and dates the French collection to 1911. Wikisource also dates the scanned edition to 1911; its preface is dated Cairo, 1 March 1911, and its last leaf identifies Boehme & Anderer, Cairo. A [French biographical account](http://www.rdl.com.lb/1999/3709/art2.html) instead gives 1910 for the collection; the cited preface and edition catalogue are the basis for the 1911 label here, not proof that no earlier version exists. Each work's verse, stanza layout and printed beginning and following boundary were checked in this copy. Completeness refers to this printing and does not establish the limits of every possible version.
  - The historical works and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The adapted Wikisource page transcriptions are credited to their contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). Each reader source points to the first pinned page revision; the printed range shows when to follow the next page and its history for the other contributors. Page revisions fix the transcription, not necessarily subsequent changes to the scan. Indentation, spacing, capital accents and some punctuation are normalized; no modern translation is reproduced.
  - *À Pauline* retains the print's apparent mixed-type `n’oubIie` and `pourqoui`, corrected on Wikisource to `n’oublie` and `pourquoi`. *Automne* restores the printed `Et des cœurs` where the Wikisource snapshot reads `Et de cœurs`; *Balance-toi !* retains the printed `dance`, corrected there to `danse`, and restores the accent on `à l’entour`. *Superstition*'s separately printed, unattributed proverbial motto is described in context rather than incorporated into the verse; *À mon cousin*'s subtitle is likewise recorded. *Fragment*, *Les oiseaux du Nil* and *Au moukattam* are deferred because fragmentary or dotted passages leave completeness unresolved; *Qui conduit la lune ?* is explicitly marked as translated from modern Greek and is not counted as a French original.
  - [BnF's authority record](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb11961296r) identifies Ziadé with Lebanon, gives Nazareth in Palestine as her birthplace and records residence in Egypt; the Academy also describes her Lebanese family and schooling. These are associations with the poet or publication, not claims about a poem's composition place. [Carmen Boustani's 1990 study](https://www.persee.fr/doc/grif_0770-6081_1990_num_43_1_1836) describes her birthplace differently, so the records do not assert a specific birthplace. Composition dates are not established. Faith tags follow the selected poems' prayer or explicit religious content, not an inference about denomination.
  - The [review manifest](../scripts/sources/may-ziade-fleurs-reviewed.json) preserves the page wikitext, revision IDs and hashes, the PDF hash, page ranges, scan-based corrections and stanza lengths. To reproduce or check the records, download the [Commons PDF](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/63/Isis_Copia_-_Fleurs_de_r%C3%AAve%2C_1911.pdf) as `fleurs-de-reve-1911.pdf` and run `python3 scripts/import-may-ziade-fleurs.py <reviewed-scan-directory> --check`. The scan is linked, not redistributed.
- Eleven separately titled poems by Brazilian poet Narcisa Amália follow the historical [*Nebulosas* scan hosted by UFSC](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/midias/stream?id=153383&raw=1). The title leaf identifies Garnier as publisher but has no date; the [UFSC catalogue](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/documentos?id=133827) identifies this copy as the 1872 first edition. Each poem's words, beginning, ending and stanza layout were checked in that scan and the [Internet Archive's different physical copy](https://archive.org/download/Nebulosas/Nebulosas.pdf). The copies share typesetting and are not independent textual witnesses; completeness means complete **in this printing**, not in every possible version. The catalogue's separately labelled Archive link points to a different book in a multi-book item; the `Nebulosas.pdf` link here is the checked copy. The historical works and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The scans are linked, not reproduced, and no modern TEI transcription or translation is reproduced.
  - The verse retains historical spelling; indentation, spacing and some punctuation are normalized. The apparently separated `volta rei` in *Afflicta* is retained and disclosed rather than silently changed. *Sadness* is a Portuguese poem despite its English title. *O Africano e o Poeta* gives voice to an enslaved African and a poet; its historical language and perspective are retained. Separately attributed epigraphs are omitted and their printed attributions are identified in each poem's context. *Consolação* is a response attributed in the printing to J. Ezequiel Freire, and *Os dous trophéos* is by Victor Hugo; neither is counted as Amália's original. *Fragmentos* and the dotted-gap *Desalento* are deferred because completeness is unresolved. The book's [endnote to *Recordação*](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/midias/stream?id=153383&raw=1#page=204) calls it her first poetic production and identifies its dedicatee as a childhood companion.
  - UFSC's [author record](https://literaturabrasileira.ufsc.br/autores/?id=6909) and a [review of Anna Faedrich's 2020 study](https://www.convergencialusiada.com.br/rcl/article/view/470) give 1852–1924; [EIS Editora's biographical page](https://eiseditora.com.br/narcisa-amalia/) instead gives 1856, although that page also describes her as 28 in 1880. The differing birth years are disclosed rather than resolved here. Brazil is an association with the poet, not a claim that each poem was composed there; composition dates are not asserted. The Faith tag on *Voto* reflects its explicit prayer, not an inferred denomination.
  - The [review manifest](../scripts/sources/narcisa-nebulosas-reviewed.json) pins both PDF hashes, printed and PDF-page ranges, transcribed readings, and stanza lengths. Check the generated records with `python3 scripts/import-narcisa-nebulosas.py <reviewed-scan-directory> --check`, supplying the two named PDF files from the links above. The two scan-page offsets change within the books, so the manifest records them explicitly rather than deriving them from the table of contents.
- Eleven complete Spanish poems attributed to Teresa de Jesús (Teresa of Ávila) follow the poetry section of Vicente de la Fuente’s [*Escritos de Santa Teresa*, volume I](https://bibliotecavirtualmadrid.comunidad.madrid/bvmadrid_publicacion/es/bib/3810.do), whose title leaf says 1877. Each original’s words, printed beginning, and ending were checked against that edition; this establishes completeness in the named printing, not an authoritative boundary for every manuscript. The Madrid library’s image labels are offset by one page in this section: image position 557 starts the poetry on printed p. 509. A second [Toronto scan](https://archive.org/details/bibliotecadeauto53madruoft) also has 1877 on its title leaf, despite a conflicting Commons description; the compared poetry pages share the Madrid copy’s typesetting and are not independent manuscript witnesses. The works and 1877 edition are public domain in the United States; check local law. Source transcriptions, scan hashes and stanza boundaries appear in the [review manifest](../scripts/sources/teresa-1877-reviewed.json); spacing and punctuation are normalized, and each first line supplies its title. Modern scholarly commentary is not republished.
  - Teresa’s poetic attributions and textual boundaries are complex: [Monte Carmelo’s discussion](https://teresavila.com/poesias/) describes manuscript loss and overlap with other Carmelites. Fuente’s own introduction differentiates certain, probable and doubtful attributions. His incomplete III and XVIII, the doubtful XI and XIX–XXIII, and the incomplete XXVIII are excluded; II is another overlapping glosa, not counted as another work here. His I is the longer version printed in 1877, not silently combined with shorter modern versions. In VI the first refrain’s printed `pura mí` is retained and disclosed, while its later refrains have `para mí`. Separate Latin epigraphs are not included as original Spanish verse. For XXV, Fuente says the last stanza is absent from a Madrid manuscript copy but present in the Guadalajara copy. Poem XVI’s historical heading dates the occasion to 1571, but exact composition dates and places are not asserted.
  - Seven English translations were independently transcribed and checked against the [1913 *Minor Works of St. Teresa*](https://archive.org/details/minorworksofstte00tere), translated by the Benedictines of Stanbrook and revised by Benedict Zimmerman. The translations are public domain in the United States as 1913 publications; check local law. Their stanza boundaries match the selected 1877 originals, though these poetic renderings may differ in wording. The book’s full translation of I extends through pp. 6–10; the [Academy of American Poets’ online selection](https://poets.org/poem/souls-desire) reproduces only an excerpt. No modern translation is reproduced.
  - To check the generated records, run `python3 scripts/import-teresa-1877.py <reviewed-scan-directory> --check` with the files and hashes listed in the manifest. The English file is the [Internet Archive PDF](https://archive.org/download/minorworksofstte00tere/minorworksofstte00tere.pdf). Madrid image positions are printed page + 48; the reviewed JPEG object ID is position + 11178537, obtainable via `https://bibliotecavirtualmadrid.comunidad.madrid/bvmadrid_publicacion/i18n/media/object-view.do?id=<ID>&recordDownload=0`. The Toronto JPEGs are Wikimedia Commons thumbnails of the [DjVu](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Biblioteca_de_Autores_Espa%C3%B1oles_(Vol._53).djvu): image number is printed page + 46, requested with `iiurlparam=page<N>-1400px` and `iiurlwidth=1400` through the Commons imageinfo API; the title page uses image 9 at 900px. Hashes identify the exact reviewed files and may not match subsequently regenerated thumbnails.
- Twenty-four numbered sonnets traditionally attributed to Louise Labé follow the [1887 Boy edition](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Livre:Lab%C3%A9_-_%C5%92uvres,_t._1-2,_%C3%A9d._Boy,_1887.djvu), pp. 93–107. Sonnet I is Italian; II–XXIIII are French originals, not translations. Each sonnet's fourteen lines and its boundary were checked against that printing; this does not establish the limits or reading of every earlier witness. The poems were published under Labé's name in 1555. Their authorship has been debated: Mireille Huchon proposed a male-authored literary hoax, while [Michèle Clément and Michel Jourde](https://www.france-memoire.fr/qui-etait-louise-labe-2/) challenge that hypothesis using archival and publication evidence. The separately attributed *Escriz de divers poëtes* are not included. The [BnF authority](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb119103702) gives Labé's birth as approximately 1524 and death in 1566. France is a poet association, not a claim about each poem's place of composition.
  - The historical works and 1887 edition are public domain in the United States; check local law. The Wikisource page transcriptions and our adaptations are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); linked scan-page histories identify contributors. The first line supplies each title. Long s and spacing are normalized, stanza breaks are editorial, and punctuation and accents may follow Wikisource rather than the print. Scan-based corrections to its transcription and hashes of both witnesses appear in the [review manifest](../scripts/sources/labe-1887-reviewed.json). A reader's source link starts at the first scan page of that sonnet; the printed page range indicates when it continues onto the next page. The page revision fixes the transcription, not necessarily subsequent changes to the underlying scan. The [1910 Sansot printing](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Livre:Lab%C3%A9_-_%C3%89l%C3%A9gies_et_Sonnets,_Sansot,_1910.djvu) serves only for identified comparisons, not as a source of silently substituted lines: for example, Boy's XIV reads `A l’heur passé` and `tes graces`, versus `A leur passé` and `ces graces` in 1910. Boy's VII retains `par si long temps`, while 1910 has `pas si long tems`. Other material comparisons are disclosed on the affected reader pages. Long s in the 1910 italic can resemble f and is not by itself evidence of a variant. No translations or exact composition dates or places are asserted.
- Fourteen poems by Marceline Desbordes-Valmore follow the [Wikisource scan and transcription of *Les Pleurs*](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Livre:Desbordes-Valmore_-_Les_Pleurs,_1834.djvu). Wikisource identifies the copy as the 1834 Paris Goullet printing, and its [Google Books catalogue](https://books.google.com/books?id=eco5AAAAcAAJ) identifies it as edition 2. This is not the [1833 Charpentier first edition](https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/bpt6k1067280q); the DjVu starts with a half-title and the imprint identification here relies on the catalogues. The historical poems and 1834 edition are public domain in the United States; check local law. Wikisource contributors' transcriptions and our adaptations are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); consult the linked article and scan-page histories for contributors. No translations are supplied.
  - Each selection's separate title, number, verse and printed ending were reviewed against the named scan. These boundaries establish what is complete **in that printing**, not necessarily every textual tradition. Punctuation and accents may follow Wikisource rather than the print; spacing is normalized and stanzas split by page turns are rejoined. Separately printed epigraphs are omitted from Desbordes-Valmore's verse and their printed attributions described in context. Wikisource adds `Hernani ! Doña Sol !` to *La Sincère*'s Hugo epigraph, but those words are absent from the 1834 title leaf. *Détachement* restores the period after `orage`; *Une Ondine* changes the period after `d’amour` to the printed comma. The [review manifest](../scripts/sources/desbordes-valmore-pleurs-1834.json) pins the article revisions, extracted readings, scan and comparison hashes, stanza lengths and corrections. Article permalinks alone do not freeze transcluded scan-page text; scans are linked, not republished.
  - The opening of *La Fiancée polonaise* is partly obscured by ink in the 1834 copy; the separately printed [1831 *Mémorial de la Scarpe*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Le_M%C3%A9morial_de_la_Scarpe,_6e_ann%C3%A9e,_n._097,_1831-08-13_-_p._3_-_La_Fianc%C3%A9e_polonaise.jpg) supports `Qui frappe à l’heure` and `De mon lointain hameau`. The earlier version places a different `Des mânes sentinelles` stanza last, while the 1834 `Des anges sentinelles` appears second; the editions are not blended. *La Crainte* is deferred: the [archived original leaf](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/archive/5/50/20200410054429%21Desbordes-Valmore_-_Les_Pleurs%2C_1834.djvu/page166-1280px-Desbordes-Valmore_-_Les_Pleurs%2C_1834.djvu.jpg) lacks its final quatrain and the [current replacement](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Desbordes-Valmore_-_Les_Pleurs,_1834.djvu/166) is visibly composite, with its source edition not established. *Agar* is marked a fragment, and works credited as imitations or identified as English-derived by [Camille Islert's study](https://doi.org/10.58282/colloques.8662) are excluded from this originals selection. *L’Enfant au rameau* receives a Faith tag based on its blessing and prayer; no denomination is inferred. Exact composition dates and places are not asserted.
- Twelve Gaspara Stampa sonnets follow the [1913 Salza edition](https://it.wikisource.org/wiki/Rime_(Stampa)), checked against its scan and the [1554 Venice printing](https://doi.org/10.3931/e-rara-5318), digitized by Basel University Library. The 1554 PDF is the complete book, not its separately downloadable *Capitoli* subset. Both printings separately present these fourteen-line works; that does not establish every textual tradition. Salza supplied headings and edited and reordered the collection: his numbering is not presented as an authorial or first-edition sequence, and his headings are omitted. [Recent scholarship](https://folia.unifr.ch/unifr/documents/312802) discusses his changes to the wider collection. The [Library of Congress](https://www.loc.gov/item/94030764/) gives Stampa's approximate birth year as 1523; [Lyra](https://lyra.unil.ch/agents/252) gives approximately 1525.
  - The 1913 readings are retained: in II, `quanto ’l sol giri e guardi` differs from the 1554 `quanto ’l Sol miri, e guardi`; in VII, `in sembiante` differs from `in sembianti`; in IX, `e non vorrebbe` differs from `e nol vorrebbe`. V has `son` where 1554 prints `Sono`. These witnesses are not silently combined. The first line supplies each display title. Exact composition dates and places are not asserted, and no translations are supplied.
- Four love sonnets and ten spiritual sonnets by Vittoria Colonna follow the separately numbered sequences of the [1760 Bergamo *Rime*](https://it.wikisource.org/wiki/Rime_(Vittoria_Colonna)), each checked against its scan and following printed boundary. [Lyra, the University of Lausanne's poetry catalogue](https://lyra.unil.ch/agents/3), identifies all ten spiritual incipits and love II and IV as Colonna's; scholarly work identifies [love I](https://folia.unifr.ch/documents/331886/files/4.%20Tarsi%20Poesia%20femminile%20del%20Cinquecento.pdf?download), and [Bullock's 1982 edition](https://archive.org/details/270ColonnaRimeSi077) includes love III. These later sources support attribution, not a license to reproduce their modern editorial text. Colonna's birth year is given as 1490 or 1492 by the [University of Chicago Library](https://www.lib.uchicago.edu/efts/IWW/BIOS/A0011.html). The spiritual poems are tagged Faith on the basis of their texts and printed section, not the poet's identity; the love poems are not tagged devotional and no denomination is assigned.
  - The 1760 print has `ghiaccio` in spiritual IX, correcting Wikisource's `ghìaccio`. Its decorative initials are joined to the opening words; the printed capital letters after the apostrophes in `S’Alla` and `S’In` are retained. Punctuation and accents can follow the transcription rather than the print, spacing is normalized and stanza breaks are editorial. The [study of love I](https://www.italianisti.it/pubblicazioni/atti-di-congresso/i-cantieri-dellitalianistica-ricerca-didattica-e-organizzazione-agli-inizi-del-xxi-secolo-2016/OTTAVIANI.pdf) quotes `per altra tromba`, whereas this 1760 reading is `Per altra lingua`; the versions are not blended. These boundaries apply to the named printing, not necessarily all manuscripts or editions.
  - The historical poems and the 1760 and 1913 editions are public domain in the United States; check local law. The Wikisource transcriptions and adaptations are credited to contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); consult each linked article and scan-page history for contributors. Article revision links alone do not freeze later changes to transcluded scan pages: the [review manifest](../scripts/sources/italian-women-sonnets.json) also pins the extracted readings, corrections, scan leaves, 1554 comparison pages and source-image hashes. Scans are linked, not republished; Basel requests attribution for its digital copy.
- Shakespeare, *Sonnets*: [Project Gutenberg eBook 1041](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/1041), text of the historic sonnet sequence. The eBook's notice asks readers outside the United States to check local law. The importer extracts poem text, not the ebook's license boilerplate.
- José Martí, *Versos sencillos* XXXIX: [Spanish Wikisource](https://es.wikisource.org/wiki/Versos_sencillos/XXXIX).
- Luís de Camões, *Amor he hum fogo*: [Portuguese Wikisource](https://pt.wikisource.org/wiki/Amor_he_hum_fogo_que_arde_sem_se_ver), historical spelling from the linked edition.
- Victor Hugo, *Demain, dès l'aube*: [French Wikisource](https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Les_Contemplations/%C2%AB_Demain,_d%C3%A8s_l%E2%80%99aube,_%C3%A0_l%E2%80%99heure_o%C3%B9_blanchit_la_campagne_%C2%BB).
- Giacomo Leopardi, *L'Infinito*: [Italian Wikisource](https://it.wikisource.org/wiki/Versi_del_conte_Giacomo_Leopardi/L%27Infinito), 1826 edition. The poem [first appeared in *Nuovo Ricoglitore* in 1825](https://www.internetculturale.it/directories/ViaggiNelTesto/leopardi/eng/b13.html).
- Leopardi's [*La ricordanza*](https://it.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Versi_del_conte_Giacomo_Leopardi/La_ricordanza&oldid=3768364) and [*La sera del giorno festivo*](https://it.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=Versi_del_conte_Giacomo_Leopardi/La_sera_del_giorno_festivo&oldid=3768365) follow the 1826 *Versi* edition, preserving its spellings and titles. These transcriptions are credited to Wikisource contributors under CC BY-SA 4.0; consult the linked revision histories for contributors. The early *La ricordanza* differs from the later *Alla luna*.
- Frederick Townsend's English versions of *The Infinite* and *To the Moon* are separately transcribed and credited from [*The Poems of Giacomo Leopardi* (New York, 1887), Gutenberg 19315](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/19315). These translations follow later textual versions, not the exact 1826 originals; *To the Moon* includes lines absent from the earlier text. Gutenberg identifies the edition as public domain in the United States; rights elsewhere may differ.
- Li Bai, *Quiet Night Thought*: [Chinese Wikisource](https://zh.wikisource.org/wiki/%E9%9D%9C%E5%A4%9C%E6%80%9D), *Three Hundred Tang Poems* variant. The seven AI-assisted reading aids are new, unreviewed interpretations, not historical translations; verify them before relying on them.
- Matsuo Bashō, *The Old Pond*: [Japanese Wikisource, 1903 collection](https://ja.wikisource.org/wiki/%E8%8A%AD%E8%95%89%E4%BF%B3%E5%8F%A5%E5%85%A8%E9%9B%86); Lafcadio Hearn's English rendering appears in [*Exotics and Retrospectives*](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/42735/42735-h/42735-h.htm), first published in 1898.
- Bashō's *Summer Grass* and *The Mogami River* follow the main readings of [*Oku no Hosomichi*, Japanese Wikisource revision 247172](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E3%81%8A%E3%81%8F%E3%81%AE%E3%81%BB%E3%81%9D%E9%81%93&oldid=247172), which identifies Miyake's 1911 edition as its source. Compare the [edition's scanned p. 51](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:NDL888916_%E6%96%B0%E9%87%88%E5%A5%A5%E3%81%AE%E7%B4%B0%E9%81%93.pdf?page=31) and [p. 60](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:NDL888916_%E6%96%B0%E9%87%88%E5%A5%A5%E3%81%AE%E7%B4%B0%E9%81%93.pdf?page=36). The inline variant notes are omitted from the verse, but acknowledged in context. Wikisource credits its text contributors under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); its [source notes](https://ja.wikisource.org/wiki/%E3%83%88%E3%83%BC%E3%82%AF:%E3%81%8A%E3%81%8F%E3%81%AE%E3%81%BB%E3%81%9D%E9%81%93) name CES1596 as the transcriber; consult the linked revision history for contributors. [Iwate University](https://chs.iwate-u.ac.jp/heritage/html/literature-sub-natsukusa.html) and [Doshisha Women's College](https://www.dwc.doshisha.ac.jp/research/faculty_column/12912) corroborate the attribution and contexts, including an earlier variant of the river verse.
- Forty further Bashō hokku follow Wikisource's scan-backed [1903 *Bashō Haiku Collection*](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E8%8A%AD%E8%95%89%E4%BF%B3%E5%8F%A5%E5%85%A8%E9%9B%86&oldid=143752), edited by Kōzan Ōtsuka, whose dates (1880–1911) are documented by the [National Diet Library](https://ndlsearch.ndl.go.jp/en/books/R100000002-I000002980052). The originals and the 1903 edition are public domain in the United States; Wikisource transcriptions are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), with each scan page linked at its recorded revision. Consult the page histories for contributors. Ōtsuka warned that his wider anthology could contain misattributions, so this selection is restricted to works also identified as Bashō's in the [Bashō Memorial Association's list of 50 *Oku no Hosomichi* verses](https://www.basho-bp.jp/?page_id=28), and each was compared with the separately transcribed 1911 travelogue above. The association text is a comparison, not the source of the historical reading. Sora's and others' verses, the two already in Verso, and unresolved candidates are excluded. The complete single-line texts were checked against their 1903 scan pages; most pages were only singly proofread on Wikisource.
  - The editions differ: the 1903 *grass-door* verse reads `世ぞ`, while the 1911 transcription gives `世は` as its main reading; the *cicada* verse reads `しみ入る` versus the 1911 main `しみ込`, with a variant noted there. The early-rice verse has `右` in 1903 but `道` in the 1911 transcription. These readings are not silently combined. In the toad verse, the [1903 scan](https://ja.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Basho_Haiku_Zenshu.djvu/77) prints `かひや`, correcting Wikisource's `かいや`. Two printed vertical repetition marks are expanded for horizontal reading: `あか〳〵` → `あかあか` and `きり〴〵` → `きりぎり`. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/basho-oku-reviewed.json) records page revisions, source and comparison readings, attribution checks, edits and output hashes. No translations, exact composition dates or composition places are asserted.
- Forty other Bashō hokku use the same scan-backed 1903 anthology and rights terms. The [Bashō Memorial Association's 43-verse *Nozarashi* journey list](https://www.basho-bp.jp/?page_id=20) independently identifies the selections as Bashō's; it is used for attribution, not as the source of the reproduced historical readings. Each verse was checked against its linked 1903 scan page and compared with the separately transcribed [*Nozarashi Kikō*, Wikisource revision 179298](https://ja.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%E9%87%8E%E3%81%96%E3%82%89%E3%81%97%E7%B4%80%E8%A1%8C&oldid=179298). Wikisource's [bibliographic note](https://ja.wikisource.org/wiki/%E3%83%88%E3%83%BC%E3%82%AF:%E9%87%8E%E3%81%96%E3%82%89%E3%81%97%E7%B4%80%E8%A1%8C) identifies the comparison as a digital transcription of 贄川他石's 1929 edition; the comparison was not used to silently rewrite the 1903 text. The 1903 anthology's scan pages may have only one proofreader.
  - Readings differ: the roadside verse has `道ばた` in 1903 versus `道の辺` in the 1929 transcription; the water-drawing verse has `こもりの僧` versus `氷の僧`. The spring-haze verse ends `朝霞` in 1903 and `朝がすみ` in the 1929 transcription, but the association lists `薄霞`. The 1903 cherry verse has `ふたつ中に活けたる` versus `ふたつの中に活たる` in the 1929 transcription. Other material differences are noted with each work. One vertical repetition mark, `とく〳〵`, is expanded to `とくとく` for horizontal reading; headings, headnotes, ruby and indentation are omitted. The association's nos. 10, 18 and 25 are not imported because their 1903 matches have unresolved differences. His travelling companion Chiri's verses are not attributed to Bashō. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/basho-nozarashi-reviewed.json) pins the source-page revisions, 1903 and 1929 readings, attribution hashes, editing note and output hash. No translations, exact composition dates or places are supplied.
- Yosa Buson's *Rapeseed Flowers* and *The Spring Sea* are transcribed from [Ōno Shachiku, *Yosa Buson* (1897), pp. 132–133, scan 75](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/pid/875927/1/75). In the spring-sea verse, the vertical repetition mark after のたり is expanded to のたり; otherwise the edition's wording is retained. The [National Diet Library's IIIF manifest](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/875927/manifest.json) marks the scan PDM; the 1897 edition is public domain in the United States. [Yoshikai's account](https://yaenk.sakura.ne.jp/archives/kotenkouza/3165) and a [Japanese government article](https://www.gov-online.go.jp/eng/publicity/book/hlj/html/202207/202207_12_jp.html) independently corroborate the verses' attribution; they are cited as context, not as permission to reproduce their modern text. A [library reference](https://crd.ndl.go.jp/reference/detail?page=ref_view&id=1000162094) describes competing evidence for the spring-sea location, which is not asserted here. Buson's death year is rendered 1783 or 1784 in different sources and calendars; the [Kyoto National Museum](https://knmdb.kyohaku.go.jp/eng/20928.html) gives the traditional date and 1784. The four English display titles are editorial labels, not full translations; no translations are supplied for these additions.
- Thirty-two further Buson hokku are manually transcribed from [Ozawa Takeji's *Buson Haiku Collection* (1926)](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/pid/1019573), with each work linked to its scan spread and printed page. The [National Diet Library's IIIF manifest](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/1019573/manifest.json) marks the material PDM; the pre-1931 edition is public domain in the United States, while local terms can differ. Each complete one-line verse was read against its scan and compared with the separately published [Kigosai association's Buson list](https://kigosai.sub.jp/bs/?page_id=25582). Kigosai does not identify the edition underlying that list, so it supports the attribution and comparison but is not claimed as an independent historical edition. The 1926 reading is retained: for example, `寢`, `遲`, `內` and `歸` correspond to the comparison's `寝`, `遅`, `内` and `帰`; `ものかたり` corresponds to its `ものがたり`. These differences are noted on the affected reader pages. The [review manifest](../scripts/sources/buson-1926-reviewed.json) records the printed page, scan-image hashes and comparison readings. Printed subject headings are not presented as poem titles, and no translations, exact composition dates or places are asserted. The two existing Buson works remain unchanged and are not imported again.
- Thirty-nine hokku attributed to Kobayashi Issa are manually read from [Ōtsuka Kōzan's *Issa Haiku Collection* (1902)](https://ndlsearch.ndl.go.jp/en/books/R100000002-I000000504315), whose [National Diet Library manifest](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/874848/manifest.json) marks the digitized copy PDM. The originals and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. Each selection is separately printed in the anthology and linked to its spread and printed page; those boundaries do not establish the history of every linked-verse or manuscript context. Printed seasonal headings and headnotes are not treated as titles. The single-line texts use normalized character forms (for example `寝`, `銭`, `来`, `実` and `蔵`) and expand printed repetition marks for horizontal reading, rather than claiming a fully diplomatic transcription. The first line supplies the title. No translations, composition dates, places or religious classifications are supplied.
  - Each attribution is also checked against [David Lanoue's Issa archive](https://haikuguy.com/issa/), linked per poem. His site says its translations are based on the 1976–79 *Issa zenshū*, but the textual relationship between that collected edition and the 1902 anthology is not established; this is independent corroboration, not a claim of independently descended manuscripts. His English translations are rights reserved and are **not** reproduced. The comparison's modern reading is not substituted for the printing: for example, 1902 has `頭` where the comparison has `首`, `寺へ` where it has `寺に`, and `出したり` where it has `出しけり`; other material differences are disclosed on the reader pages. Ambiguous possible matches are deferred. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/issa-1902-reviewed.json) pins the scan spreads, printed pages, comparison snapshot and matched-reading hashes; modern archive pages can change and scans are linked, not republished. Ōtsuka cautioned about possible misattributions in his separate Bashō anthology, so the cross-check matters even though that warning does not establish an error in this Issa selection. [Japan Search](https://jpsearch.go.jp/gallery/ndl-OjV3NzqKO3Q) gives Issa's death year as 1827 under the old calendar; the [Issa archive](https://haikuguy.com/issa/aboutissa.html) explains the corresponding Western date in January 1828. Japan is a poet association, not an asserted composition place.
- Seventy-three hokku attributed to Kaga no Chiyo, also known as Chiyo-ni, are manually read from the *Chiyo-ni kushū* section of [*Nihon Haisho Taikei*, vol. 9 (1927)](https://ndlsearch.ndl.go.jp/en/books/R100000002-I000000779464), edited by Katsumine Shinpū. Its title page credits Kihaku (`既白`) as compiler of the underlying collection; the [Chiyojo Haiku Museum](https://www.hakusan-museum.jp/chiyojohaiku/about/) dates Kihaku's collection to 1764. The 1927 volume's exact copy-text and manuscript descent are not established here. The [National Diet Library manifest](https://dl.ndl.go.jp/api/iiif/1179392/manifest.json) marks the scan PDM; the originals and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States, while local terms may differ. Each work is separately printed under the collection's `乾` or `坤` heading and linked to its printed page and spread. Other poets' separately attributed prefatory verses are excluded. Printed boundaries establish what this anthology presents, not every earlier linked-verse or manuscript context.
  - The museum's searchable catalogue, reached from its HTML page, was used to check each corresponding attribution and its cited source; 25 selected rows also mention an autograph, which was not separately inspected. Because the museum cites *Chiyo-ni kushū* and draws on later scholarship, this is not a claim of independent manuscript descent. Its modern commentary and workbook are not republished, and its [site terms](https://www.hakusan-museum.jp/about-hp/) restrict republication and direct links to non-HTML files. Texts follow the 1927 scan rather than substituting the museum's modern readings. For example, the scan has `静なり` and `日の暮る` where the museum gives `静かなり` and `日の暮るゝ`; material differences appear on the reader pages. The printing's `真如平等` and `三界唯心` headnotes are identified in context for the two affected poems. The celebrated morning-glory/well-bucket verse is deferred because a printed glyph remains uncertain. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/chiyo-ni-1927-reviewed.json) pins six scan spreads, the private comparison snapshot and the selected museum rows. Some historical character forms are normalized and printed repetition marks adapted or expanded for horizontal reading; seasonal headings and other headnotes are not presented as titles. No translations, exact composition dates, composition places or religious classifications are asserted. The [NDL authority](https://id.ndl.go.jp/auth/ndlna/00272237) gives the poet's dates as 1703–1775; Japan is an author association, not a composition-place claim.
- *Let the Days Do as They Will*: traditionally attributed to Al-Shāfiʿī. The [Arabic Wikisource transcription, revision 456958](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=%D8%AF%D8%B9_%D8%A7%D9%84%D8%A3%D9%8A%D8%A7%D9%85_%D8%AA%D9%81%D8%B9%D9%84_%D9%85%D8%A7_%D8%AA%D8%B4%D8%A7%D8%A1&oldid=456958) does **not** identify its source edition. It is credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/); its attribution and textual history need further research. Consult the linked revision history for contributors and check the applicable terms before reuse.
- Eight works by the Tunisian poet Maḥmūd Qābāḍū follow volume 1 of his *Dīwān*, compiled by Muḥammad al-Sanūsī. The [Bavarian State Library's IIIF manifest](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/presentation/v2/bsb11368507/manifest) identifies the poet and compiler, volume, call number `A.or. 1173-1/3`, and the catalogue date `1294/1878`; 1294 AH spans 1877–78. [NYU's catalogue](https://sites.dlib.nyu.edu/viewer/books/aub_aco002915/display?lang=en) gives the poet's dates as approximately 1815–1871, while the [Tunisian National Library](https://kitab.bnt.nat.tn/ar/notices/?periode=17) gives 1814–1871. The Tunisian library's scanned copy was also consulted, but its catalogue and PDF were not reliably accessible over HTTPS in a browser. The two library copies share the same typesetting, not two independent editions. The historical originals and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The Bavarian library marks its digital images [NoC-NC](https://rightsstatements.org/vocab/NoC-NC/1.0/); they are linked, not republished here. Consult the library's conditions before reusing its images.
  - Each work's printed heading, complete text and following boundary were checked against the two scans, on [pp. 22](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00155/full/full/0/default.jpg)–[23](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00154/full/full/0/default.jpg) and [50](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00127/full/full/0/default.jpg)–[51](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00126/full/full/0/default.jpg). Separately presented works include a three-couplet birth chronogram and a two-couplet seal inscription; dates printed under them are occasion annotations, not independently verified composition dates. The [p. 23 heading](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00154/full/full/0/default.jpg) says the couscous satire builds on a half-line by another writer, without naming the writer or specifying the half-line. These boundaries establish what this printing presents, not completeness in every tradition. The preceding `أهي الجبال رست بها الغبراء` is excluded: on [p. 21](https://api.digitale-sammlungen.de/iiif/image/v2/bsb11368507_00156/full/full/0/default.jpg) the editor says he found only these verses in drafts, and the text skips to another passage.
  - Each candidate was also compared with a separately presented online [al-Dīwān](https://www.aldiwan.net/cat-poet-Mahmoud-Qabadu) transcription; its underlying edition and textual relationship to the printing are not established. In the toothache poem, the print has `عناء` and `شفاء`, versus `عنائي` and `شفائي` online. The illness poem continues from p. 22 to p. 23 and prints `للاستعفاء`, where the online text has `بلا استعفاء`. The print's `أهناها` and `أملاها` correspond to the online `أهنؤها` and `أملؤها`. These readings are not silently combined. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/qabadu-tunis-reviewed.json) pins the Tunisian PDF, five Bavarian page images, page mappings, comparison links, counts and text hashes; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/qabadu-tunis-reviewed.txt) records the scan-based readings. Vowel marks and decorative dividers are omitted, spacing and some spelling normalized, and printed half-lines placed on separate lines. Tunisia is an author association, not an asserted composition place. No translations, exact composition dates or religious classifications are supplied.
- Six originals by Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-Maʿarrī are transcribed from the [Hindiyya *Saqṭ al-zand*](https://dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco000324/columbia_aco000324_lo.pdf#page=136); its [title page](https://dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco000324/columbia_aco000324_lo.pdf#page=5) identifies Cairo, 1901 / 1319 AH. We also consulted the [higher-resolution export of the same scanned copy](https://dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco000324/columbia_aco000324_hi.pdf#page=137), SHA-256 `ea9bb53abf45144dfa089e496cc47792226ce8bef6842c0372f78923a287619b`, to check fine marks. The historical originals and the 1901 printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. The [Academy of American Poets](https://poets.org/poet/al-maarri) gives the poet's dates as 973–1057; the [*Encyclopedia of World Biography*](https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/abu-l-ala-al-maarri) gives 1058 as the death year. Syria is associated with the poet, not asserted as a composition place. No translation, exact composition date or religious classification is supplied.
  - The 1901 print presents six works separately, with three, four, ten, eight, fifteen and ten couplets. We checked their headings, every half-line, endings and following headings against the [1869 Bulāq *Sharḥ al-tanwīr*](https://archive.org/download/sharhaltanwirala00abaluoft/sharhaltanwirala00abaluoft.pdf#page=97) and the manuscript-based [1948 *Shurūḥ Saqṭ al-zand*, section 4](https://dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco001465/columbia_aco001465_lo.pdf#page=275), where they are numbered 70–75. Its [title page](https://dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/columbia_aco001465/columbia_aco001465_lo.pdf#page=5) gives 1948. Ṣalāḥ Kazzāra's [bibliographic study](https://arabacademy-sy.org/uploads/magazine/mag77/mag77-3-6.pdf#page=5) states that the 1901 edition derives from the Bulāq commentary with the commentary removed, and [describes](https://arabacademy-sy.org/uploads/magazine/mag77/mag77-3-6.pdf#page=6) the later critical edition's manuscript sources. The 1869 and 1901 printings are therefore not independent witnesses. Matching boundaries apply to these printings, not necessarily every tradition; the 1948 edition is linked for comparison, not reproduced or presumed free of later editorial rights.
  - Kazzāra reports that a different 1884 Beirut edition omitted the candle poem's last two couplets and the mother elegy entirely; we have not independently examined that printing. The 1948 apparatus reports that al-Baṭalyawsī omitted the sixth couplet of poem 74 and did not include poem 75 at all; the composite edition and the two early printings have fifteen and ten respectively. The farewell and armor poem's headnotes place them in other voices, not necessarily autobiographical ones. The 1901 mother elegy appears to print `الشأم`, while the 1948 edition prints `الشام`; the earlier scan's small mark is uncertain.
  - The 1948 apparatus reports `عن` for the farewell's `من` in al-Khwārazmī's commentary, and `على غير الأيام` for the candle's `على نوب الأيام` in al-Baṭalyawsī's. For poem 73 it records `تفتش`/`تكشف`, `بجدة`/`نجدة`, and `وأهلها`/`وإنسها`. The 1901 and Bulāq texts of poem 74 read `الشعر` and `أحيا` where the 1948 main text has `الجزء` and `أعيا`; its apparatus also records `كالنوء`/`كالنجم`, `رسولا`/`سؤالا`, `وظل`/`فظل`, `طال`/`مد`, and `يجاورني`/`تجاوزني`. These alternatives do not replace the 1901 readings.
  - Vowel marks and ornamental dividers are omitted, spelling and spacing normalized, and half-lines printed separately; first half-lines serve as titles. In the armor poem, the two occurrences of `الكر` have different printed vowel marks. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/al-maarri-1901-reviewed.json) pins the three importer scans, source and comparison pages, counts and text hashes; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/al-maarri-1901-reviewed.txt) records the historical readings. The scans are linked, not republished.
- Malak Hifni Nasif's `يا هذه لا تعذلي` follows the [University of Chicago *Al-Nisāʾiyyāt* scan](https://archive.org/details/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi), whose catalogue dates it 1328 AH/[1910]. The scanned copy appears to lack its title page, so that date is a catalogue identification, not a scanned imprint. A [contemporary *al-Muqtabas* review](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-224415/224415%20-%20Al%20Muqtabas_issue-5_Year-1910.pdf#page=493) describes a 1328 AH, 176-page book of the same title and author, matching this copy; the [opening of that part](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-224415/224415%20-%20Al%20Muqtabas_issue-5_Year-1910.pdf#page=445) identifies volume 5, part 7 but does not show a Gregorian date. [Wikisource](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?curid=122036&oldid=282338) dates the issue 1 August 1910. The [Women and Memory Forum](https://wmf.org.eg/en/publication/al-nisaiyyat/) identifies Nasif as an Egyptian writer, 1886–1918, and distinguishes the 1910 and expanded 1925 printings. Egypt is a poet association, not an asserted composition place. The original and pre-1931 printing are public domain in the United States; local terms may differ.
  - The [printed introduction](https://archive.org/download/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi.pdf#page=155) quotes Shawqi and another writer before Nasif's response begins on p. 144; those excerpts are not attributed to her here. Nasif's response occupies pp. 144–146 and ends before a [new section](https://archive.org/download/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi.pdf#page=159) on p. 147. The [later comparison copy](https://archive.org/details/AAlexandrina-140749/page/n153/mode/1up) separately presents the same 60-couplet boundaries. Its archive metadata says `[1910]`, but the copy contains post-1918 material; its date and relationship to the earlier printing are not established. Matching boundaries support completeness **in those printings**, not independent textual descent or completeness in every tradition. No translation, exact composition date or religious classification is supplied.
  - A compact form in the earlier scan could be mistaken for `خومل`; we read it provisionally as `فحومل`, comparing the similar printed form in `فحنا` and the online transcriptions. The scan appears to have `بتبذل` where [Wikisource](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?curid=92957&oldid=465123), [Qafiyah](https://qafiyah.com/poems/jFwz) and the later printed copy have `بتبدل`. The earlier `فحيهل` differs from the online `فحيهلي`. The print's `خلاخل` is retained; an [errata table](https://archive.org/download/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi/alnisaiyatmajmua00nasi.pdf#page=193) proposes `خلاخلا`, but its page reference may be 114 rather than 144. These readings are provisional, not silently harmonized. The scan has `باريس` where the online transcription has `باريز`; it also supports `مقاما` and `الفضيلة` where the online transcription loses or duplicates letters at column breaks. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/nasif-nisaiyat-1910.json) pins the three scans, pages, Wikisource revision and text hash; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/nasif-nisaiyat-1910.txt) joins words split across printed columns and uses one line per printed couplet. Vowel marks and ornamental spacing are omitted and spelling normalized. Wikisource contributors' transcription informed the reading, which was adapted to the earlier scan; contributors are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). Qafiyah was also consulted for comparison; its source edition is not established here. Scans are linked, not republished.
- Two Jamīl Ṣidqī al-Zahāwī originals are manually transcribed from [*Al-Kalim al-manẓūm*, 1327 AH](https://archive.org/details/alkalimalmanm01zahwuoft), dated 1909 in the archive catalogue. The [title page](https://archive.org/download/alkalimalmanm01zahwuoft/alkalimalmanm01zahwuoft.pdf#page=196) says the poet omitted some verses from his poems without identifying every affected work; 1327 AH spans 1909–10. The [1924 *Dīwān* title page](https://archive.org/details/3326pdf_202001/page/n1/mode/1up) dates the comparison printing to 1924. An [academic study](https://jahs.qu.edu.sa/index.php/jah/article/download/550/636) identifies al-Zahāwī as an Iraqi poet, 1863–1936; Iraq is a poet association, not an asserted composition place. The historical originals and pre-1931 source printing are public domain in the United States; check local law. Scans are linked, not republished.
  - Both printings separately bound the five couplets of `لله أتعابي` and the seven of the pigeon description, supporting their completeness **in those printings**, not in every tradition or independently descended witnesses. The 1909 `برغم من دلالة مرشدي` and `ما جمعنا وسائلا لمصلحة` differ from the 1924 `برغم السعي والعلم والنهى` and `ما اتخذنا وسائلا لمنفعة`. The 1909 pigeon poem has `زينة` and `المساء`, where 1924 has `بهجة` and `السماء`; the texts are not blended. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/zahawi-kalim-1909.json) pins both scans and exact print/PDF pages; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/zahawi-kalim-1909.txt) transcribes the earlier printing without vowel marks or decorative spacing, normalizing some spelling and putting half-lines on separate lines. No translations, devotional classifications or exact composition dates are supplied.
  - `الإنسان في المستقبل` is deferred: the [1924 version](https://archive.org/details/3326pdf_202001/page/n272/mode/1up) has 15 couplets versus 17 in 1909 and is explicitly labeled as *from a poem*. `التعاون` has 25 couplets in 1909 but a substantially reworked 15-couplet 1924 version; a [1950 article's count](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?curid=119226&oldid=214005) refers to the 1909 book rather than establishing an independent complete witness. Other provisional candidates lack corroborated historical boundaries. Internal `***` dividers are not counted as separate poems.
- Ten short poems by al-Mutanabbī are manually transcribed from the [Aix-Marseille University scan of volume 1](https://odyssee.univ-amu.fr/files/original/1/15/Mutanabbi-Vol1.pdf) of his dīwān, printed at Cairo in 1308 AH (1890–91). The [university's catalogue](https://odyssee.univ-amu.fr/items/show/15) marks the scan public domain; the historical poems and pre-1931 edition are public domain in the United States, while local terms may differ. The catalogue explains that the commentary's traditional attribution to al-ʿUkbarī is disputed; it is not presented as certainly his. We checked the printed heading, all verse half-lines and the next heading for each selection. The PDF runs in reverse print-page order: PDF page `482 − printed page` locates a verse's first page; *Amin kulli shayʾ* continues from [p. 244](https://odyssee.univ-amu.fr/files/original/1/15/Mutanabbi-Vol1.pdf#page=238) to [p. 245](https://odyssee.univ-amu.fr/files/original/1/15/Mutanabbi-Vol1.pdf#page=237). The transcription omits printed vowel and recitation marks and ornamental dividers and places each half-line on a separate line; it is not a fully vocalized diplomatic edition. Untitled poems use their first half-line as a title. We do not establish a composition date, place or geographic association for these records here.
  - Every selected half-line was compared against the separately digitized [al-Wāḥidī commentary](https://shamela.ws/book/754), linked on each reader page. That digital source does not identify a print edition and contains apparent transcription errors; it is a comparison, not the source of the reproduced text. The later commentary in the 1890–91 print itself quotes al-Wāḥidī, so these witnesses are not claimed to be textually independent. The [review manifest](../scripts/sources/mutanabbi-1890-reviewed.json) records print pages, comparison paragraph anchors, the 1890–91 readings, and every difference from the comparison after vowel marks are removed. For example, the printing has `وصوت الغناء`, `بالمكرمات أهدى` and `فأقام`, while the comparison has `وحسن الغناء`, `للمكرمات أهدا` and `وأقام`. Differences and possible comparison typos are disclosed on the affected reader pages. These are complete as bounded in the named edition, not a claim that all manuscript traditions agree. No translations are supplied.
- Ten Abū Firās al-Ḥamdānī poems are manually transcribed from Rudolph Dvořák's [*Abû Firâs, ein Arabischer Dichter und Held* (1895)](https://archive.org/details/abfirseinara00abfi), his edition of al-Thaʿālibī's selection. The tenth-century originals and pre-1931 edition are public domain in the United States; local law may differ. Each separately presented selection was checked against its printed heading, all verse half-lines and the next heading, and its boundaries were compared with another historical printing. Nine are also separately printed with the same number of couplets in the [1910 Beirut *Dīwān*](https://archive.org/details/diwanabifiras00abufuoft); the dove poem has seven couplets in [Ahlwardt's 1859 printing, p. 103](https://archive.org/download/chalefelahmarsq00ahlwgoog/chalefelahmarsq00ahlwgoog.pdf#page=119), but only five in the [1910 *Dīwān*, pp. 44–45](https://archive.org/download/diwanabifiras00abufuoft/diwanabifiras00abufuoft.pdf#page=123). Matching print lengths do not prove independent manuscript descent or establish completeness in every tradition. A [1949 review](https://ar.wikisource.org/wiki/مجلة_الرسالة/العدد_842/رسالة_النقد) discusses omissions and corruptions in early printings. We exclude candidates with unresolved boundaries, including `عدتني عن زيارتكم`, which has two couplets in Dvořák and four in the 1910 printing.
  - Each reader page links to the relevant Dvořák scan page and comparison, with significant alternative readings identified rather than combined. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/abu-firas-1895-reviewed.json) records printed and PDF comparison pages and pins all three scans. The 1895 PDF page is the printed page plus six; the 1910 PDF runs in reverse printed-page order. Transcriptions omit vocalization, elongation and ornamental dividers and use modern dots on final yāʾ. Lines are arranged for reading, keeping words whole where a metrical half-line breaks within a word; they are not fully vocalized diplomatic editions. The first reading line supplies the title. Dates and places of composition are not asserted, and no translations or devotional classifications are supplied.
- Eight selections attributed to al-Khansāʾ follow the [1895 Beirut abridgment of *Anīs al-julasāʾ*](https://archive.org/details/ansaljulasfmulak00khan), edited by Louis Cheikho. The original poems and the 1895 edition are public domain in the United States; consult local law. The [BnF authority record](https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb12072941f) identifies the poet; dates are approximate. Each poem and its boundaries were checked against the 1895 scan and compared with the separately printed [al-Taqaddum *Dīwān*](https://archive.org/details/AAlexandrina-186158). Its title page gives 1348 AH; the archive catalogues it as approximately 1929, while [Wikisource's index](https://ar.wikisource.org/wiki/فهرس:ديوان_الخنساء_(مطبعة_التقدم،_1930).pdf) identifies it as 1930. The year spans 1929–30; the relationship of the two printings' underlying witnesses is not established.
  - The selected texts are separately bounded with the same number of couplets in both printings. That supports their completeness **as printed**, not an assertion that all earlier traditions agree; al-Khansāʾ's transmitted corpus also contains fragments, as the [TDV encyclopedia](https://islamansiklopedisi.org.tr/hansa) notes. For example, `يا عين جودي بدمع منك مسكوب` has seven couplets in 1895 but six in the al-Taqaddum print and is excluded. The longer `لا تخل أنني لقيت رواحا` and other candidates remain for further textual review; a Wikisource version omits a couplet present in both printings. Titles here are first half-lines, not titles in the 1895 source. Vowel marks and ornamental spacing are omitted, and each half-line is on a separate line; these are reading texts, not fully vocalized facsimiles. No translations, exact dates or places of composition, or faith classifications are asserted.
  - Significant variants are identified on the individual pages rather than silently blended: for example, 1895 has `النصب` and the comparison has `النبع`; Cheikho notes the latter as an alternative. His `يبيض سود المسائح` differs from the comparison's `يسود بيض المسائح`, which he reports in his exemplar and questions. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/khansa-1895-reviewed.json) identifies printed pages, the readings and pinned scan hashes. In the 1895 PDF, printed page `p` is PDF page `157 − p`; in the comparison it is `p + 4` for these pages. Scans are linked, not republished.
  - Six readings were informed by Wikisource contributor transcriptions and adapted to the 1895 print with vowel marks removed. The relevant pinned revisions and histories are linked in each record's rights note and recorded in the manifest; their contributors are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). The other two readings were transcribed from the 1895 scan. Some Wikisource pages for the al-Taqaddum edition are unproofread and contain errors; the comparison here is the scan, not a claim that those transcriptions are reliable or independent.
- Eighteen Warda al-Yazijī poems follow the [second edition of *Ḥadīqat al-ward*](https://archive.org/details/adqatalwardnam00yzijuoft). Despite an archive catalogue note saying the title page is absent, the [scanned title page](https://archive.org/download/adqatalwardnam00yzijuoft/adqatalwardnam00yzijuoft.pdf#page=62) identifies the edition as Beirut, 1887. The [al-Babtain dictionary](https://www.almoajam.org/lists/inner/7813) records her dates (1838–1924) and Lebanese association; it gives other publication dates and is not used to override the dated scan. The historical poems and 1887 edition are public domain in the United States; local law may differ. Wikisource contributors' transcriptions informed these readings and were adapted to the 1887 printing; the contributors are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). Each record links its pinned revision and contributor history; no translations are supplied.
  - Each poem's headnote, complete text and following boundary were checked against the 1887 scan, and its text and boundaries compared with the separately printed [third edition](https://archive.org/details/AAlexandrina-186113). That edition calls itself corrected and expanded but its [title page](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-186113/186113%20-%20%D8%AF%D9%8A%D9%88%D8%A7%D9%86%20%D8%AD%D8%AF%D9%8A%D9%82%D8%A9%20%D8%A7%D9%84%D9%88%D8%B1%D8%AF.pdf#page=5) does not give a year. Matching couplet counts establish the boundaries in those printings, not in every tradition; their textual relationship is not established. For these pages, printed page `p` is PDF page `63 − p` in 1887 and `p + 4` in the third printing. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/warda-yaziji-1887-reviewed.json) pins scan hashes, page ranges, revisions and the reading texts.
  - The 1887 `مني السلام على الذي هجر الحمى` ends `أن يجعل القرب الإله مقدما`; the third printing instead has `أن يجعل الله اللقاء مقدما`. The older reading is retained, not blended. Both scans support `المتبسما` in `أهلا وسهلا بالذي زار الحمى`, where Wikisource has `المتسما`; both print `العشيرة` and `حسن مختتم` in the elegy for Mārūn al-Naqqāsh, where Wikisource has `الشعيرة` and omits `حسن`. Vowel marks and decorative spacing are omitted, spelling conventions normalized, and each printed half-line is shown on its own line except where a word is split across the hemistich boundary. Headnotes are not part of the verse. Dates and places of composition and religious affiliation are not inferred from the poet or from her subjects.
  - Nine further works on 1887 pp. 9–15 include two independently headed two-couplet poems about Princess Tāj, not fragments of the longer greeting alongside them. Both printings separately bound each of the nine with the same opening, closing half-line and couplet count. In the longer greeting, the 1887 `أو انتقلت عن الأحياء` differs from the third printing's `أو انتقلت عن الأوطان`; the editions are not blended. The scans also correct the consulted Wikisource's garbled half-line `ذابت عليها بالصبابة مهجتي`, its omission of `حي` in `في الحي حي ما برحت كميت`, and `أفلامه` for `أقلامه`. The headnote's 1855 date for a patriarch's elevation is an occasion date, not proof of a composition date or devotional classification. The [additional manifest](../scripts/sources/warda-yaziji-1887-more.json) pins both scans, printed pages and Wikisource revisions; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/warda-yaziji-1887-more.txt) records the 1887 readings.
  - The 1887 volume's [p. 54](https://archive.org/download/adqatalwardnam00yzijuoft/adqatalwardnam00yzijuoft.pdf#page=9) begins verses praising the collection by other named writers. These are excluded rather than attributing them to Warda; for example, Wikisource's `الجهل شاع بهذا العصر وا اسفي` is in that section. Matching boundaries in the selected pages do not make the whole anthology uniformly hers.
- Twelve Maryana Marrash originals follow the [1893 *Bint Fikr* scan](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:%D8%A8%D9%86%D8%AA_%D9%81%D9%83%D8%B1.pdf); its [title page](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dd/%D8%A8%D9%86%D8%AA_%D9%81%D9%83%D8%B1.pdf#page=1) gives the poet, Beirut and 1893. Her birth year is disputed: the [Library of Congress authority](https://id.loc.gov/authorities/names/no2021093782.html) gives 1846, whereas [Arabica](https://arabica.dohainstitute.org/pages/Maryana-Marrash.aspx) gives 1848; both identify her with Syria and give 1919 as the death year. Syria is associated with the poet, not asserted as a composition place. The historical originals and 1893 edition are public domain in the United States; local law may differ. Wikisource contributors' unproofread page transcriptions informed these readings and were adapted to the scan; contributors are credited under [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), with each relevant revision and history linked on the reader page. No translations are supplied.
  - Each selection's heading, complete printed verse and following boundary were checked on pp. 14–22, where PDF and printed page numbers agree. The [scan](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/dd/%D8%A8%D9%86%D8%AA_%D9%81%D9%83%D8%B1.pdf#page=30) ends mid-work at p. 30; that final incomplete work is excluded. The [HathiTrust catalogue](https://catalog.hathitrust.org/Record/009031841) describes a `30+ p.` copy. These boundaries establish completeness in the consulted printing, not in every tradition. Online transcriptions consulted for comparison may share the scan or other sources and are not represented as independent editions. Sections labeled `دور` within a muwashshah are not counted as separate poems.
  - The separately headed two-couplet inscription beginning `لما سموت على الملائك والبشر` is for a veil over an image of the Virgin Mary; it is classified Faith on that textual basis, without asserting a denomination. The scan appears to read `وبك الخلاص غدا`, but the letter is uncertain and [Wikisource](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?curid=174580&oldid=311903) and [Qafiyah](https://qafiyah.com/poems/oFaX) give `عدا`; the scan-based `غدا` is provisional, not a silently resolved variant. Other scan-based corrections include `خاض المعامع`, `من أولاكم`, `وكبدي تقطع` and `في الطبيعة أعجم` where the unproofread Wikisource transcriptions differ. The 1874 date in the heading of the Francis Marrash elegy is his death date, not necessarily the poem's composition date. The friend Filmo poem's allusion to Mary does not by itself establish devotional intent.
  - The [manifest](../scripts/sources/maryana-marrash-bint-fikr-1893.json) pins the scan hash, pages and Wikisource revisions; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/maryana-marrash-bint-fikr-1893.txt) records the readings. Vowel marks and ornamental spacing are omitted, spelling and spacing normalized, and printed half-lines placed on separate lines; these are reading texts, not diplomatic facsimiles. Headnotes are not included as verse. No composition dates, places or other faith classifications are inferred.
  - Ten additional separately introduced works on pp. 19–24 include a two-couplet coffin inscription for Francis, distinct from the longer elegy; a photograph inscription; a birth greeting; a satire; and a dialogue proposed by a bereaved woman. The headnote does not establish that the dialogue describes an event in the poet's own life. The birth greeting begins on p. 23 and ends after two further couplets on p. 24: the scan reads `ملاك` and `خط` where [Wikisource](https://ar.wikisource.org/w/index.php?curid=174590&oldid=311912) has `ملك` and `فخط`. The scan's `شتاتي` in the proposed dialogue differs from the online `ستاتي`. The [additional manifest](../scripts/sources/arabic-voices-reviewed.json) pins the scan, pages, individual hashes and Wikisource revisions; the [reading file](../scripts/sources/arabic-voices-reviewed.txt) records the texts. The short inscriptions count as individually bounded works in this printing, not as independent manuscript traditions; the internal sections of songs are not counted separately. Gabriel's appearance in the elegy for Bashir is not treated as denomination or sufficient evidence for a devotional classification. The same original, scan, contributor-credit, and local-rights limitations apply as above.
- `للحق والوطن` adds a new Arabic voice, Ḥāfiẓ Ibrāhīm. The [1922 anthology title page](https://archive.org/download/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft.pdf#page=382) gives September 1922; the book's [pp. 204–206](https://archive.org/download/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft.pdf#page=167) separately present the 32 couplets and credit *al-Akhbār*. In this reversed PDF, the three poem pages are 167, 166 and 165. The compiler [says](https://archive.org/download/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft/mashahirshuaraal01ubayuoft.pdf#page=375) he rarely reprints previously published poems whole, so a heading alone is not assumed to prove completeness. An [undated collected edition](https://archive.org/details/AAlexandrina-125963/page/n465/mode/1up) separately presents the same opening, 32 couplets and ending on pp. 94–97 under `تصريح ٢٨ فبراير`, with a note dating publication to 1 April 1922. These boundaries hold in the two printings; their textual descent and completeness in every tradition are not established. The comparison is linked, not reproduced or assumed to be public domain.
  - The 1922 book's `يزكو` and `ولتنتخب` differ from the collected edition's `يذكو` and `وتنتخب`; neither version is silently spliced into the other. Both print `يسجح` where [Qafiyah](https://qafiyah.com/poems/sUDU) and another online transcription have `يسجج`. The 1922 text is transcribed from the scan, omitting vowel marks and ornamental dividers and normalizing spacing and some spelling; half-lines appear separately. The original as published in 1922 is public domain in the United States; check local law. [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Hafiz-Ibrahim) gives the Egyptian poet's birth year as 1871 and the [National Library of Israel](https://www.nli.org.il/en/a-topic/987007276272905171) gives approximately 1872; both support a 1932 death. Egypt is an author association, not an asserted composition place. No translation, exact composition date or religious classification is supplied. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/arabic-voices-reviewed.json) pins both scans, pages and the reading hash.
  - `لسان حال اللغة العربية` is a second complete Ibrāhīm work, transcribed from [*Dīwān Ḥāfiẓ*, vol. II, pp. 16–19](https://aco.dlib.nyu.edu/book/aub_aco003437/238). The [volume II title page](https://aco.dlib.nyu.edu/book/aub_aco003437/215) names the poet, dates the second edition 1340 AH/1922, and states that it was authorized by the author. The 23 couplets begin after a preceding poem and end before `الزوجية`. The separately printed [collected edition, pp. 253–255](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-125963/125963%20-%20%D8%AF%D9%8A%D9%88%D8%A7%D9%86%20%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8%20%D8%A7%D8%A8%D8%B1%D8%A7%D9%87%D9%8A%D9%85.pdf#page=305), has the same opening, ending and count; its `اللغة العربية تنعى حظها بين أهلها` supplies the display title and its heading claims publication in 1903. The publication date of that collected edition is not established here.
  - The [11 June 1907 issue of *al-Manār*](https://archive.org/download/almanaralmanar/almanar10.pdf#page=183) reviews an earlier volume of the dīwān and prints the same 23 couplets on [pp. 308–309](https://archive.org/download/almanaralmanar/almanar10.pdf#page=232), explicitly presenting them as a sample from that volume. Matching boundaries establish completeness in the named printings, not in every tradition or independent textual descent. The 1922 printing reads `عقمت فلم أجزع لقوم عداتي`; the 1907 and collected printings instead read `لقول عداتي`. The three scans read `فإني`, where [al-Diwan's modern transcription](https://www.aldiwan.net/poem10924.html) has `فإنني`. We retain the 1922 reading, without silently resolving the differences.
  - The original as published before 1931 is public domain in the United States; check local law. The comparison scans are linked, not republished or assumed to grant rights to their editorial matter. No translation, composition place, exact composition date or devotional category is inferred. Vowel marks, footnotes, ornaments and some punctuation are omitted; some spelling and spacing are normalized, and printed half-lines appear separately. The result is a reading transcription, not a fully vocalized diplomatic edition. The [manifest](../scripts/sources/hafiz-language-1922.json) pins the three scans, printed pages, PDF leaves and reading hash. Save the [1922 AUB PDF](https://mc.dlib.nyu.edu/files/books/aub_aco003437/aub_aco003437_lo.pdf) as `aub_aco003437_lo.pdf`, the collected PDF as `collected-125963.pdf`, and the magazine PDF as `almanar10.pdf`; run `python3 scripts/import-hafiz-language-1922.py <evidence-directory> --check`.
  - Two further separately headed works follow the same author-authorized 1922 volume II: [`الأخلاق الفاضلة`, pp. 24–25](https://aco.dlib.nyu.edu/book/aub_aco003437/246), and [`الامتيازات`, pp. 27–29](https://aco.dlib.nyu.edu/book/aub_aco003437/249). Their 10 and 18 couplets respectively are compared in order with the undated collected edition's [`سجن الفضائل`, pp. 124–125](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-125963/125963%20-%20%D8%AF%D9%8A%D9%88%D8%A7%D9%86%20%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8%20%D8%A7%D8%A8%D8%B1%D8%A7%D9%87%D9%8A%D9%85.pdf#page=496), and [`الامتيازات الأجنبية`, pp. 110–111](https://archive.org/download/AAlexandrina-125963/125963%20-%20%D8%AF%D9%8A%D9%88%D8%A7%D9%86%20%D8%AD%D8%A7%D9%81%D8%B8%20%D8%A7%D8%A8%D8%B1%D8%A7%D9%87%D9%8A%D9%85.pdf#page=482). The collected titles aid discovery. Matching openings, endings and counts establish completeness in the cited printings, not independent textual descent or completeness in every tradition.
  - The 1922 `فرويتهن وأظمأني` differs from the collected edition's `فروينهن وأظمأنني`; the 1922 `لهذا الفخر من سباب` differs from its `من سبب`. The 1922 readings are retained, not silently corrected or blended. Modern [virtues](https://www.aldiwan.net/poem10501.html) and [privileges](https://www.aldiwan.net/poem10513.html) transcriptions were comparison aids, not the historical or rights basis. Vowel marks, footnotes, ornaments and some punctuation are omitted; some spelling and spacing are normalized. Each complete couplet is shown as one reading line, with words split across 1922 print lines rejoined. These are reading transcriptions, not fully vocalized diplomatic editions. No translation, exact composition date, composition place or religious category is supplied. The historical originals published before 1931 are public domain in the United States; check local law. The comparison scan is linked, not republished or assumed to clear its editorial matter.
